In reality, the AI ecosystem involves cross-contamination: models ingest synthetic data from other models, produce new synthetic text, and contaminate shared corpora. We propose a bilayer coupled SIR/SIRS framework -- a phenomenological mean-field model treating data corpora and AI models as two interacting populations, each with susceptible, infected, and recovered compartments linked by cross-layer transmission. The SIRS variant (our primary recommendation) incorporates immunity waning, reflecting that filtered corpora and retrained models remain susceptible to re-contamination. We derive the basic reproduction number $R_0 = \sqrt{β_D β_M / [(γ_D+μ_D)(γ_M+μ_M)]}$ via the Next Generation Matrix and apply standard epidemic threshold results to the bilayer system. Illustrative scenario-based calibration from public AI text prevalence data yields supercritical dynamics ($R_0 > 1$) across three scenarios; Sobol sensitivity analysis identifies synthetic-text detection as the highest-leverage parameter. A bipartite-network agent-based model confirms mean-field consistency ($R^2 > 0.96$) for dense networks but degrades under heterogeneity. GPT-2 contamination chain experiments (192 runs across WikiText and Shakespeare) show dose-response degradation and diversity loss qualitatively consistent with the threshold picture. Matched-budget source-diversity experiments (1,088 runs) provide suggestive evidence that multi-source mixing modestly attenuates collapse, but the effect vanishes at lower contamination fractions. Intervention analysis identifies detection-based filtering and herd immunity as the highest-leverage strategies.

</details>


### [103] [Predict and Reconstruct: Joint Objectives for Self-Supervised Language Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05173)
*Aimen Boukhari*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid pre-training objective combining JEPA-style latent-space prediction loss with MLM, showing the hybrid encoder produces more uniform embeddings, richer spectral geometry, and better semantic-to-lexical balance compared to pure MLM, despite similar downstream accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLM encourages representations anchored to surface-form token identity rather than deeper semantic structure. The authors aim to improve representation quality by incorporating a JEPA-inspired predictive loss that operates in latent space.

Method: A hybrid objective combining JEPA-style latent-space prediction loss with standard MLM over a single shared encoder, with a learnable scalar balancing the two objectives. Pre-trained on English Wikipedia using identical architectures and compute budgets (NVIDIA H100).

Result: Hybrid encoder produces more uniform embeddings (uniformity < -0.16 vs -0.05 for MLM), richer spectral geometry under max pooling, encodes less surface-level lexical information, and achieves better semantic-to-lexical balance, with similar linear-probe downstream accuracy.

Conclusion: The JEPA predictive objective reshapes the latent space in ways that standard accuracy metrics alone cannot capture, suggesting geometric differences are consistent and significant.

Abstract: Masked language modelling (MLM) has been the dominant pre-training objective for text encoders since BERT, yet it encourages representations that are strongly anchored to surface-form token identity rather than deeper semantic structure. Inspired by the success of Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) (LeCun, 2022) in vision and audio, we propose a hybrid pre-training objective that combines a JEPA-style latent-space prediction loss with a standard MLM objective over a single shared encoder. A learnable scalar parameter continuously balances the two objectives during training. We pre-train both a hybrid model and a pure-MLM baseline on English Wikipedia using identical architectures and compute budgets (NVIDIA H100). Extensive representation analysis across five GLUE benchmarks (SST-2, MRPC, MNLI, CoLA, STS-B) using four pooling strategies reveals that the hybrid encoder produces significantly more uniform embeddings (uniformity less than -0.16 vs -0.05 for MLM), exhibits richer spectral geometry under max pooling, encodes less surface-level lexical information, and achieves a better semantic-to-lexical balance. Despite similar linear-probe downstream accuracy, the geometric differences are consistent and significant, suggesting that the JEPA predictive objective reshapes the latent space in ways that standard accuracy metrics alone cannot capture.

</details>


### [104] [Improving Heart-Focused Medical Question Answering in LLMs via Variance-Aware Rubric Rewards with GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05174)
*Arash Ahmadi,Parisa Masnadi,Sarah Sharif,Charles Nicholson,David Ebert,Mike Banad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Variance-Aware Reward Framework using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for post-training LLMs on heart-focused medical QA. By replacing binary/ordinal rubric aggregation with continuous analytical reward functions, the method improves accuracy from 0.362 to 0.502 and F1 from 0.532 to 0.668 on a heart-related HealthBench subset, matching a 120B model with a 14B backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying general-purpose LLMs in healthcare is hindered by data privacy, inference costs, and limited edge-device suitability. Smaller models with robust post-training strategies are needed to ensure reliable medical reasoning without relying on large-scale, privacy-sensitive data or expensive infrastructure.

Method: The authors apply GRPO for post-training LLMs on heart-focused medical QA, using rubric-based supervision from RaR-Medicine. They introduce a Variance-Aware Reward Framework that extends both Explicit and Implicit Aggregation strategies by replacing weighted binary criterion scoring and single Likert-style scores with continuous analytical reward functions derived from criterion-level rubric outcomes, enabling richer optimization signals for sparse, multi-criteria feedback and stable on-policy RL.

Result: On a held-out heart-related subset of HealthBench, the best GRPO variant improves accuracy from 0.362 to 0.502 and F1 from 0.532 to 0.668 over the Qwen3-14B base model, remaining competitive with GPT-OSS-120B (0.508 accuracy, 0.674 F1).

Conclusion: Carefully designed rubric-based rewards offer a practical strategy for improving heart-focused medical QA in LLMs, with potential extension to other rubric-based tasks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong promise in healthcare applications. Yet deploying general-purpose models in real-world settings remains difficult due to data privacy constraints, inference costs, and limited suitability for edge or on-device use. These challenges motivate the development of smaller, more efficient models that require robust post-training strategies to ensure reliable medical reasoning. In this work, we investigate Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for post-training LLMs on heart-focused medical question answering with rubric-based supervision derived from RaR-Medicine. We propose a Variance-Aware Reward Framework that extends the Explicit Aggregation and Implicit Aggregation strategies of Rubrics as Rewards by replacing weighted binary criterion aggregation and single overall Likert-style scoring with continuous analytical reward functions derived from criterion-level rubric outcomes. This formulation provides richer optimization signals for feedback that is sparse, multi-criteria, and difficult to verify automatically, and enables more stable on-policy reinforcement learning. On a held-out heart-related subset of HealthBench, our best GRPO variant improves accuracy from 0.362 to 0.502 and F1 from 0.532 to 0.668 relative to the Qwen3-14B base model, while remaining competitive with GPT-OSS-120B (0.508 accuracy, 0.674 F1). Our findings show that carefully designed rubric-based rewards provide a practical strategy for improving heart-focused medical question answering in LLMs, with potential to extend to other rubric-based tasks.

</details>


### [105] [Generic Triple-Latent Compression with Gated Associative Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05175)
*Liu Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes triple-latent sequence models with a token state and compressed pair-memory to capture higher-order token interactions. It improves Transformer baselines on WikiText-2 and MiniMind benchmarks, and a gated key-value retrieval variant improves recall but is seed-sensitive and slower.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To capture higher-order token interactions without requiring benchmark-specific parsing, addressing limitations of standard sequence models that lack explicit mechanisms for modeling complex token dependencies.

Method: Proposes generic triple-latent sequence models that maintain a running token state and a compressed pair-memory pathway. A recall-focused gated key-value retrieval extension is also introduced.

Result: Improves a small Transformer baseline on byte-level WikiText-2 and tokenizer-based MiniMind benchmark. The gated key-value retrieval variant improves associative recall but is seed-sensitive and significantly slower in the reference implementation.

Conclusion: Triple-latent models offer a promising direction for capturing higher-order token interactions, but the retrieval extension needs optimization for speed and stability.

Abstract: We study generic triple-latent sequence models that maintain a running token state and compressed pair-memory pathway to capture higher-order token interactions without benchmark-specific parsing. The triple-latent family improves a small Transformer baseline on byte-level WikiText-2 and on a tokenizer-based MiniMind language-model benchmark, while a recall-focused gated key-value retrieval extension improves associative recall but remains seed-sensitive and much slower in the current reference implementation.

</details>


### [106] [PEFT of SLM for Telecommunications Customer Support: A Comparative Study of LoRA Configurations with Energy Consumption Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05176)
*Lucas Tamic,Ilan Jaffeux-Cheniout,Xavier Marjou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic study of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) using LoRA on Qwen2.5-3B for telecommunications customer support. The authors introduce a combinatorial synthetic data generation method producing ~30K training examples, evaluate 16 LoRA configurations, and find that validation loss diverges from human-aligned qualitative rankings—the best validation loss (0.5024) ranks only 6th-7th qualitatively, while the worst loss (0.6807) ranks first. Contributions include synthetic data construction, LoRA target module insights, and energy-performance trade-off analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs show strong NLU/NLG performance but their evaluation and adaptation to domain-specific constraints in telecommunications customer support remain limited. Data sovereignty, regulatory constraints, and handling of sensitive customer/network information complicate the use of externally hosted foundation models in this domain.

Method: Systematic study of PEFT using LoRA applied to Qwen2.5-3B. A combinatorial synthetic data generation approach based on a glossary of 52 industry-specific terms, producing ~30,000 training examples across 1,560 distinct problem scenarios via a generative pipeline powered by Gemini 2.0 Flash. Evaluation of 16 LoRA configurations by varying hyperparameters and target modules, with evaluation extending to energy consumption analysis and qualitative LLM-as-a-judge framework using GPT-5.2 and Claude 4.5 Sonnet.

Result: Clear divergence between quantitative and qualitative performance: models achieving the lowest validation loss do not necessarily obtain the best human-aligned rankings. Best validation loss (0.5024) ranks only 6th-7th in qualitative evaluation, while the worst loss (0.6807) ranks first according to both judges.

Conclusion: (1) A combinatorial method for synthetic dataset construction, (2) insights into the impact of target module selection for LoRA injection, (3) evidence that validation loss alone is insufficient for selecting fine-tuning configurations in conversational AI, and (4) an energy-performance trade-off analysis for sustainable LLM deployment.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) show strong performance in natural language understanding and generation, their evaluation and adaptation to domain-specific constraints in telecommunications customer support remain limited. In addition, data sovereignty, regulatory constraints, and the handling of sensitive customer and network information complicate the use of externally hosted foundation models in this domain.
  We present a systematic study of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) applied to Qwen2.5-3B to build a domain-specific conversational assistant. We introduce a combinatorial synthetic data generation approach based on a glossary of 52 industry-specific terms, producing approximately 30,000 training examples across 1,560 distinct problem scenarios via a generative pipeline powered by Gemini 2.0 Flash.
  We evaluate 16 LoRA configurations by varying hyperparameters and target modules. Our evaluation extends beyond standard metrics by incorporating energy consumption analysis and qualitative assessment using an LLM-as-a-judge framework with GPT-5.2 and Claude 4.5 Sonnet.
  Results show a clear divergence between quantitative and qualitative performance: models achieving the lowest validation loss do not necessarily obtain the best human-aligned rankings. The best validation loss (0.5024) ranks only 6th-7th in qualitative evaluation, while the worst loss (0.6807) ranks first according to both judges.
  This work contributes (1) a combinatorial method for synthetic dataset construction, (2) insights into the impact of target module selection for LoRA injection, (3) evidence that validation loss alone is insufficient for selecting fine-tuning configurations in conversational AI, and (4) an energy-performance trade-off analysis for sustainable LLM deployment.

</details>


### [107] [MCBench: A Multicontext Safety Assessment Benchmark for Omni Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05177)
*Manh Luong,Tamas Abraham,Junae Kim,Amar Kaur,Rollin Omari,Gholamreza Haffari,Trang Vu,Lizhen Qu,Dinh Phung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MCBench is a new multimodal safety benchmark with 1196 scenarios across four categories, designed to evaluate Omni LLMs that process vision, audio, and text. Evaluations show these models struggle with cross-modal reasoning and subtle risks, often failing to integrate information across modalities for accurate safety judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal safety benchmarks focus only on visual inputs and cannot assess Omni LLMs that process vision, audio, and text simultaneously. There is a need for a benchmark that requires integrating multiple modalities for accurate safety assessment.

Method: MCBench creates 1196 scenarios spanning four safety categories, each unsafe scenario paired with a minimally different safe counterpart. Evaluations analyze model performance and reasoning traces to assess cross-modal integration capabilities.

Result: Omni LLMs struggle with subtle or non-physical risks but perform better when salient visual or acoustic cues are present. Models can extract modality-specific information but fail to integrate these cues effectively for safety judgments.

Conclusion: Current Omni LLMs lack robust cross-modal reasoning in safety-critical settings, highlighting the need for improved architectures and training strategies for multimodal safety.

Abstract: Existing multimodal safety benchmarks focus solely on visual inputs and cannot assess Omni Large Language Models (LLMs) that process vision, audio, and text. We introduce MCBench, a benchmark with 1196 scenarios spanning four safety categories that require integrating multiple modalities for accurate safety assessment. Each unsafe scenario is paired with a minimally different safe counterpart to assess model sensitivity. Our evaluations of state-of-the-art models reveal significant challenges. Omni LLMs struggle with subtle or non-physical risks but perform better when salient visual or acoustic cues are present. Analysis of reasoning traces shows that, although models can extract modality-specific information, they often fail to integrate these cues effectively for safety judgments. Our findings reveal that current Omni LLMs lack robust cross-modal reasoning in safety-critical settings, underscoring the need for improved architectures and training strategies for multimodal safety.

</details>


### [108] [Efficient Punctuation Restoration via Weighted Lookahead Scoring Method for Streaming ASR Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05179)
*Sungmook Woo,Hyungu Kang,Chanwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a non-autoregressive scoring method for punctuation restoration in streaming ASR that avoids free-form generation, using bounded lookahead and calibrated thresholds to achieve high F1 scores (0.893 without fine-tuning, 0.937 with fine-tuning) on IWSLT 2017, outperforming prompt-based and ELECTRA baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming ASR requires online punctuation decisions with limited future context; existing generation-based approaches suffer from latency and alignment failures under boundary-wise evaluation.

Method: Non-autoregressive scoring method that preserves the input transcript and makes decisions at each word boundary by comparing punctuation insertion hypotheses against a no-insertion baseline under a bounded K-subword-token lookahead, with decisions calibrated using weight α and validation-calibrated threshold τ (no parameter updates during inference).

Result: On IWSLT 2017, achieves 4-class macro F1 of 0.893 (no fine-tuning, K=2) and 0.937 (fine-tuned, K=2), outperforming prompt-based baseline (0.566) and fine-tuned ELECTRA baseline (0.913) under same lookahead budget.

Conclusion: The proposed scoring method effectively handles streaming punctuation restoration with bounded lookahead, and ablation studies on K show the impact of lookahead budget on performance.

Abstract: Punctuation restoration improves ASR (Automatic Speech Recognition) readability. However streaming ASR requires online decisions with limited future context. In streaming ASR, the system predicts punctuation incrementally, which makes generation-based approaches prone to latency and alignment failures under boundary-wise evaluation. This paper proposes a non-autoregressive scoring method (no free-form generation) that preserves the input transcript and makes a decision at each word boundary. Our method compares punctuation insertion hypotheses against a no-insertion baseline under a bounded K-subword-token lookahead, and calibrates decisions using a weight α and a validation-calibrated threshold τ (no parameter updates during inference). On IWSLT 2017, our scoring method achieves a 4-class macro F1 of 0.893 in the no fine-tuning setting (validation-calibrated, K=2) and 0.937 after fine-tuning (K=2), outperforming the prompt-based baseline (0.566) and a fine-tuned ELECTRA baseline (0.913) under the same lookahead budget. We analyze the impact of the lookahead budget through ablation studies on K.

</details>


### [109] [From Scoring to Explanations: Evaluating SHAP and LLM Rationales for Rubric-based Teaching Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05180)
*Ivo Bueno,Babette Bühler,Philipp Stark,Tim Fütterer,Ulrich Trautwein,Dorottya Demszky,Heather Hill,Enkelejda Kasneci*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework for sentence-level interpretability of automated rubric-based scoring, combining SHAP value attributions with LLM-generated rationales. Applied to the CLASS Quality of Feedback dimension on 6k NCTE transcript segments, fine-tuned PLMs outperform LLMs in prediction accuracy but show mid-scale label compression. SHAP provides more faithful, coherent, and transferable explanations than LLM rationales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated scoring models lack interpretability for rubric-based quality ratings of complex language performances, especially in high-stakes educational settings where understanding why a particular score is produced is crucial.

Method: A general framework combining model-agnostic Shapley-value (SHAP) attributions with LLM-generated rationales for sentence-level interpretability, instantiated on the CLASS Quality of Feedback dimension using the NCTE corpus. Fine-tuned PLMs and prompted LLMs are systematically compared on scoring performance and explanation faithfulness via deletion-based tests and cross-model analyses.

Result: Fine-tuned PLMs outperform LLMs in prediction accuracy but exhibit label compression toward mid-scale scores. SHAP identifies sentences that drive model predictions with larger and more coherent prediction shifts than LLM rationales. SHAP attributions transfer robustly across architectures, while LLM rationales have limited and inconsistent influence.

Conclusion: SHAP provides more faithful and transferable explanations for rubric-based scoring than LLM-generated rationales. The proposed framework offers a principled basis for evaluating scoring models and their explanations in high-stakes educational settings and other rubric-based language assessment tasks.

Abstract: Automated scoring models are increasingly used to assign rubric-based quality ratings to complex language performances, including classroom transcripts, yet they typically provide little insight into why a particular score is produced. We propose a general framework for sentence-level interpretability of rubric-based scoring that combines model-agnostic Shapley-value attributions with rationales generated by large language models (LLMs). Instantiated on the Quality of Feedback dimension of the CLASS framework using the NCTE corpus, the framework enables systematic comparison of fine-tuned pretrained language models (PLMs) and prompted LLMs on both scoring performance and explanation faithfulness. Across 6k annotated transcript segments, fine-tuned PLMs outperform LLMs in prediction accuracy but exhibit label compression toward mid-scale scores. Deletion-based tests show that SHAP identifies sentences that reliably drive model predictions, producing typically larger and more coherent prediction shifts than LLM-generated rationales. Cross-model analyses further reveal that SHAP attributions transfer robustly across architectures, whereas LLM rationales exert limited and inconsistent influence. Overall, the findings demonstrate that SHAP provides more faithful and transferable explanations for rubric-based scoring, and that the proposed framework offers a principled basis for evaluating both scoring models and their explanations in high-stakes educational settings and other rubric-based language assessment tasks.

</details>


### [110] [Multi-Granularity Reasoning for Natural Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05181)
*Chunling Xi,Di Liang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Multi-Granularity Reasoning Network (MGRN) for NLI that explicitly leverages hierarchical semantic features across multiple granularities (from lexical to abstract) in a progressive reasoning space, mimicking human cognitive processing, and achieves superior performance on benchmarks over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NLI methods rely on final-layer token representations from transformer models, which entangle or dilute fine-grained lexical cues, phrasal compositions, and higher-level semantics in a single representation space, limiting their ability to capture complex hierarchical semantic interactions required for effective reasoning.

Method: The proposed MGRN framework explicitly leverages hierarchical semantic features within an interactive reasoning space, progressing from shallow lexical matching to deeper semantic abstraction and logical reasoning, integrating multi-granularity semantic information in a progressive and structured manner.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple public benchmarks show that MGRN consistently outperforms strong baseline models, validating its effectiveness and robustness.

Conclusion: The multi-granularity hierarchical reasoning approach is effective for NLI, and mimicking human cognitive progression from lexical to abstract reasoning yields better performance than relying solely on final-layer representations.

Abstract: Natural Language Inference (NLI) is a fundamental task in natural language understanding that requires determining the logical relationship between a premise and a hypothesis. Despite the remarkable success of transformer-based pre-trained models, most existing approaches primarily rely on the final-layer token representations, which are often insufficient for capturing the complex and hierarchical semantic interactions required for effective reasoning. In particular, fine-grained lexical cues, phrasal compositions, and higher-level contextual semantics are typically entangled or diluted in a single representation space. To address these limitations, we propose a novel \emph{Multi-Granularity Reasoning Network} (MGRN) that explicitly leverages hierarchical semantic features within an interactive reasoning space. The proposed framework mimics the human cognitive process of language understanding, which naturally progresses from shallow lexical matching to deeper semantic abstraction and logical reasoning. By integrating semantic information across multiple granularities in a progressive and structured manner, MGRN is able to uncover intricate semantic relationships underlying natural language expressions. Extensive experiments on multiple public benchmarks demonstrate that MGRN consistently outperforms strong baseline models, validating the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [111] [LANTERN: Layered Archival and Temporal Episodic Retrieval Network for Long-Context LLM Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05182)
*Rahul Subramani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LANTERN is a lightweight memory layer that recovers 78.3% of facts lost during conversation compaction via hybrid retrieval (zero LLM calls, <25ms latency), outperforming MemGPT's LLM-driven pipeline (72.4%) across 94 multi-turn conversations with human-validated facts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models discard critical details when conversation history is compacted to fit within finite context windows, leading to information loss in multi-turn interactions.

Method: LANTERN uses a layered archival and temporal episodic retrieval network that proactively archives every conversation turn and restores relevant details after compaction via hybrid retrieval, requiring zero LLM calls and adding fewer than 25ms of latency per turn.

Result: LANTERN-Rerank recovers 78.3% of verifiable facts lost to compaction (vs. MemGPT's 72.4%; p<0.0001, d=0.43). Base LANTERN (no reranker) matches or exceeds the LLM-driven baseline (p=0.005) with zero LLM calls. Accuracy improves by 8.4 pp on average across four production LLMs.

Conclusion: LANTERN demonstrates that efficient, LLM-free retrieval-based memory restoration can outperform expensive LLM-driven compaction recovery, and the recovered context is useful across diverse model architectures. The full evaluation framework is released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Large language models discard critical details when conversation history is compacted to fit within finite context windows. We present LANTERN (Layered Archival aNd Temporal Episodic Retrieval Network), a lightweight memory layer that proactively archives every conversation turn and restores relevant details after compaction via hybrid retrieval -- requiring zero LLM calls and adding fewer than 25ms of latency per turn. On 94 real multi-turn conversations (1,894 ground-truth facts, human-validated at kappa=0.81), LANTERN-Rerank recovers 78.3% of verifiable facts lost to compaction, significantly outperforming a faithful reimplementation of MemGPT's LLM-driven extraction and multi-query search pipeline (72.4%; Wilcoxon p<0.0001, 95% CI [+3.1, +8.6] pp, d=0.43) at a fraction of the inference cost. Even without the reranker, base LANTERN matches or exceeds this LLM-driven baseline (p=0.005) using zero LLM calls. When four production LLMs answer fact-bearing questions using LANTERN-restored context, accuracy improves by 8.4 percentage points on average (Wilcoxon p<0.05 for each model individually), demonstrating that the recovered context is useful across diverse model architectures. We release the full evaluation framework -- paired significance tests, failure analysis, fact-type stratification, and compaction robustness analysis -- to support reproducibility and future work.

</details>


### [112] [The Granularity Gap: A Multi-Dimensional Longitudinal Audit of Sycophancy in Gemini Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05183)
*Patrick Keough*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Granularity Gap, showing that binary sycophancy metrics mask substantial social-compliance behaviors in LLMs. Evaluating six Gemini variants across three generations on 73 adversarial prompts with 8,830 graded responses (0-4 Likert scale), they find: (1) 27.2% of responses contain substantial sycophantic content despite binary metrics showing low failure rates; (2) generational progress is non-monotonic with Gen 2.5 regressing sharply; (3) an Alignment Tax exists where sycophancy trades off against truthfulness (Spearman rho = -0.63).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard alignment benchmarks treat sycophancy as a binary failure mode, which masks substantial social-compliance behaviors where models capitulate to user framing, validate questionable premises, or soften factual corrections without producing overtly false outputs. This coarse granularity prevents accurate measurement of model sycophancy.

Method: The authors evaluated six Gemini variants across generations 2.0, 2.5, and 3.0 on 73 adversarial prompts under three guardrail conditions (Control, Simple, Protocol), yielding 8,830 graded responses. They used a 0-4 Likert scale validated against a human annotator triad (Fleiss kappa = 0.71; Cohen kappa = 0.78 vs AI consensus; 95.9% binary accuracy, 100% specificity).

Result: Three key findings: (1) 27.2% of responses contain substantial sycophantic content (Likert >= 2.0) and 22.7% reach moderate or severe levels (>= 3.0), while binary win-rate framing reports only modest failure rates with coarse metrics explaining just 29% of graded variance. (2) Generational progress is non-monotonic: Gen 2.5 regresses sharply (mean Control 2.64) relative to Gen 2.0 (1.90) and Gen 3.0 (2.01), with Gen 2.5 showing inverse scaling while Gen 3.0 restores standard scaling. (3) An Alignment Tax: Spearman rho = -0.63 between sycophancy and truthfulness. Egotistical Validation prompts act as a sycophancy trap (mean 3.27), nearly double Unethical Proposals (1.72).

Conclusion: Binary sycophancy metrics are insufficient; continuous measurement reveals substantial hidden sycophancy. Simple guardrails outperform elaborate Protocol scaffolding on flagship models, but distilled Gen 3.0 Flash inverts this pattern, suggesting small models may structurally require chain-of-thought scaffolding. The authors release their dataset and rubric to support continuous sycophancy measurement.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as high-stakes advisors, yet standard alignment benchmarks treat sycophancy as a binary failure mode. We introduce the Granularity Gap: coarse binary metrics mask substantial social-compliance behaviors where models capitulate to user framing, validate questionable premises, or soften factual corrections without producing overtly false outputs. We evaluate six Gemini variants across generations 2.0, 2.5, and 3.0 on 73 adversarial prompts under three guardrail conditions (Control, Simple, Protocol), yielding 8,830 graded responses. Using a 0-4 Likert scale validated against a human annotator triad (Fleiss kappa = 0.71; Cohen kappa = 0.78 vs AI consensus; 95.9 percent binary accuracy, 100 percent specificity), we quantify sycophancy as continuous rather than binary. Three findings emerge. First, 27.2 percent of responses contain substantial sycophantic content (Likert >= 2.0) and 22.7 percent reach moderate or severe levels (>= 3.0), while binary win-rate framing reports only modest failure rates; coarse metrics explain just 29 percent of graded variance. Second, generational progress is non-monotonic: Gen 2.5 regresses sharply (mean Control 2.64) relative to Gen 2.0 (1.90) and Gen 3.0 (2.01), and Gen 2.5 shows inverse scaling (Pro 1.94 worse than Flash 1.71) while Gen 3.0 restores standard scaling. Third, we document an Alignment Tax: Spearman rho = -0.63 between sycophancy and truthfulness, indicating social compliance trades against factual accuracy. Egotistical Validation prompts act as a sycophancy trap (mean 3.27), nearly double Unethical Proposals (1.72). Simple guardrails outperform elaborate Protocol scaffolding on flagship models, but distilled Gen 3.0 Flash inverts this, suggesting small models may structurally require chain-of-thought scaffolding. We release the dataset and rubric to support continuous sycophancy measurement.

</details>


### [113] [LoRi: Low-Rank Distillation for Implicit Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05315)
*Ryan Solgi,Jiayi Tian,Zheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a low-rank distillation framework for implicit chain-of-thought reasoning, which aligns teacher and student hidden-state trajectories in a shared low-rank tensor subspace using first- and second-order statistics, improving performance on multi-step reasoning tasks across LLaMA and Qwen model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implicit chain-of-thought (iCoT) methods often underperform explicit CoT prompting, and the paper identifies that hidden-state reasoning trajectories exhibit low-rank structure, motivating a distillation approach that leverages this property.

Method: The method aligns teacher and student reasoning trajectories in a shared low-rank tensor subspace by matching first- and second-order statistics, capturing global reasoning structure with a compact latent representation.

Result: The approach consistently improves performance across LLaMA and Qwen model families at different scales on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, approaching explicit CoT accuracy and outperforming prior iCoT distillation methods, especially on challenging multi-step tasks.

Conclusion: Low-rank distillation effectively transfers reasoning capabilities by exploiting the low-rank structure of hidden-state trajectories, bridging the gap between implicit and explicit chain-of-thought reasoning.

Abstract: Implicit chain-of-thought (iCoT) methods aim to internalize reasoning in large language models, but often underperform explicit CoT prompting. We empirically find that hidden-state reasoning trajectories exhibit low-rank structure. Motivated by this observation, we propose a low-rank distillation framework that transfers reasoning by aligning teacher and student trajectories in a shared low-rank tensor subspace using first- and second-order statistics. The resulting formulation captures the global structure of reasoning while supporting a compact latent reasoning process. We evaluate the method across multiple model families, including LLaMA and Qwen, at different scales on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Our approach consistently improves performance, especially on challenging multi-step tasks, approaching explicit CoT accuracy and outperforming prior iCoT distillation methods.

</details>


### [114] [A Model of Multi-turn Human Persuadability Using Probabilistic Belief Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05330)
*Jared Moore,Noah Goodman,Nick Haber,Max Kleiman-Weiner*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PERSUASIONTRACE is a framework for studying persuasion in human-LLM dialogues that records multi-turn belief reports, annotates rhetorical strategies, and evaluates simulators. It reveals that humans cluster into two belief-update types, LLMs are persuasive across topics/modalities, and standard LLM simulators fail to replicate human belief dynamics—while a Bayesian-network simulated target achieves near-human fidelity (81 vs 80), outperforming baseline LLM targets (64).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing persuasion studies rely on pre/post belief change measurements, which identify whether persuasion occurred but miss where and how beliefs moved within a dialogue. This work aims to provide process-level evaluation of persuasion in multi-turn human-LLM interactions.

Method: The authors build a web-based experimental platform (PERSUASIONTRACE) that records multi-turn belief reports, annotates persuader turns with rhetorical dimensions (logos/pathos/ethos), and evaluates simulators by fidelity to real human belief dynamics. They compare vanilla LLM simulators against a Bayesian-network simulated target that maintains an explicit latent belief state.

Result: Human targets group into two clusters of multi-turn belief updates and exhibit differential susceptibility to rhetorical strategies. LLMs are persuasive across generic/personalized topics, text/audio modalities, and multi-turn interactions. The Bayesian simulated target achieves near-human fidelity (81 vs 80), while baseline LLM targets score substantially lower (64).

Conclusion: PERSUASIONTRACE reframes persuasion evaluation from endpoint movement alone to process fidelity, providing a stronger basis for scientific analysis and safer optimization of persuasive systems.

Abstract: Large language models can shift human beliefs across high-stakes domains, but most persuasion studies rely on pre/post belief change. These endpoint measures identify whether persuasion occurred, yet miss where and how beliefs moved within a dialogue. We present PERSUASIONTRACE, a framework for studying persuasion in human-LLM interaction. Built on a web-based experimental platform, PERSUASIONTRACE contributes a tool for multi-turn persuasion studies and a process-level evaluation protocol: it records multi-turn belief reports from human or simulated targets of persuasion, annotates persuader turns with rhetorical dimensions (logos/pathos/ethos), and evaluates simulators by fidelity to real human belief dynamics. Using this framework, we find that human targets group into two clusters of multi-turn belief updates and exhibit susceptibility to rhetorical strategies, and that LLMs are persuasive across generic and personalized topics, text and audio modalities, and multi-turn interactions. Prior work has chiefly used vanilla-prompted LLMs to simulate human targets, but we show that these simulators fail to replicate human belief dynamics. We introduce a Bayesian-network simulated target that maintains an explicit latent belief state over time so each persuader message yields cognitively realistic belief updates. In human-likeness evaluation, our Bayesian target scores near a human reference (81 vs 80), while baseline LLM targets score substantially lower (64). PERSUASIONTRACE reframes persuasion evaluation from endpoint movement alone to process fidelity, providing a stronger basis for scientific analysis and safer optimization of persuasive systems.

</details>


### [115] [Self-supervised User Profile Generation for Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05336)
*Clark Mingxuan Ju,Yuwei Qiu,Tong Zhao,Neil Shah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BUMP is a self-supervised framework that trains an LLM profile generator via GRPO with a bidirectional in-batch ranking objective, using a small LLM judge to score how well profiles and held-out interactions retrieve each other via multi-positive NDCG, eliminating the need for expensive labeled downstream task rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for LLM personalization via profile generation require expensive labeled downstream task supervision (explicit rewards) for every target task, which is costly and sparse.

Method: BUMP trains a profile generator using GRPO with a bidirectional in-batch ranking objective: (i) profile-as-query retrieves the user's held-out interactions vs. others' interactions, (ii) held-out-interaction-as-query retrieves the user's profile vs. others' profiles. Both directions are scored with multi-positive NDCG, forming a dense reward without any task labels.

Result: On the LaMP benchmark, BUMP matches or outperforms closed-source APIs and prior methods that rely on labeled rewards, while requiring no task labels during training.

Conclusion: Self-supervised bidirectional ranking can effectively train profile generators for LLM personalization without any downstream labeled data, offering a scalable and label-free alternative to existing approaches.

Abstract: Personalizing large language models (LLMs) has become a central challenge as LLMs are deployed across recommendation, search, dialogue, and content generation -- settings where the same query should yield different answers given different users. A promising route is to summarize each user's interaction history into a natural-language memory or profile and prepend it to the prompt to facilitate personalization. Existing methods learn such profile generators with explicit rewards derived from labeled downstream tasks, which are expensive and sparse as they require annotated supervision for every target task. In light of this challenge, we introduce Bidirectional User Modeling via Profiles (BUMP), a self-supervised framework that trains a profile generator without any downstream labels. Specifically, given a user's interaction history, we use GRPO to train an LLM to emit a free-form textual profile under a bidirectional in-batch ranking objective: a small LLM judge measures (i) how well the generated profile, used as a query, ranks the user's own held-out interactions above interactions from other users in the batch, and (ii) how well a held-out interaction, used as a query, ranks the user's own profile above profiles of other users. Both directions are scored with multi-positive NDCG and combined into a dense reward per rollout; other users in the batch supply free negatives, so every training example yields supervision from raw interaction logs alone. Evaluated on the LaMP benchmark, BUMP matches or outperforms closed-source APIs and prior methods relying on labeled rewards, while requiring no task label at training.

</details>


### [116] [Trajectory Dynamics in Language Model Hidden States Predict Human Processing Costs Beyond Surprisal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05346)
*Elan Barenholtz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces trajectory extrapolation error, a novel measure of processing cost derived from the trajectory of hidden states in transformer language models. It predicts human reading times independently of and orthogonally to surprisal, revealing two dissociable components of incremental processing: word-level prediction error and sensitivity to local interpretive momentum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Surprisal theory reduces rich sequential representations to a single scalar per word, discarding directional information about how interpretation evolves. Dynamical-systems approaches suggest trajectory, not just position, should shape processing.

Method: At each word, fit a linear trajectory to preceding hidden states of a transformer LM; measure deviation from the extrapolated path as trajectory extrapolation error. Tested on Natural Stories corpus with self-paced reading times, across GPT-2 sizes and Pythia/RoPE architectures, with displacement control.

Result: Trajectory extrapolation error is nearly orthogonal to surprisal (r=.044), independently predicts reading times, especially in garden-path sentences, strengthens with model scale (GPT-2 Small→Large), and replicates across architectures. Displacement control shows opposite predictive direction.

Conclusion: Two dissociable processing cost components exist: surprisal (word-level prediction error) and trajectory extrapolation error (sensitivity to local momentum of unfolding interpretation).

Abstract: Human language comprehension unfolds sequentially: each word is processed in the context of those that came before, and the interpretation builds incrementally over time. Surprisal, the negative log probability of a word given its context, has been the dominant predictor of incremental processing cost. But surprisal reduces rich sequential representations to a single scalar at each word, discarding information about the direction in which the interpretation has been evolving. Dynamical-systems approaches suggest that the trajectory of the evolving interpretive state, not just its position at each moment,should shape processing, and language itself may have local momentum, since speakers plan utterances a few words at a time. We introduce trajectory extrapolation error: at each word, we fit a linear trajectory to the preceding hidden states of a transformer language model and measure deviation from the extrapolated path. On the Natural Stories corpus, this measure is nearly orthogonal to surprisal (r = .044) and independently predicts self-paced reading times. The effect is especially pronounced in garden-path sentences, strengthens with model scale (GPT-2 Small to Large), and replicates across architectures with different positional encoding schemes (GPT-2 vs. Pythia/RoPE). A displacement control shows the effect is not reducible to representational change magnitude: displacement and extrapolation error predict in opposite directions. These findings reveal two dissociable components of processing cost: word-level prediction error (surprisal) and sensitivity to the local momentum of the unfolding interpretation (trajectory extrapolation error).

</details>


### [117] [ReasoningFlow: Discourse Structures for Understanding LLM Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05402)
*Jinu Lee,Shivam Agarwal,Amruta Parulekar,Siddarth Madala,Dilek Hakkani-Tur,Julia Hockenmaier*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ReasoningFlow is a framework that converts LRM reasoning traces into fine-grained DAGs, revealing structural similarities across diverse models, enabling better monitoring of reasoning behaviors, and showing that most erroneous steps do not derive final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs produce non-linear reasoning traces (backtracking, self-correction) that are difficult to evaluate and monitor, motivating a structured framework to capture and analyze these discourse structures.

Method: Developed an annotation schema for fine-grained DAGs; manually annotated 31 traces (2.1k steps) with high inter-annotator agreement, then scaled to automatic annotation of 1,260 traces (247.7k steps) across three tasks and five models.

Result: (1) LRMs from different base models and training data produce structurally similar traces. (2) ReasoningFlow captures diverse fine-grained behaviors (local verification, self-reflection, assumptions) for better monitorability. (3) Most erroneous steps do not contribute to final answers. (4) Causal dependencies between steps do not align with language-level discourse structure.

Conclusion: ReasoningFlow provides a structured way to analyze and monitor LRM reasoning, revealing insights about error handling, structural similarities across models, and the disconnect between causal and discourse structures.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) produce reasoning traces with non-linear structures, such as backtracking and self-correction, that complicate the evaluation and monitoring of the reasoning process. We introduce ReasoningFlow, a framework that captures the discourse structures of LRM reasoning traces into fine-grained directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). We develop and validate our annotation schema through careful manual annotation of 31 traces (2.1k steps), achieving high inter-annotator agreement, then scale to automatic annotation of 1,260 traces (247.7k steps) spanning three tasks (math, science, argumentation) and five models (Qwen2.5-32B-Inst, QwQ-32B, DeepSeek-V3, DeepSeek-R1, GPT-oss-120B). By analyzing ReasoningFlow graphs, we find: (1) LRMs exhibit structurally similar traces, despite being trained from different base models and potentially non-overlapping post-training data. (2) ReasoningFlow reveals diverse fine-grained reasoning behaviors (e.g., local verification, self-reflection, and assumptions) that can be used for better reasoning trace monitorability. (3) In LRMs, most of the erroneous steps are not used to derive final answers. (4) Mechanistic causal dependencies between steps do not reflect the language-level discourse structure. We release the dataset and code in: https://github.com/jinulee-v/reasoningflow.

</details>


### [118] [When Evidence is Sparse: Weakly Supervised Early Failure Alerting in Dialogs and LLM-Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05414)
*Avinash Baidya,Xinran Liang,Ruocheng Guo,Xiang Gao,Kamalika Das*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a two-stage approach for early failure alerting in multi-turn language interactions. First, an attention-based failure predictor learns sparse turn-level failure evidence from trajectory-level labels. Second, α-STOP, a single preference-conditioned stopping policy, selects accuracy-earliness tradeoffs at inference time. Across five benchmarks, failure evidence is sparse (4.7-11.3% of turns) and delayed (appearing after 59.0-83.6% of trajectories). The approach improves Pareto-frontier quality by 3-42% over state-of-the-art trigger policies while reducing training cost by orders of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early failure alerting is challenging because supervision is trajectory-level (success/failure), but alerts must be raised from partial interactions. Prior work naively assigns terminal labels to every prefix, but this assumption is poorly matched to multi-turn language interactions where failure evidence is sparse and often delayed.

Method: A two-stage approach: (1) An attention-based failure predictor learns sparse turn-level failure evidence from trajectory labels and estimates failure risk from partial histories. (2) α-STOP, a single preference-conditioned stopping policy that selects an accuracy-earliness operating point at inference time, avoiding training separate triggers for each preference.

Result: Across five benchmarks (customer support, task-oriented dialog, persuasion, tool use, planning): failure evidence occupies only 4.7-11.3% of turns and first appears after 59.0-83.6% of trajectories. The attention-based predictor improves Pareto-frontier quality (hypervolume) by 1-10% over naive prefix supervision. The full system improves frontier quality by 3-42% over state-of-the-art trigger policies while reducing training cost per operating point by 1-3 orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: Learning from sparse and delayed failure evidence significantly outperforms naive prefix-label supervision for early failure alerting in multi-turn language interactions. The proposed approach achieves better accuracy-earliness tradeoffs with substantially lower training costs.

Abstract: Early failure alerting requires deciding, while a dialog or agent trajectory is still unfolding, whether to flag it as likely to fail. This is challenging because supervision is typically available only as a trajectory-level success/failure label while alerts must be raised from partial interactions. Prior early-classification methods often bridge this gap by assigning the terminal label to every prefix, treating every turn as failure evidence. We hypothesize that this prefix-label assumption is poorly matched to multi-turn language interactions, where evidence of eventual failure is sparse and often delayed. In this paper, we introduce a two-stage approach that learns from this sparse evidence structure and uses the resulting risk estimates for controllable early alerting. Specifically, our attention-based failure predictor learns sparse turn-level failure evidence from trajectory labels and uses it to estimate failure risk from partial histories. We then pair this predictor with $α$-STOP, a single preference-conditioned stopping policy that selects an accuracy-earliness operating point at inference time rather than training a separate trigger for each preference. Across five benchmarks spanning customer support, task-oriented dialog, persuasion, tool use, and planning, we first show that high-relevance failure evidence occupies only 4.7-11.3% of turns and first appears after 59.0-83.6\% of trajectories on average. We further show that the attention-based predictor improves Pareto-frontier quality (hypervolume) by 1-10\% over naive prefix supervision, and that the full system improves frontier quality by 3-42\% over state-of-the-art trigger policies while reducing training cost per operating point by 1-3 orders of magnitude.

</details>


### [119] [Executable Schema Contracts: From Automatic Ingestion to Multi-Source Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05415)
*Padmaja Jonnalagedda,Yuguang Yao,Xiang Gao,Hilaf Hasson,Kamalika Das*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A system that automatically discovers executable schemas from raw multi-source data, uses them as a shared contract for knowledge graph construction and query-time retrieval, and improves QA performance over baselines via schema-conditioned routing, structural intelligence, and schema-guided construction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data spans tables, documents, and semi-structured files with implicit semantics. Querying this data requires integrating evidence across inconsistent schemas and formats, yet existing approaches either demand costly manual engineering or bypass structure entirely.

Method: (1) A closed-world field catalog constrains LLM-based schema discovery to attested fields; (2) deterministic structural analysis infers identity keys, foreign keys, and source hierarchy; (3) the resulting schema drives extraction, deduplication, and cross-source linking into a provenance-aware knowledge graph; (4) at query time, a multi-tool agent routes retrieval across structured lookup, graph traversal, and vector search using the schema (optionally extended via a monotonic protocol), returning grounded answers with traceable citations.

Result: In controlled zero-shot comparisons using the same LLM, data, and evaluation harness, the system improves over retrieval-only and decomposition-based baselines across four QA benchmarks. Ablations show that schema-conditioned routing, structural intelligence, and schema-guided construction each contribute to the gains.

Conclusion: The proposed system demonstrates that automatic schema discovery and its use as a shared contract for knowledge graph construction and query-time retrieval can effectively bridge the gap between unstructured/structured data and LLM-based question answering, outperforming baselines that ignore or bypass structure.

Abstract: Real-world data spans tables, documents, and semi-structured files with implicit semantics. Querying this data requires integrating evidence across inconsistent schemas and formats, yet existing approaches either demand costly manual engineering or bypass structure entirely. We present a system that automatically discovers an executable schema from raw multi-source data and uses it as a shared contract for knowledge graph construction and query-time retrieval. A closed-world field catalog constrains LLM-based schema discovery to attested fields; deterministic structural analysis infers identity keys, foreign keys, and source hierarchy; and the resulting schema drives extraction, deduplication, and cross-source linking into a provenance-aware knowledge graph. At query time the schema -- optionally extended via a monotonic protocol -- conditions a multi-tool agent routing retrieval across structured lookup, graph traversal, and vector search, returning grounded answers with traceable citations. In controlled zero-shot comparisons using the same LLM, data, and evaluation harness, the system improves over retrieval-only and decomposition-based baselines across four QA benchmarks, with ablations showing that schema-conditioned routing, structural intelligence, and schema-guided construction each contribute to the gains.

</details>


### [120] [ComplexityMT: Benchmarking the Interaction Between Text Complexity and Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05421)
*Joseph Marvin Imperial,Junhong Liang,Belal Shoer,Abdullah Barayan,Rodrigo Wilkens,Omar Mussa,Dawn Knight,Eugénio Ribeiro,Ekaterina Kochmar,Sowmya Vajjala,Fernando Alva-Manchego,Harish Tayyar Madabushi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces ComplexityMT, a benchmark to study how text complexity (measured via CEFR levels) interacts with machine translation quality across six languages. Experiments show higher CEFR levels increase translation difficulty, and MT shifts CEFR levels of target texts compared to original sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically investigate whether machine translation preserves or alters the complexity level of source texts, which has implications for multilingual pedagogical content generation and translation difficulty estimation.

Method: The authors use CEFR levels as a measure of text complexity and evaluate three open-weight models, one closed model, and a commercial MT system across six languages (Arabic, Dutch, English, French, Hindi, Russian) on two tasks: (1) correlation between CEFR level and translation difficulty, and (2) measuring CEFR level shifts between source and target texts.

Result: Higher CEFR levels correlate with increased translation difficulty. Machine translation shifts the CEFR level of target texts compared to original source texts for most of the languages evaluated.

Conclusion: The findings provide insights for researchers and practitioners working on multilingual pedagogical content generation and machine translation difficulty estimation, highlighting the need to account for complexity shifts in MT outputs.

Abstract: When a text is translated, does the translation retain the complexity of the original? We introduce ComplexityMT, a new challenge for assessing how text complexity and machine translation interact with and influence each other, using the Common European Framework of Reference for Languages (CEFR) levels as the measure of text complexity. Across six languages, including Arabic, Dutch, English, French, Hindi, and Russian, we evaluate three open-weight models, one closed model, and a commercial machine translation system on two tasks: i) correlation of CEFR with translation difficulty, and ii) shifts in CEFR levels of the source texts. Our experiments show that higher CEFR levels make texts more difficult to translate, and that machine translation shifts the CEFR level of the target text compared to the original source, for most languages. These findings provide new insights for researchers and practitioners working on multilingual pedagogical content generation and machine translation difficulty estimation.

</details>


### [121] [Multilingual Coreference Resolution via Cycle-Consistent Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05444)
*Adriana-Valentina Costache,Eduard Poesina,Silviu-Florin Gheorghe,Paul Irofti,Radu Tudor Ionescu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a coreference resolution pipeline for low-resource languages that uses machine translation from English to generate training data, validates quality via back-translation and BERT-based cosine similarity, and weights samples by MT cycle consistency in the loss function, achieving significant gains across four low-resource languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coreference resolution is well-studied in English but receives comparatively less attention for low-resource languages, creating a gap this work aims to mitigate.

Method: (1) Use MT from English to target low-resource language to generate/expand training data; (2) Back-translate samples and compute cosine similarity with original English using BERT latent space; (3) Integrate similarity scores into the loss function to weight samples by MT cycle consistency.

Result: Significant performance gains in coreference resolution across four low-resource languages; enables accurate coreference resolution where no previous corpora existed.

Conclusion: The proposed MT-based pipeline with cycle-consistency weighting is effective for low-resource coreference resolution and can enable the task in languages lacking annotated corpora.

Abstract: Coreference resolution is a core NLP task, having a broad range of downstream applications, e.g.~machine translation, question answering, document summarization, etc. While the task is well-studied in English, comparatively less attention is dedicated to coreference resolution in other languages, especially low-resource ones. To mitigate this gap, we propose a novel coreference resolution pipeline that harnesses machine translation (MT) from English to a target low-resource language, to generate or expand training data. To automatically validate the quality of the translated samples, we back-translate the samples and assess the similarity with the original English samples via cosine similarity in the latent space of a BERT model. The resulting similarity scores are integrated into the loss function to weight training samples according to their MT cycle consistency. Extensive experiments on four low-resource languages show that our pipeline brings significant performance gains in coreference resolution. Moreover, our pipeline enables accurate coreference resolution in languages where no previous corpora were available.

</details>


### [122] [Localizing Prompt Ambiguity in Large Language Models with Probe-Targeted Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05486)
*Govind Ramesh,Yao Dou,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PRIG is a gradient attribution method that uses a linear probe trained on prompt representations to localize ambiguity at the token level, outperforming baselines on synthetic and human-written benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt ambiguity is a latent property that causes failures in LLMs, but existing attribution methods focus on observable outputs (logits/tokens) and cannot directly localize ambiguity in the prompt.

Method: PRIG trains a linear probe to distinguish clear vs. ambiguous prompts, then attributes the probe score back to earlier token representations in the residual stream using gradient attribution.

Result: Achieves 0.840 AUROC on combined synthetic benchmarks and 0.891 AUROC on a human-written gold set, outperforming gradient baselines and GPT-5.4 on sentence-level ambiguity identification.

Conclusion: Latent prompt properties like ambiguity can be localized through intermediate representations rather than output-level attribution, and PRIG is a practical tool for identifying ambiguous spans in prompts.

Abstract: Prompt ambiguity is a common source of failure in large language models, but is difficult to localize because it is a latent property of the prompt, while existing attribution methods are designed to explain observable outputs such as logits or generated tokens. We introduce PRIG, a gradient attribution method that uses a probe logit to attribute latent ambiguity to token positions. Specifically, PRIG trains a linear probe to distinguish clear prompts from ambiguous prompts and attributes the probe score to earlier token representations in the residual stream. To enable token-level evaluation, we construct synthetic ambiguity datasets across coding, math, and writing by rewriting one task-critical sentence per prompt, and complement them with a human-written gold benchmark. In this setting, PRIG localizes ambiguous spans substantially better than gradient attribution baselines, achieving 0.840 AUROC on the combined synthetic benchmark and 0.891 AUROC on the gold set. It also outperforms GPT-5.4 on sentence-level ambiguity identification and retains useful signal out-of-domain. These results establish PRIG as a practical tool for identifying which parts of a prompt are ambiguous. More broadly, they suggest that latent prompt properties can be localized through intermediate representations, rather than through output-level attribution.

</details>


### [123] [MASF: A Multi-Model Adaptive Selection Framework for Abstractive Text summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05494)
*Ahmed Alansary,Ali Hamdi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Multi-Model Adaptive Summarization Framework that integrates multiple fine-tuned transformer models with an adaptive selection mechanism to improve abstractive summarization quality and robustness. The framework generates candidate summaries from each model, evaluates them using lexical and semantic metrics, and selects the best one. Evaluated on CNN/DailyMail, it achieves 88.63% BERTScore, outperforming several LLMs like GPT3-D2, Falcon-7b, and Mpt-7b.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relying on a single summarization model leads to inconsistent quality across articles with varying structures and topics, motivating the need for a more robust multi-model approach.

Method: The framework integrates multiple fine-tuned transformer-based summarization models, each generating an independent candidate summary. An adaptive selection mechanism evaluates summaries using automatic metrics capturing lexical similarity and semantic relevance, then selects the highest-quality summary as final output.

Result: Achieves the highest BERTScore of 88.63% on CNN/DailyMail dataset, outperforming LLMs including GPT3-D2, Falcon-7b, and Mpt-7b.

Conclusion: Leveraging multiple transformer-based models within an adaptive selection strategy effectively improves the quality and robustness of automatic text summarization systems.

Abstract: Automatic text summarization has become increasingly important due to the rapid growth of digital textual information. This paper presents a Multi-Model Adaptive Summarization Framework designed to improve the robustness and quality of abstractive text summarization. Relying on a single model often leads to inconsistent summarization quality across articles with varying structures and topics. To address this limitation, the proposed framework integrates multiple fine-tuned transformer-based summarization models and introduces an adaptive selection mechanism. In this framework, each model independently generates a candidate summary for the same input article. The generated summaries are then evaluated using automatic evaluation metrics that capture both lexical similarity and semantic relevance. Based on these scores, the framework selects the highest-quality summary as the final output. The models are fine-tuned and evaluated on the widely used CNN/DailyMail news summarization dataset. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves the highest BERTScore among all compared methods with a score of 88.63%. It also outperforms several LLMs such as GPT3-D2, Falcon-7b, and Mpt-7b, highlighting its effectiveness and robustness. These findings highlight the effectiveness of leveraging multiple transformer-based models within an adaptive selection strategy to improve the quality and robustness of automatic text summarization systems.

</details>


### [124] [CHASE: Adversarial Red-Blue Teaming for Improving LLM Safety using Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05523)
*Rahul Markasserithodi,Aditya Joshi,Yuekang Li,Ishmanbir Singh,Chris Yoo,Alan Niu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CHASE is a co-evolutionary red-blue teaming framework that uses GRPO-based adversarial training to harden LLMs against black-box prompt-rewriting attacks, achieving 43.2% reduction in StrongREJECT score with 0% false refusal on benign prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defenses against prompt-rewriting attacks rely on non-scalable human curation or white-box optimization that overfits to specific model internals, leaving models brittle against adaptive black-box adversaries encountered in deployment.

Method: CHASE uses a closed-loop red-blue teaming framework with GRPO-based attacker (multiplicative reward for bypass effectiveness and intent fidelity) and two-stage defender hardening (GRPO + rejection-sampled SFT balanced with benign data), enabling template-free RL exploration.

Result: On BeaverTails and JailbreakBench against five held-out attack families (PAIR, TAP, AutoDAN, PAP, Translation), CHASE reduces mean StrongREJECT score by 43.2% with 0% false-refusal on benign prompts, and discovers latent attack primitives that transfer across mechanistically distinct attack families.

Conclusion: Template-free RL exploration in CHASE recovers transferable attack primitives, suggesting a path toward LLM safety hardening that generalizes beyond narrow adversarial training distributions.

Abstract: Despite advances in safety alignment, prompt-rewriting attacks such as persona modulation, fictional framing and persuasion-based reformulation, can bypass safety filters even on frontier models. Existing defenses either rely on non-scalable human curation or white-box optimisation that overfits to specific model internals, leaving aligned models brittle against the very class of adaptive black-box adversaries they will face in deployment. To address this gap, we introduce CHASE (Co-evolutionary Hardening through Adversarial Safety-Escalation), a closed-loop red-blue teaming framework in which a black-box attacker and a safety-aligned defender co-evolve. The attacker is trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) under a multiplicative reward that jointly enforces bypass effectiveness and intent fidelity, while the defender is hardened on the harvested adversarial rewrites through a two-stage GRPO + rejection-sampled SFT pipeline balanced with benign data. Evaluated on BeaverTails and JailbreakBench against five held-out attack families (PAIR, TAP, AutoDAN, PAP, Translation), CHASE cuts mean StrongREJECT score by 43.2\% with 0\% false-refusal on benign prompts. Beyond the headline result, CHASE shows that template-free RL exploration recovers latent attack primitives that transfer across mechanistically distinct attack families, suggesting a path toward LLM safety hardening that generalises beyond the narrow distributions achieved thus far in adversarial training.

</details>


### [125] [Multilingual Detection of Alzheimer's Disease from Speech: A Cross-Linguistic Transfer Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05545)
*Nadine Yasser Abdelhalim,Emmanuel Akinrintoyo,Nicole Salomons*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes cross-language training for multilingual Alzheimer's detection using transformer-based models, achieving 82% F1 scores across English, Chinese, Arabic, and Hindi with 0.5s inference time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing language-specific AD detection models is resource-intensive and time-consuming; a cross-lingual solution is needed for scalable global deployment.

Method: Cross-language training of transformer-based models for binary AD classification using datasets in English, Chinese, Arabic, and Hindi.

Result: Achieved 82% F1 scores across all four languages with rapid 0.5-second inference time.

Conclusion: Cross-language training enables strong cross-linguistic generalization for AD detection, supporting potential real-time screening and global deployment.

Abstract: The development of multilingual Alzheimer's Disease Dementia (AD) detection models presents significant challenges due to the resource-intensive and time-consuming nature of language-specific model training. We propose a novel solution using cross-language training to detect AD in languages beyond those used for model training. This study investigates multilingual deep learning models for detecting AD across different languages and cognitive impairment levels. Using datasets in English, Chinese, Arabic, and Hindi, we developed transformer-based models for binary AD classification. Our approach achieved F1 scores of 82\% across all languages, demonstrating strong cross-linguistic generalization. The rapid inference time (0.5 seconds) supports potential real-time screening applications, while consistent performance across languages indicates feasibility for global deployment.

</details>


### [126] [ArcANE: Do Role-Playing Language Agents Stay in Character at the Right Time?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05553)
*Woojung Song,Nalim Kim,Sangjun Song,Chaewon Heo,Jongwon Lim,Yohan Jo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ArcANE is a benchmark for evaluating whether role-playing language agents adapt to characters' psychological trajectories across narrative arcs, showing that conditioning on Character Arc outperforms other context strategies, especially for scenarios outside the source text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks only measure factual recall at a given chapter, failing to assess whether RPLAs align with characters' evolving values and psychology, particularly in unseen scenarios.

Method: ArcANE constructs a benchmark from 17 novels and 80 characters by segmenting narratives into Character Arc phases along a psychological axis, posing identical scenarios across phases (both in-source and out-of-source). Evaluates six models with six context modes, and fine-tunes open-weight models to produce ArcANE-8B/32B.

Result: Character Arc conditioning outperforms all other context strategies across all models; the advantage is largest on out-of-source scenarios where retrieval fails. Fine-tuned ArcANE-8B/32B further widen this gap.

Conclusion: Character Arc conditioning is critical for role-playing agents to exhibit temporally coherent personality evolution, especially for generalizing to scenarios beyond the source text.

Abstract: Role-playing language agents (RPLAs) should play characters whose values and behavior evolve as the story progresses, not maintain a fixed persona. Existing benchmarks measure factual recall at a given chapter, not whether responses align with the character's psychological trajectory, especially in scenarios the source text never explores. We introduce ArcANE (Arc-Aware Narrative Evaluation), an automatically constructed benchmark spanning 17 novels and 80 principal characters. A Character Arc segments the narrative into phases along a psychological axis, and each probe poses the same scenario across phases, spanning both situations within the source text and situations beyond it. Across six models and six context modes, conditioning on the Character Arc tops every other context strategy on every model, and the gap is largest on scenarios outside the source text where retrieval has nothing to find. We further fine-tune open-weight models on the same data to obtain ArcANE-8B/32B, which widen the Arc advantage even more on scenarios outside the source text.

</details>


### [127] [AURA: Intent-Directed Probing for Implicit-Need Surfacing in Situated LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05557)
*Yang Li,Jiaxiang Liu,Jiang Cai,Mingkun Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AURA enhances standard tool-use agents by inserting an inference step that produces an IntentFrame—a structured estimate of implicit user needs with a scalar gap score controlling probe budget and tool selection—achieving better implicit-intent coverage (+0.07, p<10^-6) on a 100-query benchmark while reducing probes by 82% and eliminating forbidden-tool violations on privacy-sensitive data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Situated queries often encode implicit needs beyond their literal content (e.g., "where is Lin Wei?" may also imply checking availability or mood), but standard tool-use agents answer literally and stop, missing these unstated intents.

Method: AURA inserts an IntentFrame inference step between scene perception and tool use, which produces a structured estimate of the implicit need with a scalar gap score that dynamically controls per-query probe budget and tool selection.

Result: On a 100-query four-scene implicit-intent benchmark, AURA improves implicit-need coverage by +0.07 (p<10^-6) over ReAct-style probing; three of four scenes are individually significant; gains reproduce on a second backbone; a prompt ablation confirms the lift comes from gap calibration rather than memorization. On factual lookup, it trades raw accuracy for 82% fewer probes and zero forbidden-tool violations on a privacy-sensitive slice.

Conclusion: AURA effectively bridges the gap between literal query answering and implicit need satisfaction, with scope conditions detailed in Limitations. Code, simulator, and benchmark are publicly released.

Abstract: A situated query like "where is Lin Wei?" often encodes more than its literal content: the user may also want to know whether Lin Wei is free, in a good mood, or worth interrupting now. Standard tool-use agents answer the literal question and stop. AURA inserts an inference step between scene perception and tool use that produces an IntentFrame: a structured estimate of the implicit need with a scalar gap score that controls per-query probe budget and tool selection. On a 100-query four-scene implicit-intent benchmark, AURA improves implicit-need coverage over ReAct-style probing (Delta = +0.07, p < 10^-6); three of four scenes are individually significant, the gain reproduces on a second backbone, and a prompt ablation attributes the lift to gap calibration rather than answer memorisation. On factual lookup the controller trades raw accuracy for 82% fewer probes and zero forbidden-tool violations on a privacy-sensitive slice; scope conditions are detailed in Limitations. Code, simulator, and benchmark are released at https://github.com/innovation64/AURA.

</details>


### [128] [InfoShield: Privacy-Preserving Speech Representations for Mental Health Screening via Information-Theoretic Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05561)
*Xueyang Wu,Siyuan Liu,Kezhuo Yang,Guang Ling*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: InfoShield is a privacy-preserving framework for speech-based depression detection that uses mutual information minimization to protect demographic attributes (gender, age) while maintaining diagnostic accuracy. It introduces TimeAwareMINE to handle temporal-static misalignment in sequential speech, achieving significant privacy gains (gender inference drops from 92.6% to 55.5%, age from 55.7% to 30.3%) with only 6% F1 reduction (F1=0.784 vs prior SOTA 0.723).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical deployment of speech-based mental health screening is hindered by users' privacy concerns regarding demographic information exposure.
