Existing methods like adversarial training fail against unseen threats, while Differential Privacy degrades diagnostic performance by indiscriminately injecting noise.

Method: InfoShield minimizes mutual information between speech representations and sensitive attributes while preserving depression classification accuracy. It introduces TimeAwareMINE with cross-modal attention to address temporal-static misalignment between sequential acoustic frames and static attribute embeddings, overcoming limitations of standard MINE estimators.

Result: On the Androids Corpus, InfoShield reduces gender inference accuracy from 92.6% to 55.5% and age inference from 55.7% to 30.3%, with limited utility loss (6% F1 reduction), achieving F1=0.784 compared to prior SOTA's 0.723.

Conclusion: InfoShield effectively resolves the privacy-utility trade-off in speech-based depression screening by selectively removing demographic information without sacrificing diagnostic performance, outperforming prior state-of-the-art approaches.

Abstract: Speech-based mental health screening offers scalable depression detection, yet clinical deployment faces a significant barrier: users' privacy concerns about demographic information exposure. Current techniques struggle to resolve this conflict. Adversarial training often fails against unseen threats, whereas Differential Privacy tends to compromise diagnostic performance by injecting noise across all features. This paper presents InfoShield, which minimizes mutual information between speech representations and sensitive attributes while preserving depression classification accuracy. We identify that standard MINE estimators struggle with sequential speech due to temporal-static misalignment, and introduce TimeAwareMINE with cross-modal attention to align acoustic frames with attribute embeddings. Experiments on the Androids Corpus show InfoShield reduces gender inference from 92.6\% to 55.5\% and age inference from 55.7\% to 30.3\% with limited utility loss (6\% F1 reduction), achieving F1=0.784 compared to prior SOTA's 0.723.

</details>


### [129] [Using Large Language Models to Support High Volume Application Review for an Undergraduate Research Program](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05564)
*Varun Aggarwal,Kay Kobak,John Howarter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This work-in-progress paper describes the development and initial deployment of an LLM-based tool (using OpenAI GPT models) to evaluate ~1,200 undergraduate research fellowship Statements of Purpose at Purdue University. The system scored submissions across six rubric subcategories, generated rationales and excerpts, processed the full batch in ~4.6 hours, and reduced coordinator review time from weeks to ~4 hours.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The evaluation of thousands of undergraduate research fellowship applications (e.g., SURF at Purdue) requires significant staff time and effort, and maintaining consistency and meeting tight deadlines is challenging. The authors aim to automate and assist the initial screening process to reduce human workload and improve efficiency.

Method: The authors developed an LLM-based workflow using OpenAI GPT models (GPT-4o, GPT-5-mini, GPT-5.2). A structured rubric with six subcategories (each scored 0-3) was designed. A few human-graded SoPs were used for prompt tuning. The prompt instructed the model to output numerical scores, rationales (positive/negative aspects), and short excerpts. The full batch of 1,200 SoPs was processed via GPT-5.2 in ~4.6 hours (~14 seconds/SoP).

Result: GPT-5.2 processed 1,200 SoPs in ~4.6 hours (~14 sec/SoP). GPT-5.2 showed the best rubric adherence. Model score disagreements were more pronounced for lower-scoring submissions. The coordinator's review of LLM-annotated outputs was completed in ~4 hours (vs. multi-weeks previously).

Conclusion: The LLM-based tool effectively replicated the role of distributed human graders, providing scored and rationale-annotated outputs that enabled a single program coordinator to review the full applicant pool in ~4 hours, drastically reducing evaluation time from weeks. The approach shows promise for scaling application review in high-volume fellowship programs.

Abstract: Undergraduate research programs such as the Summer Undergraduate Research Fellowship (SURF) at Purdue University receive thousands of applications every year, requiring significant time and effort for program staff to evaluate each submission consistently and within tight timelines. This work-in-progress paper describes the development and initial deployment of a large language model (LLM)-based tool to assist in the evaluation of approximately 1,200 student Statements of Purpose (SoPs) for the SURF 2026 cycle at Purdue University. The workflow utilizes OpenAI GPT models (GPT-4o, GPT-5-mini, and GPT-5.2) and uses a structured rubric across six subcategories, each scored on a 0-3 scale. A few SoPs, graded by program staff, were used to tune the model responses. The model prompt was designed to generate both numerical scores, rationales (including positive and negative aspects) and short excerpts from each submission. Using GPT-5.2, the full batch of 1,200 SoPs was processed in approximately 4.6 hours of compute time, averaging roughly 14 seconds per SoP (with per-SoP timing varying with SoP length, which ranged from 500 to 2,000 words). Notable differences in rubric adherence were observed across model versions, with GPT-5.2 adhering most closely. Disagreement in model scores was more pronounced for lower-scoring submissions. The LLM outputs replicated the role previously played by distributed human graders, providing the program coordinator with scored and rationale-annotated outputs for the entire applicant pool. The program coordinator then reviewed these outputs alongside each applicant's SoP, applying the same downstream office criteria used in prior SURF cycles, to produce a shortlist of strong candidates. This coordinator review was completed in approximately 4 hours, compared to the multi-week coordination effort required in prior program cycles.

</details>


### [130] [Domain-Aware Mispronunciation Detection and Diagnosis Using Language-Specific Statistical Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05569)
*Huu Tuong Tu,Hanh Nguyen,Thien Van Luong,Nguyen Tien Cuong,Vu Huan,Nguyen Thi Thu Trang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a method using statistical graphs to learn phoneme confusion patterns for Mispronunciation Detection and Diagnosis (MDD), incorporating a language-specific strategy to handle pronunciation differences across native language backgrounds, achieving 59.52% F1-score on L2-ARCTIC benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MDD is increasingly important for computer-assisted language learning and speech technology, but systematic pronunciation differences across L1 backgrounds remain challenging.

Method: Constructs statistical graphs representing phoneme confusion patterns as directed graphs, and introduces a language-specific strategy to capture systematic pronunciation differences across various native language backgrounds.

Result: Achieves 59.52% F1-score on the L2-ARCTIC benchmark, outperforming several competitive baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed graph-based approach with language-specific strategy is effective for MDD, validated through extensive experiments.

Abstract: Mispronunciation Detection and Diagnosis (MDD) has gained increasing importance in computer-assisted language learning and speech technology in recent years. In this paper, we propose a method for constructing statistical graphs that enable models to learn phoneme confusion patterns represented as directed graphs. Furthermore, we introduce a language-specific strategy to capture systematic pronunciation differences across various native language (L1) backgrounds. The effectiveness of our approach is demonstrated through extensive experiments on the L2-ARCTIC benchmark, where it achieves an F1-score of 59.52%, outperforming several competitive baselines.

</details>


### [131] [TensorBench: Benchmarking Coding Agents on a Compiler-Based Tensor Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05570)
*Bobby Yan,Fredrik Kjolstad*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TensorBench is a benchmark of 199 coding tasks on a tensor compiler framework, evaluating seven coding agents with pass rates from 64.8% to 22.1%, where different agents pass different task subsets (low inter-agent agreement).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repository-level coding benchmarks face a trade-off between task difficulty and evaluation reliability; tasks that challenge frontier models often involve large codebases with incomplete test coverage, while human review does not scale.

Method: Create 199 feature-addition and refactoring tasks on an open-source tensor compiler framework (TensorBench) based on PyTorch, covering sparse formats, optimization passes, IR transformations, scheduler changes, runtime components, and numerical operators. Grade by applying agent's patch and running the framework's test suite (pre-existing tests + agent-added tests); pass requires preserving pre-existing behavior and satisfying agent-added checks.

Result: Seven coding agents evaluated across three frontier model families and one open-weight model. Pass rates range from 64.8% (strongest) to 22.1% (weakest). Low inter-agent agreement: Cohen's κ ranges from -0.07 to 0.43, with κ=0.05 for the two strongest agents.

Conclusion: TensorBench provides a benchmark that balances difficulty and reliability via automated test-based grading. The low agreement between agents suggests diverse failure modes and complementary strengths, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation.

Abstract: Repository-level coding benchmarks face a trade-off between task difficulty and evaluation reliability: tasks that challenge frontier models often involve large codebases with incomplete test coverage, while human review does not scale. We introduce TensorBench, a benchmark of 199 feature-addition and refactoring tasks on an open-source compiler-based tensor framework that extends PyTorch with first-class support for dense and sparse tensors. Tasks cover new sparse formats, dense optimization passes, IR transformations, scheduler changes, runtime components, and high-level numerical operators. TensorBench grades each run by applying the agent's patch and running the framework's test suite, which includes the pre-existing randomized regression tests and any tests the agent adds. For feature-addition tasks, a pass means that the patched repository preserves the tested pre-existing behavior and satisfies the agent-added checks for the requested feature. We evaluate seven coding agents spanning three frontier model families and one open-weight model. Pass rates under this criterion range from $64.8\%$ for the strongest agent to $22.1\%$ for the weakest. Agents pass different subsets of tasks: pairwise Cohen's $κ$ ranges from $-0.07$ to $0.43$, with $κ= 0.05$ for the two strongest agents.

</details>


### [132] [Predictable Scaling Laws of Optimal Hyperparameters for LLM Continued Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05610)
*Yongwei Zhou,Juncheng Diao,Junlin Shang,Peiguang Li,Rongxiang Weng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper discovers scaling laws for optimal hyperparameters (learning rate, batch size) during continued pre-training of LLMs, and proposes a two-stage framework that predicts optimal hyperparameters by estimating a checkpoint's "equivalent pre-training compute" via inverse scaling laws, reducing hyperparameter search overhead by up to 90% while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practices for hyperparameter selection in continued pre-training rely on heuristics or expensive grid searches, leading to training instability and excessive costs. The paper aims to find principled, efficient ways to predict optimal hyperparameters given a starting checkpoint and compute budget.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) Empirical Law Discovery — train small-scale proxy models to derive functions mapping compute budget to optimal hyperparameters via standard loss-compute scaling laws; (2) State-Aware Hyperparameter Prediction — evaluate the initial checkpoint's validation loss and use inverse scaling law to estimate its "equivalent pre-training compute," then combine with planned compute budget to predict optimal hyperparameters for the target run.

Result: The method reduces hyperparameter search overhead by up to 90% while achieving comparable or superior performance relative to baselines. The framework is model-agnostic and generalizes across architectures.

Conclusion: Optimal hyperparameters follow stable, predictable scaling laws during continued pre-training, enabling efficient, principled hyperparameter prediction without costly search. The method provides a generalizable solution for diverse continued pre-training scenarios starting from any checkpoint.

Abstract: The efficacy of continued pre-training for Large Language Models (LLMs) hinges upon hyperparameter configurations, such as learning rate and batch size. However, current practices often rely on heuristics or grid searches, leading to training instability and excessive costs. In this work, we first empirically discover that optimal hyperparameters follow stable and predictable scaling laws throughout the continued pre-training process. Leveraging these insights, we propose a novel framework to establish quantitative relationships between compute budget and optimal hyperparameters for a given checkpoint. Our approach has two stages: (1) \textit{Empirical Law Discovery}, where we train small-scale proxy models to derive functions mapping compute budget to optimal hyperparameters via standard loss-compute scaling laws; and (2) \textit{State-Aware Hyperparameter Prediction}, where we evaluate an initial checkpoint's validation loss and use the inverse scaling law to estimate its \textit{equivalent pre-training compute} -- the compute needed to achieve the same loss from scratch. Combining this with the planned compute budget, we predict optimal hyperparameters for the target run. Empirical results demonstrate that our method reduces the hyperparameter search overhead by up to 90\% while achieving comparable or superior performance relative to baselines. This model-agnostic framework generalizes across architectures, providing a principled and efficient methodology for diverse continued pre-training scenarios starting from any given point.

</details>


### [133] [What's in a Name? Morphological Shortcuts by LLMs in Pharmacology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05616)
*Kaijie Mo,Thomas Yang,Chantal Shaib,Qing Yao,William Rudman,Ramez Kouzy,Kanishka Misra,Byron C. Wallace,Junyi Jessy Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs leverage morphological affixes (prefixes/suffixes) to infer drug meanings, leading to overgeneralization and plausible but incorrect clinical reasoning about fictitious drugs like "wugcillin." The study localizes this "affix heuristic" behavior to early-mid transformer layers via activation patching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLMs rely on morphological shortcuts (affix heuristics) in high-stakes medical domains, where such overgeneralization could produce dangerous, convincingly wrong outputs about drugs.

Method: Behavioral and mechanistic study using fictitious drug names built from real affixes; a framework to disentangle whether model semantics are driven by affix, stem, or whole drug name; tested across 653 drugs with activation patching to localize neural correlates.

Result: Affix signals alone elicit class-level pharmacological responses across models. Models often induce drug meaning via affix cues but rarely explicitly indicate this reliance, and sometimes incorrectly conflate properties among affix-sharing drugs. The behavior is localized to early-mid transformer layers.

Conclusion: Morphological shortcuts (affix heuristics) pose a subtle but measurable safety risk in LLMs, particularly in high-stakes domains like pharmacology.

Abstract: The morphological form of a word can often give cues to its meaning, but purely relying on these mappings can lead to overgeneralization in high-stakes domains. In the medical domain, for instance, LLMs can confidently reason about fictitious drugs from their affixes alone (e.g., wugcillin) and generate plausible-looking clinical content. We present a behavioral and mechanistic study of LLM "affix heuristics" in pharmacology. Using fictitious drug names built from real affixes, we show that affix signals alone elicit class-level pharmacological responses. We introduce a framework for identifying whether a model's drug semantics are driven mainly by the affix, the stem, or the drug name as a whole. Applied across 653 drugs, our framework reveals that models often induce drug meaning primarily through affix cues, yet rarely explicitly indicate this reliance, and sometimes incorrectly conflate properties among affix-sharing drugs. Activation patching across models further localizes this behavior to early-mid layers. These findings show that morphological shortcuts pose a subtle but measurable risk to safety.

</details>


### [134] [An ERP Study on Recursive Locative Processing in Mandarin-Speaking Children with Autism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05620)
*Xiaoyi Wang,Chenxi Fu,Ziman Zhuang,Caimei Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This ERP study found that Mandarin-speaking children with ASD show reduced early structural prediction (P200), increased semantic integration demands (N400), and diminished syntactic reanalysis (P600) during recursive processing, along with greater neural variability, supporting a cascading processing deficit account.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursion is fundamental to hierarchical language but imposes high processing demands. Despite known syntactic difficulties in ASD, the temporal dynamics of recursive processing remain poorly understood.

Method: 24 children (12 ASD, 12 TD) completed a cross-modal sentence-picture matching task with two-level recursive locative constructions in Mandarin. ERPs (P200, N400, P600) were analyzed across three processing stages with mental age controlled.

Result: TD children showed clear P200 and P600 modulation to structural mismatch; ASD children showed attenuated early differentiation, reduced late reanalysis, enhanced N400 under mismatch, and greater inter-individual variability in hemispheric lateralization.

Conclusion: Reduced early predictive engagement in ASD leads to cascading effects: increased integration costs and diminished reanalysis efficiency during recursive processing. Temporal dynamics and neural variability are key to understanding language differences in ASD.

Abstract: Recursion enables the generation of hierarchical linguistic structures but imposes substantial processing demands during real-time comprehension. While difficulties with complex syntax have been reported in autism spectrum disorder (ASD), the temporal dynamics of recursive processing remain poorly understood. This study used event-related potentials (ERPs) to examine how Mandarin-speaking children with ASD process two-level recursive locative constructions. Twenty-four children (12 ASD, 12 typically developing, TD) participated in a cross-modal sentence-picture matching task. Neural responses were analyzed across three processing stages associated with structural prediction (P200), semantic integration (N400), and syntactic reanalysis (P600), with mental age controlled. Results revealed a systematic divergence between groups. TD children showed clear P200 and P600 modulation in response to structural mismatch, whereas ASD children exhibited attenuated early differentiation and reduced late reanalysis effects. In contrast, ASD children showed enhanced N400 responses under mismatch conditions, indicating increased semantic integration demands. In addition, the ASD group displayed significantly greater inter-individual variability in hemispheric lateralization, although lateralization strength was not associated with receptive vocabulary performance. These findings support a cascading account in which reduced early predictive engagement in ASD leads to increased integration costs and diminished reanalysis efficiency during recursive processing. More broadly, the results highlight the importance of both temporal processing dynamics and neural variability in understanding language differences in ASD.

</details>


### [135] [AdaPlanBench: Evaluating Adaptive Planning in Large Language Model Agents under World and User Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05622)
*Jiayu Liu,Cheng Qian,Zhenhailong Wang,Bingxuan Li,Jiateng Liu,Heng Wang,Jeonghwan Kim,Yumeng Wang,Xiusi Chen,Yi R. Fung,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AdaPlanBench is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents' adaptive planning under progressively revealed dual (world+user) constraints, built on 307 household tasks with a multi-turn interaction protocol. Experiments show the best model achieves only 67.75% accuracy, with performance degrading as constraints accumulate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks underexplore adaptive planning under progressively revealed dual (world and user) constraints that real-world problems often involve.

Method: AdaPlanBench: a dynamic interactive benchmark built on 307 household tasks with a scalable constraint construction pipeline that augments each task with dual constraints. Uses a multi-turn protocol where hidden constraints are revealed only upon plan violation, requiring iterative plan revision under accumulating feedback.

Result: Best model (out of ten leading LLMs) reaches only 67.75% accuracy; performance degrades as constraints accumulate; user constraints pose a particularly large challenge; failures often stem from weaker physical grounding and reduced effectiveness.

Conclusion: AdaPlanBench serves as a challenging testbed for dual-constrained interactive planning, highlighting that reliable adaptation to dynamically revealed constraints remains an open challenge for LLM agents.

Abstract: Planning for real-world problems by language models often involves both world and user constraints, which may not be fully specified upfront and are progressively disclosed through interaction. However, existing benchmarks still underexplore adaptive planning under such progressively revealed dual constraints. To address this gap, we introduce AdaPlanBench, a dynamic interactive benchmark for evaluating whether Large Language Model (LLM) agents can adaptively plan and re-plan under progressively revealed world and user constraints. AdaPlanBench is built on 307 household tasks, with a scalable constraint construction pipeline that augments each task with dual constraints. At runtime, agents interact with the environment in a multi-turn protocol where hidden constraints are revealed only when the agent proposes a plan that violates them, requiring iterative plan revision under accumulating feedback. This makes planning challenging, as agents must infer and track constraints from feedback while re-planning effectively. Experiments on ten leading LLMs show that adaptive planning under dual constraints remains challenging, with the best model reaching only 67.75% accuracy. We further observe that performance degrades as more constraints accumulate, with user constraints posing a particularly large challenge and failures often stemming from weaker physical grounding and reduced effectiveness. These results establish AdaPlanBench as a testbed for dual-constrained interactive planning and highlight the challenge of reliable adaptation to dynamically revealed constraints in LLM agents.

</details>


### [136] [When New Generators Arrive: Lifelong Machine-Generated Text Attribution via Ridge Feature Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05626)
*Zhen Sun,Yifan Liao,Zhicong Huang,Jiaheng Wei,Cheng Hong,Yutao Yue,Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes RidgeFT, a lightweight analytic update framework for lifelong machine-generated text attribution that uses frozen encoders, class-level sufficient statistics, and closed-form ridge regression to achieve replay-free incremental learning, outperforming baselines across multiple evaluation settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lifelong MGT attribution methods struggle to balance adapting to new generators while retaining knowledge of previously seen ones, particularly without exemplar replay.

Method: RidgeFT trains a task-aware encoder on the initial generator set, stores compact class-wise sufficient statistics per generator class, freezes the encoder for replay-free updates, suppresses generator-irrelevant variation via covariance calibration, improves representation with fixed random features, and updates new classes through closed-form ridge regression using class-level statistics.

Result: Across multi-topic evaluations with various initial generator setups, RidgeFT consistently achieves the best macro-F1 across domains, backbones, and incremental protocols, improving both old-class retention and new-class adaptation.

Conclusion: Feature-stable analytic updates provide a simple yet effective approach to lifelong MGT attribution, offering replay-free, lightweight continual learning.

Abstract: Machine-generated text (MGT) attribution aims to identify the specific generator responsible for a given text, thereby providing fine-grained evidence for model accountability and misuse investigation. As new large language models continue to emerge, attribution models must continuously incorporate new generators while preserving their ability to recognize previously seen ones. Prior works have shown that this lifelong MGT attribution setting is challenging, and existing methods often struggle to achieve a stable balance between adapting to new classes and retaining old ones. To address this issue, we propose RidgeFT, a lightweight analytic update framework that does not rely on exemplar replay. RidgeFT trains a task-aware encoder on the initial generator set, stores compact class-wise sufficient statistics when each generator class is first observed, and then freezes the encoder for replay-free closed-form updates. It then suppresses generator-irrelevant variation through covariance calibration, improves representation capacity with fixed random features, and updates new classes through closed-form ridge regression based on class-level sufficient statistics. Across multi-topic evaluations with varying initial generator setups, RidgeFT consistently outperforms baselines. It achieves the best macro-F1 across domains, backbones, and incremental protocols, while also improving both old-class retention and new-class adaptation. These results suggest that feature-stable analytic updates provide a simple yet effective approach to lifelong MGT attribution.

</details>


### [137] [Bootstrapping Semantic Layer from Execution for Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05634)
*Youngwon Lee,Jaejin Kim,Seung-won Hwang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GATE introduces a method for text-to-SQL that bootstraps missing grounding hypotheses from execution feedback, accumulating reusable execution-grounded memory to handle under-specified user queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world text-to-SQL often suffers from under-specification where user phrases need grounding in database values. Prior work requires pre-defined semantic layers that are often incomplete, especially in expert domains with under-documented conventions.

Method: GATE keeps multiple grounding hypotheses open, executes already-grounded SQL parts to obtain observations, then selects the hypothesis supported by execution feedback, storing it as a memory entry that records what was tested and how the open part should be written in SQL.

Result: Across real-world and controlled benchmarks, GATE consistently improves over strong baselines.

Conclusion: Execution can serve not only as validation but also as a bootstrapping mechanism for reusable memory in text-to-SQL, allowing later steps to reuse supported groundings.

Abstract: Real-world text-to-SQL is often under-specified until user phrases are grounded in how the database stores values. Prior work attempts to address this by requiring a semantic layer to specify groundings in advance, but such specifications are often incomplete, especially in expert domains where domain-specific conventions are under-documented. As this leaves multiple grounding hypotheses open for the same SQL part, we introduce GATE (Grouding After Test from Execution), which bootstraps missing groundings from execution feedback. GATE keeps grounding hypotheses open while executing the already grounded parts to obtain observations. Then, only the hypothesis supported by that observation is grounded and stored as a memory entry, recording what was tested and how the open part should be written in SQL. These entries accumulate into execution-grounded memory, allowing later steps to reuse supported groundings. Across real-world and controlled benchmarks, GATE consistently improves over strong baselines, demonstrating that execution can serve not only as validation but also as a bootstrapping mechanism for reusable memory in text-to-SQL.

</details>


### [138] [QueryAgent-R1: Bridging Query Generation and Product Retrieval for E-Commerce Query Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05671)
*Dike Sun,Zheng Zou,Jingtong Zang,Qi Sun,Huaipeng Zhaoand Tao Luo,Xiaoyi Zeng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: QueryAgent-R1 is a memory-augmented agentic framework for e-commerce query recommendation that uses chain-of-retrieval optimization to jointly optimize query relevance and downstream product engagement, achieving 2.9% CTR and 3.1% CVR improvements in online A/B tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing query recommendation methods optimize query-level relevance but neglect downstream product alignment, causing high CTR but low CVR. This gap between query popularity and product conversion needs bridging.

Method: Proposes QueryAgent-R1: (1) a memory-augmented agentic framework with chain-of-retrieval optimization that grounds query generation in real inventory retrieval; (2) a consistency reward in agentic RL to jointly optimize query relevance and downstream engagement; (3) a memory abstraction module for efficient user profiling.

Result: Outperforms strong baselines on both proprietary industrial data and public datasets. Online A/B tests on a large-scale production platform show 2.9% improvement in Query CTR and 3.1% improvement in guided CVR.

Conclusion: The chain-of-retrieval optimization paradigm effectively bridges the gap between query relevance and product-level conversion, demonstrating that aligning query generation with downstream product retrieval yields better end-to-end e-commerce search performance.

Abstract: Query recommendation in e-commerce search aims to proactively suggest queries that match users' potential interests. However, existing methods mainly optimize query-level relevance, while neglecting whether the retrieved products align with users' downstream preferences. This mismatch often leads to high query click through rates (CTR) but low product conversion rates (CVR). To bridge this gap, we propose QueryAgent-R1, a memory-augmented agentic framework that improves end-to-end alignment via chain-of-retrieval optimization. Our QueryAgent-R1 grounds query generation in real inventory retrieval, allowing the agent to validate and refine queries based on retrieved products. We also design a consistency reward in the agentic reinforcement learning (RL) process to jointly optimize query relevance and downstream engagement. In addition, we construct a memory abstraction module for efficient user profiling. To support offline evaluation, we construct two datasets based on both proprietary industrial data and public datasets, on which QueryAgent-R1 consistently outperforms strong baselines. Moreover, on a large scale production platform, QueryAgent-R1 improves Query CTR by 2.9% and guided CVR by 3.1% in online A/B tests.

</details>


### [139] [Value-and-Structure Alignment for Routing-Consistent Quantization of Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05688)
*Hancheol Park,Geonho Lee,Tairen Piao,Tae-Ho Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: VSRAQ is a post-training quantization method for Mixture-of-Experts models that preserves pre-quantization expert-selection behavior by aligning both routing values (logits/scores) and routing structures (expert ordering and top-k boundaries), reducing quantization-induced degradation without inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE models are sensitive to routing instability under quantization—small perturbations can alter top-k expert selection, changing computation paths and degrading quality. Existing quantization methods don't address this MoE-specific issue.

Method: VSRAQ combines value alignment (matching routing-relevant logits/scores) and structure alignment (preserving expert ordering and top-k decision boundaries) as a post-training quantization objective that maintains routing consistency.

Result: VSRAQ improves expert-selection consistency and outperforms reconstruction-only and router-aware baselines on recent MoE foundation models, without any inference-time overhead.

Conclusion: Preserving routing behavior during quantization is crucial for MoE models; VSRAQ achieves this through joint value-and-structure alignment and integrates seamlessly with existing quantization frameworks.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale foundation models efficiently by activating only a subset of experts for each token, but their large number of expert parameters still makes quantization essential for practical deployment. Unlike dense models, however, MoE models are sensitive to routing instability: small quantization-induced perturbations can change the top-$k$ expert selection, altering the computation path and degrading model quality. We propose Value-and-Structure Routing Alignment for Quantization (VSRAQ), a MoE-specific post-training quantization objective that preserves pre-quantization expert-selection behavior under quantization. VSRAQ combines two complementary objectives that jointly preserve expert-selection behavior: value alignment, which matches routing-relevant logits or scores, and structure alignment, which preserves expert ordering and top-$k$ decision boundaries. By maintaining routing consistency, VSRAQ reduces quantization-induced degradation without introducing any inference-time overhead and can be integrated into existing quantization frameworks. Experiments on recent MoE foundation models show that VSRAQ improves expert-selection consistency and consistently outperforms reconstruction-only and router-aware baselines.

</details>


### [140] [Rethinking LoRA Memory Through the Lens of KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05698)
*Chunsheng Zuo,Liaoyaqi Wang,William Jurayj,William Fleshman,Benjamin Van Durme*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates the interaction between parametric retrieval augmentation (document LoRA) and context-side memory (KV cache) in document-level QA, finding that LoRA adds value mainly when the KV cache is aggressively compressed, recovering 13-21 ROUGE-L points with no document context, and that QA-supervised adapters outperform those trained with next-token prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The interaction between parameter-side memory (LoRA adapters) and context-side memory (KV cache) in retrieval-augmented generation remains poorly understood, motivating a systematic study of when and how these two memory channels complement each other.

Method: The authors progressively evict document key-value states from the KV cache and measure the incremental contribution of document LoRA adapters under varying levels of retained context in document-level question answering tasks.

Result: Document LoRA provides minimal benefit when the KV cache is largely intact, but recovers 13-21 ROUGE-L points under aggressive compression (no document context); gains are largest when LoRA is applied during answer generation rather than during document encoding; QA-supervised adapters significantly outperform those trained with next-token prediction on raw context.

Conclusion: Document LoRA is better understood as decoding-time parametric memory that serves as a complementary memory channel, becoming valuable precisely when context-side evidence in the KV cache is scarce.

Abstract: Parametric retrieval augmentation encodes document information into lightweight, document-specific modules such as LoRA adapters, reducing the need to include all evidence as input context. However, it remains unclear how this parameter-side memory interacts with context-side memory stored in the KV cache. We study this interaction in document-level question answering by progressively evicting document key-value states and measuring when a document LoRA contributes beyond the retained context. We find that document LoRA adds little when the KV cache is largely intact, but becomes increasingly useful under aggressive compression, recovering 13-21 ROUGE-L points when no document context remains. The gain is largest when the base model encodes the document, and the adapter is applied only during answer generation, suggesting that document LoRA is better understood as decoding-time parametric memory than as a document encoder. Finally, QA-style supervision produces substantially stronger adapters than raw-context next-token-prediction. These results position document LoRA as a complementary memory channel whose value emerges precisely when context-side evidence is scarce.

</details>


### [141] [Beyond tokens: a unified framework for latent communication in LLM-based multi-agent systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05711)
*Yingzhuo Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified framework to categorize the emerging literature on latent communication in LLM-based multi-agent systems, where agents exchange continuous representations (embeddings, hidden states, KV-caches) instead of natural language, analyzing 18 methods (2024-2026) across three axes: what is communicated, sender-receiver alignment, and fusion mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural language as the communication protocol in LLM multi-agent systems suffers from high inference cost, irreversible information loss during discretization, and ambiguity/redundancy. Latent communication (exchanging continuous representations) offers a potential alternative to bypass the text generation bottleneck.

Method: The authors propose a 3-axis framework: (1) WHAT information is communicated (Embeddings, Hidden States, KV-Caches, or other continuous state); (2) WHICH sender-receiver alignment is used (latent-space alignment and layer alignment); (3) HOW the communicated information is fused into the receiver (concatenation, prepending, mathematical operations, cross-attention, or cache restoration). They categorize 18 representative methods from 2024-2026 under this framework.

Result: Identified five major design patterns in latent communication literature and surfaced open challenges including cross-architecture alignment, security of latent channels, compression for edge deployment, and the relationship between latent communication and latent chain-of-thought.

Conclusion: The framework provides a structured vocabulary for comparing future work and lowers the barrier to entry for new researchers in the latent communication paradigm for LLM-based multi-agent systems.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems built on large language models (LLMs) have become a prevailing paradigm for tackling complex reasoning, planning, and tool-use tasks. The dominant communication protocol in such systems is natural language: agents exchange messages token-by-token, verbalising their internal reasoning so that peers can read, verify, and respond. While convenient and interpretable, this protocol suffers from three structural drawbacks -- high inference cost, irreversible information loss during discretization, and ambiguity/redundancy of natural language. A growing body of work therefore explores an alternative protocol -- latent communication -- in which agents exchange continuous representations (embeddings, hidden states, or KV-caches) directly, bypassing the bottleneck of text generation. This paper presents a unified framework for organising the rapidly expanding literature on latent communication. We analyse existing methods along three orthogonal axes: (1) WHAT information is communicated (Embeddings, Hidden States, KV-Caches, or other continuous state); (2) WHICH sender-receiver alignment is used (latent-space alignment and layer alignment); and (3) HOW the communicated information is fused into the receiver (concatenation, prepending, mathematical operations, cross-attention, or cache restoration). Under this 3-axis framework, we systematically categorise eighteen representative methods proposed between 2024 and 2026, identify five major design patterns, and surface a set of open challenges -- including cross-architecture alignment, security of latent channels, compression for edge deployment, and the relationship between latent communication and latent chain-of-thought. We hope that this framework both lowers the barrier to entry for new researchers and provides a vocabulary for comparing future work.

</details>


### [142] [Interpreting Style Representations via Style-Eliciting Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05716)
*Junghwan Kim,David Jurgens*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes learning style-eliciting prompts—natural language instructions that capture stylistic attributes—to interpret style representations. Using 1,010 features across 26 categories, they train a decoder to recover prompts from style representations, outperforming baselines in style description and imitation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Style representations from existing models are difficult to interpret, and prior LLM-based description methods suffer from biases, hallucinations, and lack of explicit objectives or practical utility.

Method: Curated 1,010 style features across 26 categories; generated a dataset by prompting an LLM with these features; trained a decoder to map style representations back to style-eliciting prompts.

Result: Outperforms strong baselines (directly prompting LLMs with target text) on style prompt recovery, style imitation, and steering LLM outputs to match human-written text.

Conclusion: Style-eliciting prompts provide a practical and interpretable interface to stylistic information encoded in style representations.

Abstract: Style representation learning is a powerful tool for authorship analysis and modeling writing style, yet the latent nature of learned representations makes them difficult to interpret. Recent work has attempted to explain these representations by generating natural language descriptions with large language models (LLMs) conditioned on input text. However, such descriptions are often prone to the LLM's biases and hallucinations, and they lack an explicit objective and practical utility. In this work, we propose a novel framework for interpreting style representations through style-eliciting prompts: natural language instructions designed to steer LLMs to generate text that reflects specific stylistic attributes. We curate 1,010 distinct style features spanning 26 stylistic categories and construct a dataset by prompting an LLM to generate text conditioned on these features. Using this data, we train a decoder to generate a style prompt from the style representation of the generated text. We evaluate our approach on three tasks: (1) recovering original style prompts from generated text, (2) generating text in the same style using the recovered prompts, and (3) steering LLM outputs to match the style of human-written texts. Experiments demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms strong baselines that directly prompt LLMs with target text, achieving superior performance in both style description and style imitation. These results highlight that style-eliciting prompts can provide a practical and interpretable interface to stylistic information encoded in style representations.

</details>


### [143] [Narrative Knowledge Weaver: Narrative-Centric Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning for Long-Form Text Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05724)
*Qiuyu Tian,Fengyi Chen,Yiding Li,Youyong Kong,Fan Guo,Yuyao Li,Jinjing Shen,Zhijing Xie,Yiyun Luo,Xin Zhang,Yingce Xia,Zequn Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Narrative Knowledge Weaver (NKW) is a source-grounded framework that integrates textual evidence, atomic facts, graph structures, entity profiles, interactions, episodes, and storylines for long-form narrative QA. By using text, graph, and narrative tools with post-retrieval reading skills, NKW outperforms on screenplay-level story-world QA while remaining competitive on passage-centered benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form narrative QA requires reasoning over evolving story worlds (goals, character states, social relations, causal triggers, temporal position, consequences), but existing retrieval and graph-augmented methods use units (chunks, entities, relations, summaries, tool actions) that do not directly encode how evidence functions in a story.

Method: NKW aligns multiple knowledge representations (textual evidence, atomic facts, canonical graph structure, entity profiles, interactions, episodes, storylines) and uses text, graph, and narrative tools with post-retrieval reading skills to assemble evidence and audit actor, scope, polarity, state, and temporal constraints.

Result: NKW achieves strongest performance on screenplay-level story-world QA (STAGE) while remaining competitive on passage-centered benchmarks (FairytaleQA, QuALITY). Ablations, question-type analyses, graph-asset statistics, and case studies show complementary benefits for character, scene, temporal, causal, and narrative-progression reasoning.

Conclusion: Integrating diverse narrative knowledge representations with specialized reading skills provides complementary advantages for complex story-world reasoning tasks beyond what passage-centric methods achieve.

Abstract: Long-form narrative QA requires reasoning over evolving story worlds rather than isolated passages: answers may depend on earlier goals, changing character states, social relations, causal triggers, temporal position, and later consequences. Existing retrieval and graph-augmented generation methods improve evidence access, but their units--chunks, entities, relations, summaries, or tool actions--do not directly encode how evidence functions in a story. We introduce Narrative Knowledge Weaver(NKW), a source-grounded framework that aligns textual evidence, atomic facts, canonical graph structure, entity profiles, interactions, episodes, and storylines. At query time, NKW uses text, graph, and narrative tools with post-retrieval reading skills to assemble evidence and audit actor, scope, polarity, state, and temporal constraints. Across STAGE, FairytaleQA, and QuALITY, NKW is strongest on screenplay-level story-world QA while remaining competitive on more passage-centered benchmarks. Ablations, question-type analyses, graph-asset statistics, and case studies show complementary benefits for character, scene, temporal, causal, and narrative-progression reasoning.

</details>


### [144] [AdaPLD: Adaptive Retrieval and Reuse for Efficient Model-Free Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05742)
*Runheng Liu,Jincheng Xie,Wen Hu,Xingchen Xiao,Heyan Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AdaPLD is a training-free speculative decoding method that improves draft retrieval and construction via semantic similarity (when lexical matching fails) and branched reuse hypotheses, achieving up to 3.10× speedup without auxiliary draft models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reuse-based speculative decoding methods suffer from (1) limited recall due to lexically anchored retrieval under surface-form variation, and (2) brittle deterministic span copying when retrieved context is ambiguous.

Method: AdaPLD preserves high-precision lexical reuse while adding semantic similarity to recover missed reuse opportunities, and constructs branched reuse hypotheses to handle continuation uncertainty instead of a single copied span.

Result: Across diverse benchmarks, AdaPLD reduces target-model forward passes and achieves up to 3.10× decoding speedup.

Conclusion: Training-free adaptive improvement of both retrieval (via semantic similarity) and draft construction (via branched hypotheses) effectively overcomes limitations of prior reuse-based speculative decoding.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates generation by verifying multiple drafted tokens in a single target-model forward pass, reducing sequential decoding iterations. Model-free variants avoid auxiliary draft models by reusing text and model states already available during generation, but their speedup depends on the reliability of the constructed drafts. We identify two limitations of existing reuse-based methods: lexically anchored retrieval has limited recall under surface-form variation, and deterministic span copying can be brittle when the retrieved context does not uniquely determine the continuation. We propose \emph{AdaPLD}, a training-free method that adaptively improves both retrieval and draft construction. AdaPLD preserves high-precision lexical reuse while using semantic similarity to recover additional reuse opportunities when lexical matching fails. It further constructs branched reuse hypotheses to account for continuation uncertainty, rather than relying on a single copied span. Across diverse benchmarks, AdaPLD reduces target-model forward passes and achieves up to $3.10\times$ decoding speedup.

</details>


### [145] [PlanBench-V: A Spatial Planning Map Benchmark for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05744)
*Minxin Chen,He Zhu,Junyou Su,Wen Wang,Yijie Deng,Wenjia Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PlanBench-V is the first benchmark for evaluating Vision-Language Models (VLMs) on spatial planning map interpretation. It includes an expert-annotated dataset of 223 maps and 1629 QA pairs, and tests four capabilities: Perception, Reasoning, Association, and Implementation. The best 2026 model (Qwen3.6-Plus) outperforms the best 2025 model (GPT-4o) by 27%, but all models struggle with implementation tasks requiring evaluative judgment and policy sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal benchmarks focus on general visual understanding and overlook domain-specific cognitive processes in planning practice. Spatial planning map interpretation requires fine-grained visual perception, spatial reasoning, and policy-informed judgment, which current VLMs are not adequately evaluated on.

Method: Built the Spatial Planning Map Database (SPMD) with 223 expert-annotated maps and 1629 QA pairs. Proposed a theory-informed evaluation framework assessing four progressive capabilities: Perception, Reasoning, Association, and Implementation, aligned with the cognitive pipeline of planning map interpretation.

Result: Qwen3.6-Plus (2026) outperforms GPT-4o (2025) by 27%. However, all models show significant limitations on implementation-oriented tasks involving evaluative judgment, policy sensitivity, and constraint-aware decision-making.

Conclusion: Current VLMs have fundamental limitations in professional planning contexts, highlighting the need for domain-adaptive multimodal reasoning frameworks.

Abstract: Spatial planning maps are central to territorial governance, translating planning objectives, regulations, and spatial strategies into visual forms for decision-making, public communication, and institutional coordination. Their interpretation, however, requires fine-grained visual perception, spatial reasoning, and policy-informed professional judgment, creating major challenges for both human learners and AI systems. With the rapid progress of Vision-Language Models (VLMs), their use in urban planning analysis is gaining attention, yet existing multimodal benchmarks mainly target general visual understanding and overlook the domain-specific cognitive processes of planning practice. To address this gap, we introduce PlanBench-V, the first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating VLMs in spatial planning map interpretation. We first build the Spatial Planning Map Database (SPMD), an expert-annotated dataset of 223 planning maps and 1629 question-answer pairs curated by professional planners, covering diverse geographic regions and cartographic styles. We then propose a theory-informed evaluation framework assessing four progressive capabilities: Perception, Reasoning, Association, and Implementation, corresponding to the cognitive pipeline of planning map interpretation. Extensive experiments across two generations of VLMs show clear progress but persistent limitations. The best 2026 agentic reasoning model, Qwen3.6-Plus, substantially outperforms the best 2025 model, GPT-4o, by 27%. Nevertheless, all models still struggle with implementation-oriented tasks requiring evaluative judgment, policy sensitivity, and constraint-aware decision-making. These findings reveal fundamental limitations of current VLMs in professional planning contexts and highlight the need for domain-adaptive multimodal reasoning frameworks. Code and data are available at https://plangpt.github.io.

</details>


### [146] [MARDoc: A Memory-Aware Refinement Agent Framework for Multimodal Long Document QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05749)
*Kaifeng Chen,Hongtao Liu,Qiyao Peng,Jian Yang,Yongqiang Liu,Xiaochen Zhang,Qing Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MARDoc is a multi-agent framework for multimodal long-document QA that decouples the task into Explorer, Refiner, and Reflector agents, using structured memory instead of raw interaction history to reduce noise and improve multi-hop reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing iterative retrieval-reasoning agents mix retrieval traces, observations, and reasoning into a single growing context, causing key evidence to become scattered and diluted as interactions accumulate, which makes multi-hop reasoning noisy and ineffective.

Method: MARDoc decouples long-document QA into three specialized agents: (1) Explorer for multi-granularity multimodal retrieval, (2) Refiner for distilling interaction traces into structured evidence and reasoning memories, and (3) Reflector for checking evidence sufficiency and providing targeted feedback. The system relies on dynamically updated structured memory rather than full accumulated interaction history across iterations.

Result: Experiments on MMLongBench-Doc and DocBench show that MARDoc achieves strong results, outperforming same-backbone baselines.

Conclusion: Structured memory is effective for agentic document QA, as it reduces context noise while preserving answer-critical facts and their logical dependencies.

Abstract: Iterative retrieval-reasoning agents have recently shown promise for multimodal long-document question answering. However, most existing systems maintain a single growing context that mixes retrieval traces, observations, and intermediate reasoning. As interactions accumulate, key evidence becomes scattered and diluted, making multi-hop reasoning noisy. We propose MARDoc, a Memory-Aware Refinement Agent framework that decouples long-document QA into three specialized agents: an Explorer for multi-granularity multimodal retrieval, a Refiner for distilling interaction traces into structured evidence and reasoning memories, and a Reflector for checking evidence sufficiency and providing targeted feedback. Across iterations, the agents rely on a dynamically updated structured memory rather than a full accumulated interaction history. This design reduces context noise while preserving answer-critical facts and their logical dependencies. Experiments on MMLongBench-Doc and DocBench show that MARDoc achieves strong results, outperforming same-backbone baselines and demonstrating the effectiveness of structured memory for agentic document QA.

</details>


### [147] [CollabBench: Benchmarking and Unleashing Collaborative Ability of LLMs with Diverse Players via Proactive Engagement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05793)
*Hong Qian,Yuanhao Liu,Zihan Zhou,Zongbao Zhang,Hanjie Ge,Haotian Shi,Liang Dou,Xiangfeng Wang,Jingwen Yang,Aimin Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CollabBench is a benchmark for evaluating and training collaborative LLM agents in cooperative games, featuring diverse player simulation and a collaborative training paradigm that improves efficiency by 19.5% and affective performance by 24.4%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based agents excel individually but struggle with realistic human collaboration. Current conversation-level studies lack grounded interaction and behavioral execution, motivating the need for cooperative game environments that enable contextualized and immersive collaboration.

Method: CollabBench features a Diverse Player Profile Simulation pipeline to model varied player behaviors, and a Collaborative Agentic Training paradigm unifying reasoning, communication, and action via agentic rollouts, optimized with a hybrid reward balancing task efficiency and affective adaptation. Classic environments were extended to CWAH-MultiPlayer and Cook-MultiPlayer for evaluation under diverse personalities.

Result: Trained models outperformed base models with 19.5% higher efficiency and 24.4% improved affective performance.

Conclusion: Analysis reveals key collaborative limitations of existing models and offers insights for future collaborative training.

Abstract: While LLM-based agents excel at individual tasks, effective collaboration with realistic human partners remains challenging. Most of the existing conversation-level collaborative studies lack grounded interaction and behavioral execution, motivating the need for cooperative game environments that enable contextualized and immersive collaboration. To this end, this paper proposes CollabBench, a benchmark for evaluating and training collaborative agents in cooperative games. CollabBench features a Diverse Player Profile Simulation pipeline to model varied players behaviors, and a Collaborative Agentic Training paradigm that unifies reasoning, communication, and action via agentic rollouts, optimized with a hybrid reward balancing task efficiency and affective adaptation. We further extend classic environments to CWAH-MultiPlayer and Cook-MultiPlayer for systematic evaluation under diverse personalities. Experiments with efficiency and affective metrics show that our trained models outperform base models, achieving 19.5% higher efficiency and 24.4% improved affective performance. Further analysis reveals key collaborative limitations of existing models and offers insights for future collaborative training.

</details>


### [148] [Can LLMs Be Constrained to the Past? Improving Knowledge Cutoff through Recall-Based Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05804)
*Michiro Asai,Ailiang Lin,Yu Kishimoto,Takao Obi,Satoshi Kosugi,Kotaro Funakoshi,Manabu Okumura*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes recall-based prompting (Self-Recall and Question-Recall) to improve LLM adherence to knowledge cutoff constraints, outperforming direct-answer and reasoning baselines, especially on counterfactual questions. Introduces MHEB benchmark for multi-cutoff evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on knowledge cutoff prompting relies on direct-answer generation, which fails when post-cutoff knowledge is causally related but not explicitly queried by the question.

Method: Proposes Self-Recall (SR) — asking the model to restate its cutoff constraint — and Question-Recall (QR) — requiring the model to recall question-relevant valid information under the cutoff. Also constructs the Multi-cutoff Historical Event Benchmark (MHEB) for robustness evaluation.

Result: SR and QR outperform direct-answer and step-by-step reasoning baselines across three benchmarks, with strongest gains on counterfactual questions. On MHEB, cutoff performance varies with cutoff distance, and combining SR+QR yields best results.

Conclusion: Recall-based prompting strategies effectively improve LLM constraint adherence under knowledge cutoffs, and multi-cutoff evaluation reveals sensitivity to temporal distance.

Abstract: Prompted knowledge cutoff instructs a large language model (LLM) to act as if information beyond a specified cutoff date were unavailable. However, prior work mainly relies on direct-answer generation, which struggles when post-cutoff knowledge is not explicitly queried but is only causally related to the question. To address this limitation, we propose two recall-based prompting strategies: Self-Recall (SR), which asks the model to restate its cutoff constraint, and Question-Recall (QR), which requires the model to recall question-relevant information valid under the cutoff. Across three existing benchmarks, our methods outperform both direct-answer prompting and conventional step-by-step reasoning baselines, with particularly strong improvements on counterfactual questions. To investigate robustness across different cutoff settings, we further construct the Multi-cutoff Historical Event Benchmark (MHEB), which evaluates the same question under multiple cutoff years. Results show that knowledge cutoff performance varies with cutoff distance, while combining SR and QR consistently yields the best performance.

</details>


### [149] [ProSPy: A Profiling-Driven SQL-Python Agentic Framework for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05836)
*Zhaorui Yang,Huawei Zheng,Sen Yang,Yuhui Zhang,Haoxuan Li,Zhizhen Yu,Xuan Yi,Chen Hou,Defeng Xie,Chao Hu,Minfeng Zhu,Dazhen Deng,Haozhe Feng,Danqing Huang,Yingcai Wu,Peng Chen,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ProSPy is a profiling-driven SQL-Python agentic framework for enterprise-scale Text-to-SQL that structures reasoning into four stages: automatic profiling, schema pruning, dialect-agnostic SQL views, and Python-based analysis, achieving 60.15% and 60.51% execution accuracy on Spider 2.0-Lite and Spider 2.0-Snow with Claude-4.5-Opus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise-scale Text-to-SQL faces challenges including large heterogeneous schemas, incomplete metadata, dialect-specific SQL syntax, and complex analytical questions unsolvable with single SQL queries, which existing LLM-based approaches struggle to handle.

Method: ProSPy is a four-stage agentic framework: (1) automatic profiling extracts fine-grained data evidence, (2) progressive schema pruning reduces large schemas to task-relevant contexts, (3) a dialect-agnostic SQL interface fetches intermediate views, (4) Python performs flexible downstream analysis. This combines SQL's efficiency for large databases with Python's analytical flexibility.

Result: ProSPy achieves 60.15% and 60.51% execution accuracy on Spider 2.0-Lite and Spider 2.0-Snow with Claude-4.5-Opus, consistently outperforming strong baselines with both open-source and proprietary models without majority voting.

Conclusion: ProSPy is robust to SQL dialect variations, reduces reliance on unreliable metadata, and achieves a favorable trade-off between schema recall and precision, making it effective for enterprise-scale Text-to-SQL scenarios.

Abstract: Large language models have substantially advanced Text-to-SQL systems, yet applying them to enterprise-scale databases remains challenging. Real-world databases often contain large and heterogeneous schemas, incomplete metadata, dialect-specific SQL syntax, and complex analytical questions that are difficult to solve with a single SQL query. To address these challenges, we propose ProSPy, a Profiling-driven SQL--Python agentic framework for enterprise-scale Text-to-SQL. ProSPy structures the reasoning process into four stages: it first extracts fine-grained data evidence through automatic profiling, progressively prunes large schemas into task-relevant contexts, fetches intermediate views through a dialect-agnostic SQL interface, and finally performs flexible downstream analysis with Python. This design combines the efficiency of SQL over large databases with the flexibility of Python-based analysis, while reducing reliance on unreliable metadata and improving robustness across SQL dialects. Experiments on Spider 2.0-Lite and Spider 2.0-Snow show that ProSPy consistently outperforms strong baselines with both open-source and proprietary models, achieving execution accuracies of 60.15% and 60.51% with Claude-4.5-Opus, without majority voting. Further analysis shows that ProSPy is robust to SQL dialect variations and achieves a favorable trade-off between schema recall and precision.

</details>


### [150] [Mechanistic Insights into Functional Sparsity in Multimodal LLMs via CoRe Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05843)
*Ruoxi Sun,Quantong Qiu,Juntao Li,Zecheng Tang,Yihang Lou,Min Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper reveals functional sparsity in MLLMs, identifying specialized CoRe heads (top 5%) that are critical for cross-modal retrieval, while most other heads attend broadly. Ablating CoRe heads severely degrades performance; leveraging this sparsity accelerates inference without sacrificing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite MLLMs' strong performance on vision-language tasks, how they extract query-relevant visual features from noisy contexts is poorly understood. The authors aim to uncover the internal retrieval mechanism for better interpretability.

Method: Introduces a token-level metric called Retrieval Attention Mass (RAM) to identify specialized Context-aware Retrieval (CoRe) heads. Uses causal interventions (ablation) and acceleration experiments to validate the functional importance of these heads.

Result: CoRe heads act as dedicated information extractors with clear functional division from other heads. Ablating top 5% of CoRe heads significantly degrades multimodal reasoning; leveraging this sparsity accelerates inference while maintaining performance.

Conclusion: MLLMs exhibit a structural principle of functional sparsity in cross-modal retrieval, refining understanding of mechanistic interpretability and providing foundations for future architecture design and model optimization.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate remarkable proficiency on complex vision-language tasks, the mechanisms by which they extract query-relevant visual features from complex, noisy contexts remain opaque. In this paper, we present an in-depth interpretability study that uncovers a profound structural property within MLLMs: functional sparsity in cross-modal retrieval. Leveraging a token-level metric termed Retrieval Attention Mass (RAM), we identify and characterize a highly specialized subset of attention heads, referred to as Context-aware Retrieval (CoRe) heads. Across diverse visual domains and model scales, we observe a clear functional division: CoRe heads act as dedicated information extractors, while most other heads distribute attention over broader contextual regions. Causal interventions further demonstrate the necessity of these specialized heads. Ablating only the top 5% of CoRe heads causes significant degradation in multimodal reasoning performance, whereas ablating lower-ranked heads has minimal effect. Moreover, acceleration experiments validate the utility of CoRe heads, showing that leveraging this localized sparsity significantly accelerates inference while maintaining robust task performance. Our findings reveal a structural principle of functional sparsity within MLLMs, refining the current understanding of mechanistic interpretability and laying a theoretical foundation that can inspire future architecture design and model optimization.

</details>


### [151] [Towards Truly Multilingual ASR: Generalizing Code-Switching ASR to Unseen Language Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05846)
*Gio Paik,Hyunseo Shin,Soungmin Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether code-switching ASR capabilities learned from limited language pairs can generalize to unseen language pairs via model merging and domain generalization, finding only modest transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code-switching ASR is challenging due to scarcity of multilingual CS speech resources across diverse language pairs, and existing approaches (synthetic data generation, pair-specific fine-tuning) face scalability issues as language pairs grow combinatorially with supported languages.

Method: The authors investigate whether CS capabilities from limited seen language pairs can generalize to unseen pairs through model merging and domain generalization methods.

Result: Merged bilingual CS-ASR models show only modest generalization to unseen language pairs, indicating limited transfer of bilingual CS capabilities across language pairs.

Conclusion: The transfer of bilingual CS-ASR capabilities across language pairs is limited, suggesting that current model merging and domain generalization approaches are insufficient for scalable zero-shot generalization to unseen language pairs.

Abstract: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) has become a key technology for human--AI interaction. However, code-switching ASR (CS-ASR) remains particularly challenging due to the severe scarcity of multilingual CS speech resources across diverse language pairs. Existing approaches primarily improve CS-ASR performance through synthetic CS speech generation or pair-specific fine-tuning on limited bilingual datasets. Nevertheless, these approaches face an inherent scalability limitation, as support for CS must be developed separately for language pairs whose number grows combinatorially with the number of supported languages. In this work, we investigate whether CS capabilities learned from a limited set of seen language pairs can generalize to unseen language pairs through model merging and domain generalization methods. Our experiments show that merged bilingual CS-ASR models modestly generalize to unseen language pairs, suggesting limited transfer of bilingual CS capabilities across language pairs.

</details>


### [152] [Forgive or forget: Understanding the context of hate in audio retrieval systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05857)
*Arghya Pal,Sailaja Rajanala,Raphael C. -W. Phan,Shekhar Nayak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a post hoc causal debiasing framework with sentiment-controlled mediator to reduce toxicity in text-to-audio retrieval while preserving semantic relevance, introducing Forgive (re-ranking/filtering) and Forget (counterfactual prompt generation) variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing strategies like rephrasing and summarization for handling toxic retrieval in text-to-audio systems risk altering intent or omitting details; a method that preserves semantic relevance while suppressing harmful speech is needed.

Method: Post hoc causal debiasing framework with a sentiment-controlled mediator; model-agnostic integration with existing retrieval pipelines. Two variants: Forgive (re-ranks and filters toxic audio via logit adjustment) and Forget (generates counterfactual toxic prompts to mitigate harmful retrievals).

Result: Consistent toxicity reduction with minimal loss in retrieval accuracy, improving both safety and reliability.

Conclusion: The proposed causal debiasing approach effectively balances toxicity suppression and semantic relevance preservation in text-to-audio retrieval, offering a model-agnostic, plug-and-play solution.

Abstract: Handling toxic retrieval in text-to-audio systems is challenging due to contextual dependencies. Existing strategies (e.g., rephrasing, summarization) risk altering intent or omitting details. We propose a post hoc causal debiasing framework with a sentiment-controlled mediator to preserve semantic relevance while suppressing harmful speech. Our approach is model-agnostic and integrates seamlessly with existing retrieval pipelines. We introduce two variants: Forgive, which re-ranks and filters toxic audio via logit adjustment, and Forget, which generates counterfactual toxic prompts to mitigate harmful retrievals. Experiments show consistent toxicity reduction with minimal loss in retrieval accuracy, improving both safety and reliability.

</details>


### [153] [ReverseEOL: Improving Training-free Text Embeddings via Text Reversal in Decoder-only LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05858)
*Ailiang Lin,Zhuoyun Li,Yusong Wang,Keyu Mao,Kotaro Funakoshi,Manabu Okumura*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ReverseEOL enhances frozen LLMs for text embeddings by combining forward embeddings with reversed-input embeddings, providing complementary contextual information without training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal attention in decoder-only LLMs prevents earlier tokens from attending to future context, producing biased contextualized representations for text embedding tasks.

Method: ReverseEOL augments standard forward embedding with an additional reversed embedding from reversed input text, then combines both embeddings to create a richer final representation.

Result: Significant improvements on STS and MTEB benchmarks across diverse LLM architectures and scales compared to existing training-free baselines.

Conclusion: The reversal mechanism is necessary and effective for enhancing training-free text embeddings from frozen LLMs without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have opened new avenues for generating training-free text embeddings. However, the causal attention in decoder-only LLMs prevents earlier tokens from attending to future context, leading to biased contextualized representations. In this work, we propose Reverse prompting with Explicit One-word Limitation (ReverseEOL), a simple yet effective method for enhancing the representational capability of frozen LLMs. ReverseEOL augments the standard forward embedding with an additional reversed embedding derived from the reversed input text. Since reversing the input exposes each token to context inaccessible in the original order, the resulting reversed embedding effectively provides complementary information to the original one. As a result, combining the forward and reversed embeddings yields a richer final representation. Comprehensive experiments on STS and MTEB benchmarks demonstrate that ReverseEOL significantly improves the performance of existing training-free baselines across a broad range of LLMs with diverse architectures and scales. Extensive ablations and analyses further confirm the necessity of our reversal mechanism.

</details>


### [154] [TARPO: Token-Wise Latent-Explicit Reasoning via Action-Routing Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05859)
*Liting Zhang,Shiwan Zhao,Xuyang Zhao,Zichen Xu,Jianye Wang,Qicheng Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TARPO is a pure RL framework that adaptively switches between discrete token generation and continuous latent reasoning at each step using a lightweight action head router, outperforming existing explicit and latent reasoning RL baselines across diverse benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent reasoning is promising for LLMs but its deterministic continuous representations limit policy exploration in RL. The paper aims to address this by introducing stochasticity back into the reasoning process.

Method: TARPO introduces a lightweight action head router that observes the current hidden state and samples a routing decision from a binary mode-selection space, preserving stochasticity of discrete token sampling. The LLM backbone and router are jointly optimized end-to-end with a shared group-relative advantage signal.

Result: Experiments across Qwen2.5 (1.5B to 7B) and Llama-3.1-8B backbones show TARPO consistently outperforms existing explicit and latent reasoning RL baselines across diverse benchmarks. TARPO learns adaptive token-wise switching behaviors with stable training dynamics.

Conclusion: TARPO effectively combines the benefits of both discrete CoT and continuous latent reasoning by adaptively switching between them at each token step, achieving superior RL-based reasoning performance.

Abstract: Latent reasoning has emerged as a promising alternative to discrete Chain-of-Thought (CoT) in large language models (LLMs), enabling more expressive reasoning by operating over continuous representations. However, the inherently deterministic nature of continuous representations limits policy exploration in reinforcement learning (RL). To address this, we propose TARPO (Token-Wise Latent-Explicit Reasoning via Action-Routing Policy Optimization), a pure RL framework that adaptively switches between discrete token generation and continuous latent reasoning at each step. TARPO introduces a lightweight action head router that observes the current hidden state and samples a routing decision from a binary mode-selection space, preserving the stochasticity of discrete token sampling from the vocabulary. The LLM backbone and router are jointly optimized end-to-end with a shared group-relative advantage signal. Extensive experiments across Qwen2.5 (from 1.5B to 7B) and Llama-3.1-8B backbones demonstrate that TARPO consistently outperforms existing explicit and latent reasoning RL baselines across diverse benchmarks. Further analysis shows that TARPO learns adaptive token-wise switching behaviors while maintaining stable training dynamics. Our code is available at https://github.com/NKU-LITI/TARPO-master.

</details>


### [155] [Analysis of the Neglect-Zero Effect in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05864)
*Jin Tanaka,Daiki Matsuoka,Ryoma Kumon,Hitomi Yanaka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs exhibit the neglect-zero effect (a human cognitive bias of ignoring empty-set configurations), using a structural priming paradigm. Results suggest LLMs do not display this bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine whether LLMs' language processing resembles human cognitive processing, specifically testing for the neglect-zero effect, a known human bias where people ignore vacuous truth conditions involving empty sets.

Method: The study uses a structural priming paradigm: primes are designed to force LLMs to consider zero-models, and the analysis examines whether LLMs also consider zero-models in target sentences. Two types of inferences driven by the neglect-zero effect are compared against an inference without the effect.
