### [182] [Ouvia: A User-centered Framework for Measuring Usability of Speech Translation in Real-World Communication Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06177)
*Giuseppe Attanasio,Beatrice Savoldi,Daniel Chechelnitsky,Matteo Negri,Marine Carpuat,Maarten Sap,André F. T. Martins*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Ouvia is a user-centered evaluation framework for speech translation that measures real-world usability through one-to-one communication scenarios (English→Portuguese). Testing over 1,750 interactions across healthcare and everyday settings with four ST systems and diverse demographic groups, it finds only ~50% of interactions are rated usable, with significant demographic gaps, and QA-based evaluation is a much stronger predictor of usability than standard metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ST evaluation focuses on decontextualized testbeds and holistic quality rather than end users' actual communication needs, leading to a gap between benchmark scores and real-world usability.

Method: Custom web app with multi-phase study design: 1,750+ one-to-one English→Portuguese interactions across healthcare and everyday scenarios, mediated by four ST systems, with speakers from three English dialects and two genders. User-perceived usability was collected and compared against standard quality metrics.

Result: Only ~50% of interactions rated as usable; significant usability gaps across demographic groups; QA-based evaluation is a substantially stronger predictor of real-world usability than standard holistic quality metrics.

Conclusion: Situated, user-centered evaluation frameworks that go beyond holistic quality scores are essential; the question of who the technology serves and how well must be central to ST evaluation.

Abstract: Speech translation (ST) is increasingly adopted in user applications, yet its evaluation largely focuses on decontextualized testbeds and holistic quality, rather than end users' communication needs. We introduce Ouvia, an evaluation framework for measuring user-perceived usability of speech translation outputs in real-world settings. Ouvia focuses on one-to-one communication: an English speaker needs to convey a request to a Portuguese speaker, and the message is automatically translated. Through a custom web app and multi-phase study design, we collect more than 1,750 such interactions in healthcare and everyday situations, mediated by four ST systems, involving speakers from three English dialects and two genders. We find that modern ST serves people only to a limited extent -- only around half of interactions are rated as usable -- with significant gaps in reported usability across demographic groups. Moreover, among quality metrics, we find that QA-based evaluation is a substantially stronger predictor of real-world usability than standard approaches. Together, these findings stress the importance of situated, user-centered evaluation frameworks that go beyond holistic quality scores and attend to who the technology serves -- and how well.

</details>


### [183] [The Tell-Tale Norm: $\ell_2$ Magnitude as a Signal for Reasoning Dynamics in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06188)
*Jinyang Zhang,Hongxin Ding,Yue Fang,Weibin Liao,Muyang Ye,Junfeng Zhao,Yasha Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper shows that the l2 norm of hidden states in LLMs is an endogenous signal of reasoning intensity. Using SAEs as probes, the authors find reasoning feature activations spike in late layers, and theoretically prove the l2 norm bounds these activations. They propose three test-time scaling techniques (adaptive layer-wise recursion, state steering, and response selection) guided by l2 norms that improve reasoning without additional training, validated across architectures and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no principled, model-intrinsic signal that captures layer-wise reasoning dynamics in LLMs. The authors aim to bridge this gap by identifying the l2 norm of hidden states as a faithful indicator of reasoning intensity.

Method: (1) Use SAEs as diagnostic probes to observe reasoning feature activations; (2) Theoretically prove that the l2 norm of hidden states bounds the activation strength of SAE reasoning features; (3) Conduct empirical correlation analysis and causal interventions to validate l2 norm as an indicator; (4) Propose three l2-norm-guided test-time scaling techniques: Adaptive Layer-wise Reasoning Recursion, Endogenous Reasoning State Steering, and l2-guided Response Selection.

Result: l2-norm-based techniques significantly improve reasoning performance across various model architectures and benchmarks, requiring no additional training or data, and are compatible with advanced inference engines.

Conclusion: The l2 norm of hidden states is a principled yet simple signal to perceive and control LLM latent reasoning dynamics, offering test-time scaling methods that enhance reasoning performance without extra training.

Abstract: Recent work has sought to understand Large Language Models (LLMs) reasoning, yet a principled, model-intrinsic signal that captures its layer-wise reasoning dynamics remains underexplored. We bridge this gap by demonstrating that the l2 norm of hidden states serves as an endogenous signal of the model's reasoning intensity. Using Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) as a diagnostic probe, we observe that LLMs' internal reasoning is marked by a sharp increase in reasoning feature activations concentrated in late layers. Motivated by this pattern, we establish a formal link between reasoning intensity and the model's latent geometry and theoretically prove that the l2 norm of hidden states bounds the activation strength of SAE reasoning features. Empirical correlation analysis and causal interventions further validate the l2 norm as a faithful indicator, where heightened norms consistently correspond to critical reasoning steps. We then introduce three test-time scaling techniques guided by l2 norms: (i) Adaptive Layer-wise Reasoning Recursion, (ii) Endogenous Reasoning State Steering, and (iii) l2-guided Response Selection, which requires no additional training or data and is compatible with advanced inference engines. Experiments across model architectures and benchmarks show that l2-norm-based techniques significantly improve reasoning performance, offering a principled yet simple lens to perceive and control LLM latent reasoning dynamics. Our code is available at https://github.com/zjy1298/The-Tell-Tale-Norm.

</details>


### [184] [Improving Answer Extraction in Context-based Question Answering Systems Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06197)
*Hafez Abdelghaffar,Ahmed Alansary,Ali Hamdi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Fine-tuning RoBERTa-base on SQuAD1.1 achieves strong QA performance with ROUGE-L 86.84%, BLEU 28.24%, and BERTScore 95.38%, addressing issues of contextual understanding and answer precision in LLM-based QA systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current QA systems struggle with contextual understanding, answer consistency, and generalization, often producing irrelevant or imprecise responses despite having correct context.

Method: Fine-tune a pre-trained LLM (RoBERTa-base) on the SQuAD1.1 benchmark dataset using context-question-answer triplets for supervised training.

Result: Fine-tuned RoBERTa-base achieved ROUGE-L 86.84%, BLEU 28.24%, and BERTScore 95.38% on SQuAD1.1.

Conclusion: Targeted fine-tuning substantially improves the reliability and precision of QA systems, confirming the effectiveness for context-based QA tasks.

Abstract: Question answering (QA) systems have achieved notable progress with the advent of large language models (LLMs). However, they still face challenges in accurately extracting and generating precise answers from given contexts, particularly when dealing with complex or ambiguous queries. Existing approaches often struggle with contextual understanding, answer consistency, and generalization across diverse domains. In this work, we propose a question answering system based on large language models, where the input consists of a textual context and a corresponding question, and the output is a concise and accurate answer. The motivation behind this research lies in addressing the limitations of current QA systems, particularly their tendency to produce irrelevant or imprecise responses despite having access to the correct context. Our methodology involves fine-tuning a pre-trained LLM on a benchmark QA dataset to improve its contextual comprehension and answer extraction capabilities. Specifically, we utilize the Stanford Question Answering Dataset (SQuAD1.1), which provides high-quality context-question-answer triplets for supervised training and evaluation. Experimental results show that the fine-tuned Roberta-base model achieves the highest performance, attaining a ROUGE-L score of 86.84%, a BLEU score of 28.24%, and a BERTScore of 95.38%. These results indicate strong accuracy and answer relevance, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach for context-based question answering tasks. Furthermore, the findings confirm that targeted fine-tuning substantially improves the reliability and precision of QA systems.

</details>


### [185] [Dense Contexts Are Hard Contexts: Lexical Density Limits Effective Context in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06203)
*Giovanni Dettori,Matteo Boffa,Danilo Giordano,Idilio Drago,Marco Mellia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Lexical density (rate of distinct information per token) is identified as a third factor, beyond input length and position, that systematically degrades LLM long-context performance. In "find-the-needle" benchmarks with identical length (~12k tokens) and controlled position, higher lexical density sharply reduces retrieval accuracy (e.g., near-perfect models drop below 60%). Reducing density restores performance, showing effective context capacity depends on lexical density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research focuses on input length and position of relevant information as causes of degraded LLM long-context performance, but lexical density—how densely distinct information is packed—has been largely overlooked. The authors aim to demonstrate that this factor systematically reduces the effective context window.

Method: Three "find-the-needle" style benchmarks with identical length (~12k tokens) and controlled needle position, but increasing lexical density, are used to test open-weight LLMs (9B-685B). Within-benchmark density is varied while keeping other properties unchanged to rule out task-type confounds.

Result: Higher lexical density causes a sharp performance collapse: models near-perfect in sparse contexts drop below 60% retrieval score in denser benchmarks. Reducing density generally restores performance, especially in high-density regimes where degradation is most severe.

Conclusion: Effective context capacity is a function of lexical density, not just length or position. This has direct implications for real-world LLM systems operating on compact, information-rich inputs.

Abstract: Input length and the position of relevant information are widely cited as the primary causes of degraded LLM long-context performance. Here, we study lexical density -- the rate at which a context introduces distinct information -- as a third, largely overlooked factor that systematically reduces the effective context window of LLMs. We quantify the impact of lexical density on open-weight LLMs (9B-685B) using three "find-the-needle" style benchmarks with identical length (~12k tokens) and controlled needle position, but increasing density of information. We observe a sharp performance collapse in higher-density benchmarks: models that are near-perfect in sparse contexts drop below 60% retrieval score on denser ones. To rule out task-type confounds, we vary and control the density within each benchmark while keeping all other properties unchanged. Reducing density generally restores performance, especially in the high-density regimes where degradation appears. These results show that effective context capacity is a function of lexical density, with direct implications for real-world LLM systems operating on compact, information-rich inputs.

</details>


### [186] [FiLM-Based Speaker Conditioning of a SpeechLLM for Pathological Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06211)
*Fernando López,Santosh Kesiraju,Jordi Luque*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) with x-vectors for speaker-conditioned ASR adaptation to pathological speech, showing competitive performance against fine-tuning baselines while preserving question-answering capabilities on Spanish and English datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pathological speech from neurological conditions remains a significant challenge for ASR systems, which have advanced remarkably for standard speech.

Method: Injecting x-vector-derived information via Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) into each transformer layer of a frozen ASR encoder to adapt internal representations to individual pathological speakers without modifying base model weights.

Result: Speaker-conditioned ASR is competitive with established adaptation strategies while retaining performance on non-conditioned speech.

Conclusion: The adapted model preserves the ability to answer speech-related questions, demonstrating that speaker conditioning via FiLM is effective for pathological speech ASR without compromising general capabilities.

Abstract: Automatic speech recognition (ASR) has advanced remarkably for standard speech; however, pathological speech from neurological conditions remains a significant challenge. We investigate speaker conditioning via Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), injecting x-vector-derived information into each transformer layer of a frozen ASR encoder to adapt internal representations to individual pathological speakers without modifying base model weights. We benchmark this for the ASR task against standard and parameter-efficient fine-tuning baselines, complemented by post-processing, on Spanish and English pathological speech. Additionally, we evaluate if the adapted model preserves the ability to answer speech-related questions. Results show that speaker-conditioned ASR is competitive with established adaptation strategies while retaining performance on non-conditioned speech.

</details>


### [187] [Benchmarking Open-Source Layout Detection Models for Data Snapshot Extraction from Institutional Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06242)
*AJ Carl P. Dy,Aivin V. Solatorio*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for data snapshot extraction—identifying and localizing semantically meaningful figures and tables in institutional documents (humanitarian reports, World Bank papers, project appraisals). Experiments show that current layout detection models struggle to generalize to these documents, confusing analytical vs. non-analytical content, fragmenting composite artifacts, and missing contextual information, highlighting a gap between generic layout analysis and operationally useful extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current document layout analysis treats figures and tables as uniformly relevant objects rather than semantically meaningful analytical artifacts, failing to meet the operational needs of extracting reusable analytical information from institutional documents.

Method: The authors create a benchmark dataset with annotations for semantically meaningful figures and tables across three document types (humanitarian reports, World Bank policy research working papers, project appraisal documents), then evaluate multiple open-source layout detection models on both detection performance and spatial extraction quality.

Result: Current models struggle to generalize to operational institutional documents despite strong academic benchmark performance. Key failure modes include confusion between analytical and non-analytical content, fragmentation of composite analytical artifacts, and incomplete extraction of contextual information.

Conclusion: There is a persistent gap between generic document layout analysis and operationally useful data snapshot extraction. The authors release source PDFs, annotations, metadata, and code to support future research.

Abstract: Institutional documents contain substantial amounts of operational and analytical information embedded within figures and tables. Current approaches for extracting visual content from documents are largely built around generic document layout analysis, where figures and tables are treated as uniformly relevant document objects rather than semantically meaningful analytical artifacts. In this work, we introduce a benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for \textit{data snapshot extraction}, the task of identifying and localizing semantically meaningful visual artifacts within institutional documents. The benchmark spans humanitarian reports, World Bank policy research working papers, and project appraisal documents, and includes annotations for figures and tables that contain reusable analytical information. Using this dataset, we benchmarked multiple open-source layout detection models and evaluated both detection performance and spatial extraction quality. Our results show that current models struggle to generalize to operational institutional documents despite strong performance on conventional academic benchmarks. Common failure modes include confusion between analytical and non-analytical content, fragmentation of composite analytical artifacts, and incomplete extraction of contextual information required for interpretation. These findings highlight a persistent gap between generic document layout analysis and operationally useful data snapshot extraction. We release the source PDFs, annotation dataset, metadata, and source code to support future research in operational document intelligence. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ai4data/data-snapshot and the source code is available at https://github.com/worldbank/ai4data/tree/main/experimental/data-snapshot.

</details>


### [188] [From Self to Other: Evaluating Demographic Perspective-Taking in LLM Hate Speech Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06266)
*Paloma Piot,Javier Parapar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether persona-conditioned LLMs can simulate human-like disagreement in hate speech annotation across three dimensions: inter-group disagreement, in-group sensitivity, and vicarious prediction. Results show no model consistently captures all three, but Llama 3.1 with vicarious prompting best approximates human disagreement patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hate speech annotation is subjective across demographic groups, but collecting diverse annotations at scale is costly. Persona-conditioned LLMs are proposed as a scalable alternative, but it is unclear whether they actually reflect how different groups disagree.

Method: The authors evaluate three dimensions of human social judgment: (i) inter-group disagreement (whether personas from different groups disagree in human-like ways), (ii) in-group sensitivity (increased sensitivity when content targets one's own identity), and (iii) vicarious prediction (predicting how another group would react). They test multiple LLMs with identity prompts and vicarious prompting.

Result: No model consistently captures all three dimensions; performance is highly model-dependent and does not emerge reliably from minimal identity prompts alone. Llama 3.1 with vicarious prompting yields the highest cross-group agreement across most demographic axes and provides the closest approximation to human disagreement patterns.

Conclusion: Vicarious prompting with Llama 3.1 may provide a more reliable setting for automatic hate speech annotation aligned with human judgments, though current models still fail to consistently replicate all aspects of human disagreement.

Abstract: Hate speech detection is inherently subjective: people from different demographic groups perceive the same content very differently. Collecting enough annotations from multiple demographic groups is costly and difficult to scale. Persona-conditioned Large Language Models (models prompted to adopt a specific demographic identity) have been proposed as a way to simulate diverse perspectives at scale. But do they actually reflect how different groups disagree? We evaluate three aspects of human social judgement: (i) whether personas from different groups disagree in human-like ways (inter-group disagreement), (ii) whether they become more sensitive when content targets their own identity (in-group sensitivity), and (iii) whether they can accurately predict how another group would react (vicarious prediction). Our results show that no model consistently captures all three dimensions, and performance is highly model-dependent and does not emerge reliably from minimal identity prompts alone. However, vicarious prompting with Llama 3.1 yields the highest cross-group agreement in most demographic axes and provides the closest overall approximation to human disagreement patterns, indicating that this configuration may provide a more reliable setting for automatic annotation aligned with human judgements.

</details>


### [189] [Many Circuits, One Mechanism: Input Variation and Evaluation Granularity in Circuit Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06267)
*Alireza Bayat Makou,Jingcheng Niu,Subhabrata Dutta,Iryna Gurevych*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Circuit discovery methods produce structurally distinct subgraphs for the same task under different input statistics, but these differences are "phantom specialization"—they don't reflect genuine functional differences. Across 75 circuits from five Pythia models doing literal sequence copying, structurally different circuits implement the same computation: band-specific edges transfer broadly, a shared core recovers ≥99% performance, and internal representations are interchangeable. Standard evaluation (source-level) obscures this; edge-level evaluation reveals the many-to-one structure-to-function mapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the assumption that structural differences between discovered circuits imply distinct functional mechanisms, which is commonly used in mechanistic interpretability research.

Method: Vary input token-frequency statistics while holding the task (Literal Sequence Copying) fixed across four frequency bands plus a control, in five Pythia models (70M-1.4B). Extract 75 circuits using circuit discovery, then perform cross-condition transfer tests (band-specific edge transfer), core subgraph analysis, causal interchange interventions, and repeated extractions within the same band. Compare source-level vs. edge-level evaluation.

Result: Structurally distinct circuits implement the same computation: band-specific edges transfer broadly across bands, a core subgraph shared across most bands recovers ≥99% performance, and causal interchange confirms internal representations are interchangeable. Repeated extractions within the same band sample from an equivalence class of valid subgraphs. Source-level evaluation inflates apparent faithfulness; edge-level evaluation reveals many-to-one mapping from structure to function.

Conclusion: Structural differences between circuits are not sufficient evidence for distinct mechanisms. Exposing phantom specialization requires edge-level evaluation and cross-condition transfer tests.

Abstract: Circuit discovery methods identify subgraphs that explain specific model behaviors, and structural differences between discovered circuits are commonly interpreted as evidence of distinct mechanisms. We test this assumption by varying input statistics while holding the task fixed, and show that the resulting structural differences exhibit apparent specialization but do not correspond to functional differences, a pattern we term phantom specialization. Using Literal Sequence Copying across four token-frequency bands plus a control condition in five Pythia models (70M-1.4B), we extract 75 circuits and find that structurally distinct circuits implement the same computation: band-specific edges transfer broadly across bands, a core shared across most bands recovers at least 99% of circuit performance, and causal interchange interventions confirm that internal representations are interchangeable across frequency bands. Repeated extractions within the same frequency band further suggest that discovery algorithms sample from an equivalence class of valid subgraphs rather than recovering a unique mechanism. Standard evaluation practice obscures this pattern: source-level evaluation inflates apparent faithfulness, while edge-level evaluation reveals the many-to-one mapping from structure to function. Our results show that structural differences between circuits are not sufficient evidence for distinct mechanisms, and that exposing this requires edge-level evaluation and cross-condition transfer tests.

</details>


### [190] [FOXGLOVE: Understanding Goal-Oriented and Anchored Writing Feedback from Experts and LLMs on Argumentative Essays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06271)
*Yijun Liu,Yifan Song,John Gallagher,Sarah Sterman,Tal August*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces FOXGLOVE, a dataset comparing feedback from writing instructors and LLMs on argumentative essays. Instructors and LLMs distribute feedback similarly across goals and positions but diverge on specific sentences. LLMs write more complex, less questioning feedback that receives higher quality ratings (largely due to length), enabling systematic comparison of human and machine feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the growing use of LLMs for writing feedback, no systematic comparison exists between LLM and expert human feedback along key dimensions identified in writing research: goal-orientation, anchoring to specific sentences, and prioritization.

Method: FOXGLOVE dataset: 696 feedback comments from trained writing instructors on 69 twelfth-grade argumentative essays, paired with 1,644 comments from four frontier LLMs under a shared protocol (2,340 total). Expert quality ratings provided on a subset of both instructor and LLM comments.

Result: Instructors and LLMs distribute feedback similarly across goals and essay positions, but diverge on which specific sentences receive feedback. LLMs write more complex feedback, use fewer questions, and receive higher quality ratings—though much of this advantage is attributable to longer comment length.

Conclusion: FOXGLOVE enables systematic comparison of alignment and divergence between human and LLM feedback, revealing both similarities (goal/position distribution) and key differences (sentence-level targeting, complexity, questioning style, and length-driven quality perception).

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate writing feedback, there remains no systematic comparison of LLM and expert feedback on the dimensions that writing research identifies as central to revision: goal-orientation, anchoring to specific sentences, and prioritization. We introduce FOXGLOVE, a dataset of 696 feedback comments written by trained writing instructors on 69 twelfth-grade argumentative essays, paired with 1,644 comments generated from four frontier LLMs under a shared protocol, totaling 2,340 comments. We provide expert quality ratings on a subset of both instructor and LLM comments. We find that instructors and LLMs distribute feedback similarly across goals and essay positions, yet instructors and models diverge on the specific sentences on which to provide feedback. Additionally, we find that models tend to write more complex feedback and use fewer questions than instructors. LLM feedback also receives higher ratings on most dimensions of quality, as rated by instructors, but much of this advantage appears to be attributable to lengthier comments. FOXGLOVE enables systematic comparison of where human and LLM feedback align, diverge, and differ.

</details>


### [191] [LLMs Can Leak Training Data But Do They Want To? A Propensity-Aware Evaluation of Memorization in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06286)
*Gianluca Barmina,Peter Schneider-Kamp,Lukas Galke Poech*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces PropMe, a propensity-aware framework for evaluating LLM memorization under non-adversarial conditions, and SimpleTrace, a pipeline for attributing model outputs to training data. Experiments on two open models show a gap between worst-case extractability and ordinary leakage propensity, with the latter being low overall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memorization evaluations focus on whether models can be forced to reveal training data (capability), not whether they do so under ordinary, non-adversarial use. The authors argue for a more comprehensive view that includes propensity (natural leakage tendency).

Method: PropMe is a propensity-aware framework that transforms existing memorization metrics into propensity metrics. SimpleTrace is a lightweight tracing pipeline using infini-gram to deterministically attribute model generations to training corpora, computing verbatim, near-verbatim, and propensity-transformed metrics.

Result: A consistent gap between capability and propensity: prefix attacks elicit stronger memorization signals than generic or dataset-specific prompts, while propensity scores remain low. DFM Decoder (continually pre-trained from Comma) shows reduced memorization and memorization propensity for Common Pile.

Conclusion: Memorization audits should report both worst-case extractability and ordinary leakage propensity for a comprehensive view of memorization. The results also show that continual pre-training on different data can reduce memorization capability.

Abstract: Large language models can reproduce training data, but existing memorization evaluations mostly measure whether models can be forced to do so, rather than whether they do so under ordinary use. We introduce PropMe, a propensity-aware framework for memorization evaluation that contrasts prefix-based capability attacks with non-adversarial evaluations. We propose a metric transformation that, applied to existing functions, allows to create propensity metrics. We further introduce SimpleTrace, a lightweight tracing pipeline built on infini-gram that deterministically attributes model generations to large-scale training corpora and computes verbatim, near-verbatim, and propensity-transformed memorization metrics. Evaluating two fully-open models: Comma and DFM Decoder on two datasets: Common Pile and Dynaword in two languages, we find a consistent gap between capability and propensity: prefix attacks elicit substantially stronger memorization signals than generic or dataset-specific prompts, while propensity scores remain low overall. Thus, the models can reveal training data when directly elicited, but rarely do so in more common non-adversarial settings. We also find that DFM Decoder, which is continually pre-trained from Comma, exhibits reduced memorization and memorization propensity for Common Pile, confirming that memorization capability can decrease when later training emphasizes partially different data. Our results suggest, and we encourage, that memorization audits should report both worst-case extractability and ordinary leakage propensity in order to have a more comprehensive view of this phenomenon.

</details>


### [192] [Decomposing Factual Sycophancy in Language Models: How Size and Instruction Tuning Shape Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06306)
*Victor De Marez,Luna De Bruyne,Walter Daelemans*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper decomposes factual sycophancy in LLMs into two channels—truth margin (baseline preference for truth) and manipulation sensitivity (how much social pressure shifts that preference)—and analyzes how model size (0.3B–32B parameters) and instruction tuning affect each across 13 manipulation types. Key findings: vulnerability is mainly governed by size; instruction tuning increases truth margin but its behavioral effect depends on manipulation type and model size; scaling improves margin for both base and instruction-tuned models but differentially affects sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Factual sycophancy (models abandoning correct answers under social pressure) is typically measured by flip rates, which conflate two distinct mechanisms: the model's inherent preference for truth (truth margin) and how susceptible it is to manipulation (manipulation sensitivity). The authors aim to disentangle these channels to better understand what drives sycophancy and how interventions like instruction tuning truly affect robustness.

Method: The authors decompose factual sycophancy into two channels: truth margin (baseline preference for verifiably correct answers) and manipulation sensitivity (the degree to which social pressure shifts that preference). They evaluate 56 open-weight LLMs (0.3B–32B parameters) across 13 manipulation types, analyzing how model size and instruction tuning separately affect each channel.

Result: (1) Vulnerability to factual sycophancy is primarily governed by model size. (2) Instruction tuning changes how size affects robustness: small instruction-tuned models become less robust, large ones become more robust. (3) Instruction tuning primarily increases truth margin, but its behavioral effect varies by manipulation type. (4) Scaling base models increases truth margin but also mildly increases manipulation sensitivity; scaling instruction-tuned models increases truth margin faster and decreases sensitivity.

Conclusion: Factual sycophancy is not a single scalar property. Evaluations should report channel-specific, manipulation-specific, and size-conditioned robustness rather than relying solely on flip rates.

Abstract: Factual sycophancy occurs when a language model abandons a correct, verifiable answer under social pressure. Because a flip occurs only when pressure toward a false answer exceeds the model's neutral preference for the truth, flip rates conflate two mechanisms: the strength of that baseline preference (truth margin), and how far pressure shifts it (manipulation sensitivity). We decompose factual sycophancy into these channels and use them to separate the effects of size and instruction tuning across 56 open-weight models spanning 0.3B-32B parameters and 13 manipulation types. We find that vulnerability is governed mainly by size, but instruction tuning changes how size acts: small instruction-tuned models can become less robust, whereas large instruction-tuned models usually become more robust. Instruction tuning primarily increases truth margin, but its behavioral effect depends on manipulation type. Scaling also changes the two channels differently: base models gain margin but become mildly more manipulation-sensitive, whereas instruction-tuned models gain margin faster and become less sensitive. Factual sycophancy is therefore not a single scalar property. Evaluations should report channel-specific, manipulation-specific, and size-conditioned robustness rather than flip rates alone.

</details>


### [193] ["Chi nas dal soch el sent de legn" -- Auditing Text Corpora for Lombard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06349)
*Edoardo Signoroni,Pavel Rychlý*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A manual audit of parallel and monolingual corpora for Lombard (an under-resourced Italian language continuum) reveals that web-scraped data suffers from severe language misidentification, boilerplate text, and noise; valid data shows conflicting orthographic systems and representational bias heavily favoring Western Lombard over Eastern varieties, highlighting the need for variety-aware, community-driven data curation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many under-resourced languages lack high-quality datasets for NLP tasks like Machine Translation; while large web-scraped corpora appear abundant, their actual quality and representativeness for low-resource language varieties remain unexamined, motivating a systematic audit.

Method: Manual audit of parallel and monolingual corpora for Lombard; analysis of orthographic composition across web-scraped datasets, curated corpora, and benchmarks; comparison of valid Lombard portions.

Result: Web-scraped datasets contain severe language misidentification, boilerplate text, and non-linguistic noise, making perceived abundance illusory. High-quality Lombard data is heavily skewed toward Western Lombard varieties, with Eastern varieties marginalized; conflicting orthographic systems exist across corpora.

Conclusion: The field should prioritize variety-aware, community-driven data curation over purely quantity-driven scraping for under-resourced languages like Lombard.

Abstract: Several of the world's languages are still under-resourced in terms of Natural Language Processing (NLP) tools. This is mostly due to the lack of high-quality datasets to train, develop, and evaluate systems and models for several tasks, such as Machine Translation (MT). We conduct a manual audit of the parallel and monolingual corpora available for Lombard, an under-resourced language continuum from Italy. Our analysis reveals that the perceived abundance of web-scraped data is an illusion, with massive datasets plagued by severe language misidentification, boilerplate text, and non-linguistic noise. Furthermore, we analyze the orthographic composition of the valid Lombard portions across web-scraped datasets, curated corpora, and benchmarks. Our findings show conflicting orthographical systems and severe representational bias across all corpora: high-quality data is heavily skewed towards Western Lombard varieties, with Eastern ones left on the margins. This underscores the need for variety-aware, community-driven data curation rather than purely quantity-driven scraping.

</details>


### [194] [EDIT: Evidence-Diagnosed Intervention Training for Rule-Faithful LLM Grading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06350)
*Zhihao Wu,Linhai Zhang,Taiyi Wang,Runcong Zhao,Peter Andrews,Cesare Aloisi,Yulan He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EDIT is a two-phase framework (SFT + RL) that uses internal model signals (posterior belief over final mark and input-grounding scores) to locate and revise problematic reasoning steps in LLM rubric grading, outperforming SFT and RL baselines on multi-subject benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable rubric grading requires grounding in mark schemes and student evidence, but existing credit-assignment and intervention methods designed for self-contained reasoning tasks (e.g., math) fail because they cannot identify where grading reasoning goes wrong or how the model's belief about the final mark changes during reasoning.

Method: EDIT consists of two phases: (1) EDIT-SFT locates problematic reasoning steps using internal model signals (posterior belief over final mark and input-grounding scores) and revises only those steps with rubric checklist help; (2) EDIT-RL calibrates the grader using belief-guided reward shaping that penalizes large harmful belief drifts while allowing helpful exploration.

Result: EDIT consistently outperforms strong SFT and RL baselines on in-domain and out-of-domain splits across two real-world, multi-subject grading benchmarks. Ablation studies confirm internal-state diagnostics drive these gains.

Conclusion: Internal model signals (belief and grounding) are effective for diagnosing and correcting rubric-faithful grading reasoning, and a two-phase approach combining targeted SFT revision with belief-guided RL calibration yields more reliable LLM graders.

Abstract: Reliable rubric grading requires more than accurate score prediction. Each judgement must be grounded in the mark scheme and evidence from the student answer. Existing credit-assignment and intervention methods, primarily designed for self-contained reasoning tasks such as mathematics reasoning, struggle in this setting because they do not identify where grading reasoning goes wrong or how the model's belief about the final mark changes during reasoning. We propose Evidence-Diagnosed Intervention Training (EDIT), a two-phase framework for training more rubric-faithful LLM graders. First, EDIT-SFT locates problematic reasoning steps using internal model signals: posterior belief over the final mark and input-grounding scores. It then revises only these local steps with help from a rubric checklist. Second, EDIT-RL calibrates the grader with belief-guided reward shaping, penalising large harmful belief drifts while still allowing helpful exploration. Experiments on two real-world, multi-subject grading benchmarks demonstrate that EDIT consistently outperforms strong supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning baselines on both in-domain and out-of-domain splits, with ablation studies confirming that internal-state diagnostics drive these gains.

</details>


### [195] [Emergent Language as an Approach to Conscious AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06380)
*Zengqing Wu,Chuan Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes using emergent language (EL) in multi-agent reinforcement learning as a generative methodology to study consciousness-relevant structures, avoiding human language priors. A proof-of-concept shows agents develop self-referential communication, including an echo-mismatch detection circuit, from minimal starting conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to studying artificial consciousness either use theory-derived checklists (discriminative) or directly engineer consciousness-inspired modules (architectural), but both may produce structures that are artifacts of human language priors rather than genuinely emergent properties.

Method: A generative methodology using emergent language in multi-agent reinforcement learning, where agents begin with no language, no self-concept, and minimal exposure to human text, and develop communication solely under task pressure.

Result: In a minimal proof-of-concept environment, agents developed self-referential communication, including an echo-mismatch detection circuit that cannot be predicted from task structure or architecture alone, but emerges from specific environmental affordances.

Conclusion: Emergent language methodology enables causal attributability of consciousness-relevant structures to task demands rather than inherited priors, offering a generative tool for studying consciousness in artificial systems.

Abstract: The question of whether artificial systems can be conscious remains open, in part because existing approaches either evaluate systems against theory-derived checklists (discriminative) or engineer consciousness-inspired modules directly (architectural); both leave open whether observed structures are artifacts of human language priors. We propose a generative methodology: emergent language (EL) in multi-agent reinforcement learning, where agents start from minimal (no language, no concept of self, minimal exposure to human text) and develop communication under task pressure alone, ensuring causal attributability to task demands rather than inherited human language priors. We position our methodology by discussing how EL serves as a generative tool for studying consciousness-relevant structure, including the role of environment complexity and the interpretation of emergent communication. As a proof of concept, we instantiate this methodology in a minimal environment and show that agents develop self-referential communication, including an echo-mismatch detection circuit that is not predicted by task structure or architecture alone but emerges from a specific environmental affordance.

</details>


### [196] [CollabSim: A CSCW-Grounded Methodology for Investigating Collaborative Competence of LLM Agents through Controlled Multi-Agent Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06399)
*Jiaju Chen,Bo Sun,Yuxuan Lu,Yun Wang,Dakuo Wang,Bingsheng Yao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces CollabSim, a simulation framework for evaluating collaborative competence in LLM-based multi-agent systems, drawing on CSCW theory to assess agents' ability to coordinate, establish common ground, and repair misalignment under constrained communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MAS evaluations focus on task outcomes or individual agent proficiency, but fail to assess collaborative competence—the capacity for common ground, shared understanding, and misalignment repair—which recent studies show is a key failure point in MAS.

Method: CollabSim is a configurable simulation framework combining a theory-grounded definition of collaborative capabilities, controlled manipulation of interaction conditions, and action-level probing of agents' internal states, tested across four LLMs.

Result: Experiments show CollabSim can capture condition effects, separate model performance patterns, and reveal task-dependent effects of agent design.

Conclusion: CollabSim enables systematic analysis of collaborative competence in MAS, bridging the gap between CSCW research on human teamwork and LLM-based agent evaluation.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models have shown growing promise, with their effectiveness resting on agents' ability to coordinate through text-based channels much as human teams do. Yet recent study suggests that MAS often falter not because agents lack individual task-solving ability, but because they lack collaborative competence: the capacity to establish common ground, maintain shared task understanding, balance individual and collective incentives, and repair misalignment as interaction unfolds. Decades of research in Computer-Supported Cooperative Work have characterized these requirements for human teams coordinating under constrained communication, yet existing MAS evaluations focus mainly on task outcomes or single-agent proficiency in reasoning, planning, and tool use. To enable a systematic analysis of agents' collaborative competence in MAS, we introduce CollabSim, a configurable simulation framework that combines a theory-grounded definition of collaborative capabilities, controlled manipulation of interaction conditions, and action-level probing of agents' internal states. Experiments across four LLMs show that CollabSim can capture condition effects, separate model performance patterns, and reveal task-dependent effects of agent design.

</details>


### [197] [A Komi-Yazva--Russian Parallel Corpus and Evaluation Protocol for Zero- and Few-Shot LLM Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06420)
*Petr Parshakov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: First Komi-Yazva–Russian parallel corpus (457 sentence pairs, 74 narratives) with leakage-aware evaluation protocol. LLMs tested in zero-shot and retrieval-based few-shot settings; few-shot consistently outperforms zero-shot, but gains beyond small context are limited. Evaluation conclusions depend heavily on metric choice and failure handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of annotated resources and standardized evaluation protocols for endangered, extremely low-resource languages like Komi-Yazva, and to rigorously study LLM translation capabilities in such settings with leakage-aware and reproducible methodology.

Method: Constructed a 457-sentence-pair parallel corpus with provenance, sentence-level alignment, and story IDs. Evaluated LLMs on Komi-Yazva→Russian translation using story-level cross-validation, deterministic retrieval for few-shot prompting, strict output validation, reference-based and judge-based metrics, and uncertainty estimates.

Result: LLMs produce non-trivial translations; performance varies by model family and prompting regime. Retrieval-based few-shot prompting consistently improves over zero-shot, but returns diminish beyond small context. Metric choice and failure handling materially affect evaluative conclusions.

Conclusion: The corpus serves both as a dataset contribution and a reproducible evaluation testbed. Results highlight the sensitivity of evaluation design in endangered-language MT and the need for standardized protocols in low-resource settings.

Abstract: We present the first Komi-Yazva--Russian parallel corpus together with an explicit evaluation protocol for studying LLM translation in an endangered, extremely low-resource setting. The dataset contains 457 aligned sentence pairs from 74 narrative texts and is accompanied by documented provenance, sentence-level alignment, and story identifiers that enable leakage-aware evaluation. We use this setup to compare modern large language models on Komi-Yazva-to-Russian translation under severe parallel-data scarcity in zero-shot and retrieval-based few-shot regimes. The protocol includes story-level cross-validation, deterministic retrieval for few-shot prompting, strict validation of generated outputs, complementary reference-based and judge-based metrics, and story-level uncertainty estimates. Across models, LLMs produce non-trivial translations, but performance varies strongly by model family and prompting regime. Retrieval-based few-shot prompting consistently improves over zero-shot prompting, while gains beyond a small retrieved context remain limited. The results show that evaluative conclusions in this setting depend materially on metric choice and failure handling, so the paper frames the corpus as both a dataset contribution and a reproducible evaluation testbed for endangered-language machine translation.

</details>


### [198] [Reinforcement Learning Elicits Contextual Learning of Unseen Language Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06428)
*Hanxu Hu,Zdeněk Šnajdr,Pinzhen Chen,Jannis Vamvas,Rico Sennrich*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes using reinforcement learning with chrF reward to train LLMs to translate unseen low-resource languages by leveraging in-context linguistic knowledge, outperforming in-context learning and supervised fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for LLM-based translation of unseen/low-resource languages (continued training or in-context grammar books) overfit specific languages and show limited zero-shot transfer. LLMs need to acquire the meta-skill of utilizing in-context linguistic knowledge rather than memorizing specific languages.

Method: A reinforcement learning (RL) approach with surface-level chrF translation metric as reward, training LLMs to translate completely unseen languages given rich linguistic context.

Result: RL-trained models effectively extract and apply relevant linguistic information from provided context, achieving better translations on completely unseen languages compared to in-context learning or supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Outcome-based RL can extend beyond conventional reasoning tasks (math, coding) to serve as a recipe for language learning from context.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that large language models (LLMs) can translate unseen or low-resource languages by undergoing continued training or even by encoding a grammar book in their context. However, both methods typically overfit specific languages, with limited zero-shot transfer at test time. To translate extremely low-resource languages at scale, we argue that LLMs must acquire the meta-skill of utilizing in-context linguistic knowledge rather than memorizing specific languages. In this paper, we propose a reinforcement learning (RL) approach to unseen language translation given rich linguistic context, using a surface-level translation metric (chrF) as the reward. Empirically, despite the lightweight reward, our RL-trained models effectively extract and apply relevant linguistic information from the provided context, leading to better translations on completely unseen languages than in-context learning or supervised fine-tuning. Our analyses suggest that outcome-based RL can extend beyond conventional reasoning tasks like math and coding to serve as a recipe for language learning from context.

</details>


### [199] [Revising Context, Shifting Simulated Stance: Auditing LLM-Based Stance Simulation in Online Discussions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06443)
*Xinnong Zhang,Wanting Shan,Hanjia Lyu,Zhongyu Wei,Jiebo Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a counterfactual context revision framework to audit LLM-based stance simulation, evaluating how semantically independent changes to conversational contexts affect simulated user stances. Both text-only and multimodal (meme-based) revision strategies are tested, showing effective and robust stance transitions across different polarization preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate social media users and infer how individuals may respond to online discussions. However, it remains unclear whether these simulations reflect precise user-specific beliefs or whether they are highly sensitive to semantically independent changes in conversational contexts.

Method: Given an original online conversation, the authors first infer a target user's stance toward a specific topic. They then apply controlled revision strategies to the conversational context and simulate the user's stance again under the revised context. They compare text-only revision strategies with a multimodal one that incorporates meme-based context and evaluate two main effectiveness metrics: average directional stance shift and stance transition rate.

Result: The results reveal effective and robust stance transitions in both text-only and multimodal strategies across different polarization-preference mechanisms.

Conclusion: This study contributes an evaluation framework for understanding the context sensitivity of LLM-based stance simulation. More broadly, it highlights both the promise and risk of using LLMs to simulate online opinion dynamics.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate social media users and infer how individuals may respond to online discussions. However, it remains unclear whether these simulations reflect precise user-specific beliefs or whether they are highly sensitive to semantically independent changes in conversational contexts. In this work, we study counterfactual context revision as a framework for auditing LLM-based stance simulation. Given an original online conversation, we first infer a target user's stance toward a specific topic. We then apply controlled revision strategies to the conversational context and simulate the user's stance again under the revised context. We compare text-only revision strategies with a multimodal one that incorporates meme-based context and evaluate two main effectiveness metrics, i.e., average directional stance shift and stance transition rate. The results reveal effective and robust stance transitions in both text-only and multimodal strategies across different polarization-preference mechanisms. Our study contributes an evaluation framework for understanding the context sensitivity of LLM-based stance simulation. More broadly, it highlights both the promise and risk of using LLMs to simulate online opinion dynamics.

</details>


### [200] [Latent Reasoning with Normalizing Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06447)
*Guancheng Tu,Xiangjun Fu,Suhao Yu,Yao Tang,Haoqiang Kang,Lianhui Qin,Yizhe Zhang,Jiatao Gu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: NF-CoT introduces normalizing flows to generate continuous latent thoughts within LLMs, preserving key CoT advantages (autoregressive decoding, KV-cache, likelihood estimation) while reducing reasoning costs and improving code generation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Textual chain-of-thought forces reasoning through a discrete, serial, communication-oriented token stream where each step must be verbalized before proceeding, limiting bandwidth and efficiency. Existing latent reasoning methods sacrifice key advantages of CoT such as autoregressive generation, probabilistic sampling, KV-cache compatibility, and tractable likelihood estimation.

Method: NF-CoT instantiates a TARFlow-style normalizing flow inside the LLM backbone, defining a tractable probability model over compact continuous thoughts distilled from explicit CoT. Continuous-thought positions use an NF head, while text positions use the standard LM head—both within the same causal stream, enabling exact likelihoods, left-to-right decoding with KV cache, and policy-gradient optimization in latent space.

Result: On code-generation benchmarks, NF-CoT improves pass rates over explicit-CoT and prior latent-reasoning baselines while substantially reducing intermediate reasoning cost.

Conclusion: NF-CoT demonstrates that latent reasoning with normalizing flows can match or exceed the effectiveness of explicit CoT while preserving the desirable properties of autoregressive LLMs (e.g., KV-cache, likelihood estimation) and reducing computational overhead.

Abstract: Large language models often improve reasoning by generating explicit chain-of-thought (CoT), demonstrating the importance of intermediate computation. However, textual CoT forces this computation through a discrete, serial, and communication-oriented token stream: each reasoning step must be verbalized before the model can proceed, even when the underlying update is semantic, uncertain, or only partially formed. Latent reasoning offers a higher-bandwidth alternative by performing intermediate computation in compact continuous states before committing to text. Yet existing latent-reasoning methods often sacrifice key advantages that make CoT effective in autoregressive language models, including native left-to-right generation, probabilistic sampling, compatibility with KV-cache decoding, and tractable likelihood estimation. We propose NF-CoT, a latent reasoning framework that preserves these advantages by modeling continuous thoughts with normalizing flows. NF-CoT instantiates a TARFlow-style normalizing flow inside the LLM backbone, defining a tractable probability model over compact continuous thoughts distilled from explicit CoT. Continuous-thought positions are generated by an NF head, while text positions are generated by the standard LM head within the same causal stream. This design provides exact likelihoods for latent thoughts, enables probabilistic left-to-right decoding with the original KV cache, and supports direct policy-gradient optimization in the latent reasoning space. On code-generation benchmarks, NF-CoT improves pass rates over explicit-CoT and prior latent-reasoning baselines while substantially reducing intermediate-reasoning cost.

</details>


### [201] [Human Adults and LLMs as Scientists: Who Benefits from Active Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06464)
*Mandana Samiei,Eunice Yiu,Anthony GX-Chen,Dongyan Lin,Jocelyn Shen,Blake A. Richards,Alison Gopnik,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Adults typically struggle with conjunctive causal reasoning in passive settings, but active exploration substantially improves performance, though conjunctive rules still require more tests than disjunctive ones. LLMs approach human accuracy but show less efficient exploration and similar performance gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the well-known "conjunctive handicap" in causal learning persists when adults are granted agency through active exploration, rather than relying on passive observation paradigms.

Method: Modified "blicket detector" task where adult participants freely intervene to identify causal objects under conjunctive or disjunctive rule structures. Human performance is also compared to a range of large language models in the same setting.

Result: Active exploration substantially improves adults' conjunctive causal reasoning, though conjunctive rules still require more tests to infer than disjunctive rules. Some state-of-the-art LLMs approach human-level hypothesis inference accuracy but exhibit less efficient exploration strategies and similar conjunctive-disjunctive performance gaps.

Conclusion: Active agency mitigates but does not eliminate the conjunctive handicap in causal learning. LLMs show comparable inference accuracy but less efficient exploration strategies.

Abstract: A long-standing finding in the causal learning literature is that adults struggle to identify conjunctive causal rules, where an effect requires the simultaneous presence of multiple causes, while performing better in disjunctive settings. However, most demonstrations of this ``conjunctive handicap'' rely on passive observation paradigms with limited evidence, where learners have no control over evidence generation. This paper asks whether this bias persists when adults are granted agency through active exploration. Using a modified ``blicket detector'' task, adult participants freely intervened to identify causal objects under conjunctive or disjunctive rule structures. We show that active exploration substantially improves adults' conjunctive causal reasoning, although conjunctive rules still require more tests to infer than disjunctive rules. We further compare human performance to a range of large language models in the same setting. While some state-of-the-art models approach human-level performance on hypothesis inference accuracy, they often exhibit less efficient exploration strategies and similar conjunctive-disjunctive performance gaps.

</details>


### [202] [You Only Index Once: Cross-Layer Sparse Attention with Shared Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06467)
*Yutao Sun,Yanqi Zhang,Li Dong,Jianyong Wang,Furu Wei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CLSA proposes cross-layer sparse attention that shares both KV cache and routing indices across layers on top of KV-sharing architectures like YOCO, achieving fine-grained token sparsity with amortized routing overhead, leading to up to 7.6x decoding speedup and 17.1x throughput improvement at 128K context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLM inference is bottlenecked by decoding efficiency, especially with long chains of thought. Existing sparse attention methods face a trade-off: structured block sparse methods are fast but hurt quality, while token sparse methods are accurate but offer limited speedup due to expensive top-k routing over the full cache.

Method: CLSA is built on KV-sharing architectures (e.g., YOCO). It shares not only the KV cache across cross-decoder layers but also the routing index: a single indexer computes token-level top-k selection once, and the resulting index is reused across layers, preserving fine-grained selectivity while amortizing routing overhead.

Result: Experiments on short-context and long-context benchmarks show CLSA is both accurate and efficient, achieving up to 7.6x decoding speedup and 17.1x overall throughput improvement at 128K context.

Conclusion: CLSA provides a more complete architectural solution for long-context LLMs that jointly advances model quality and inference efficiency by improving pre-filling, KV-cache storage, and long-context decoding simultaneously.

Abstract: Long-context inference in modern LLMs is increasingly constrained by decoding efficiency, especially in reasoning-heavy settings where models generate long intermediate chains of thought. Existing sparse attention methods often face a practical efficiency-quality trade-off. Structured block sparse methods typically provide stronger acceleration but incur noticeable quality loss, while token sparse methods are usually more accurate yet deliver limited end-to-end speedup because top-k routing over the full cache remains expensive. In this work, we propose cross-layer sparse attention (CLSA), which is built on top of KV-sharing architectures such as YOCO. The core idea is to share not only the KV cache across cross-decoder layers, but also the routing index. A single indexer computes token-level top-k selection once and reuses the resulting index across layers, thereby preserving the fine-grained selectivity of token sparse attention while amortizing the routing overhead. The resulting architecture improves all major inference bottlenecks jointly, including pre-filling, KV-cache storage, and long-context decoding. Experiments across short-context and long-context benchmarks show that CLSA is both accurate and efficient, achieving up to 7.6x decoding speedup and 17.1x overall throughput improvement at 128K context. These results suggest a more complete architectural solution for long-context LLMs that jointly advances model quality and inference efficiency.

</details>


### [203] [Self-Augmenting Retrieval for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06474)
*Paul Jünger,Justin Lovelace,Linxi Zhao,Dongyoung Go,Kilian Q. Weinberger*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SARDI is a training-free, retriever-agnostic RAG framework for discrete diffusion language models that leverages low-confidence discarded tokens during denoising as lookahead signals to guide retrieval, achieving superior performance on multi-hop QA with up to 8x higher throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion models discard low-confidence predicted tokens during iterative denoising; the authors observe that even these low-confidence tokens often surface salient entities early on, providing a useful lookahead signal that can guide retrieval before the final output is ready.

Method: SARDI (Self-Augmenting Retrieval for Diffusion Language Models) is a dynamic RAG framework that captures lookahead tokens (discarded low-confidence predictions) at each denoising step and uses them to query a retriever for relevant evidence, which is then incorporated into the diffusion model's context for subsequent denoising steps. It is training-free and retriever-agnostic.

Result: Across five multi-hop QA benchmarks, SARDI outperforms existing training-free diffusion and autoregressive retrieval baselines while achieving up to 8x higher throughput.

Conclusion: Discarded tokens from discrete diffusion models are a valuable and previously overlooked signal for retrieval-augmented generation, and SARDI effectively exploits this signal to boost performance without additional training or retriever modifications.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion language models generate text by iteratively denoising an entire response in parallel. At each step, they predict tentative tokens for every masked position, committing the confident predictions to the output and discarding the unconfident ones. We show that the discarded tokens are in fact a useful lookahead signal for retrieval-augmented generation: even low-confidence tokens often surface salient entities early in the denoising trajectory, enabling retrieval of stronger evidence before the output is finalized. We exploit this through Self-Augmenting Retrieval for Diffusion Language Models (SARDI), a dynamic RAG framework that uses these lookahead tokens to guide retrieval during denoising. SARDI is training-free, retriever-agnostic, and applicable to any reasoning-capable discrete diffusion language model. Across five multi-hop QA benchmarks, SARDI outperforms current training-free diffusion and autoregressive retrieval baselines at up to $8\times$ higher throughput.

</details>


### [204] [Operation-Guided Progressive Human-to-AI Text Transformation Benchmark for Multi-Granularity AI-Text Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06481)
*Sondos Mahmoud Bsharat,Jiacheng Liu,Xiaohan Zhao,Tianjun Yao,Xinyi Shang,Yi Tang,Jiacheng Cui,Ahmed Elhagry,Salwa K. Al Khatib,Hao Li,Salman Khan,Zhiqiang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: OpAI-Bench is a benchmark for studying progressive human-to-AI text transformation across multiple granularities (document, sentence, token, span). It constructs nine sequentially revised versions per sample under predefined AI coverage levels and five edit operations across four domains. Experiments reveal that detectability depends on edit operation, domain, and revision history, with mixed-authorship intermediate versions often harder to detect than fully human or heavily AI-edited endpoints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-text detection benchmarks focus on final outputs and lack understanding of how AI authorship signals emerge, accumulate, or disappear throughout progressive human-AI co-editing processes in real-world drafting and revision workflows.

Method: OpAI-Bench constructs nine sequentially revised versions from human-written documents under predefined AI coverage levels and five representative AI edit operations, covering four domains with complete authorship provenance at document, sentence, token, and span granularities. Evaluation uses 8 document-level, 7 sentence-level, and 2 token/span-level detectors.

Result: AI-text detectability is governed by proportion of AI-edited content, edit operation, domain, and cumulative revision history. Mixed-authorship intermediate versions are often harder to detect than fully human or heavily AI-edited endpoints, revealing non-monotonic detection patterns.

Conclusion: OpAI-Bench provides a controlled testbed for analyzing whether, when, and how AI-assisted writing becomes detectable under realistic progressive editing scenarios, exposing limitations of existing benchmarks that miss non-monotonic detection patterns.

Abstract: As AI writing assistants become increasingly integrated into real-world drafting and revision workflows, many documents are no longer purely human-written or AI-generated, but instead result from progressive human-AI co-editing. However, existing AI-text detection benchmarks largely focus on final outputs and provide limited understanding of how AI authorship signals emerge, accumulate, or disappear throughout the revision process. We introduce OpAI-Bench, an operation-guided benchmark for studying progressive human-to-AI text transformation across document, sentence, token, and span granularities. Starting from human-written documents, OpAI-Bench constructs nine sequentially revised versions for each sample under predefined AI coverage levels and five representative AI edit operations, covering four domains while preserving complete authorship provenance at multiple granularities. The benchmark supports comprehensive evaluation with 8 document-level detectors, 7 sentence-level detectors, and 2 fine-grained token/span-level detectors. Experiments reveal that AI-text detectability is governed not only by the proportion of AI-edited content, but also by edit operation, domain, and cumulative revision history. Interestingly, we notice that mixed-authorship intermediate versions are often harder to detect than both fully human and heavily AI-edited endpoints, exposing non-monotonic detection patterns missed by existing benchmarks. OpAI-Bench provides a controlled testbed for analyzing whether, when, and how AI-assisted writing becomes detectable under realistic progressive editing scenarios. Our code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/VILA-Lab/OpAI-Bench.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [205] [OLIVE: Online Low-Rank Incremental Learning for Efficient Adaptive Exoskeletons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05234)
*Dong Liu,Yanxuan Yu,Ben Lengerich,Tony Geng,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OLIVE is a parameter-efficient online adaptation framework for wearable exoskeletons that decomposes control policy updates into low-rank residual form, enabling real-time personalization via on-body sensor feedback with reduced computational cost, and achieves significant improvements in gait smoothness, effort reduction, and motion stability across diverse terrains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing exoskeleton controllers rely on static gait policies that cannot adapt to dynamic real-world environments or individual user characteristics, limiting their practical utility for restoring mobility.

Method: OLIVE uses low-rank residual decomposition (ΔW = AB^T with rank r ≪ min(d,k)) to reduce online update cost from O(dk) to O(r(d+k)). Parameters are updated via reward-shaped policy gradient from on-body sensors (EMG, IMU, vibration). A gating mechanism modulates personalization strength by context, and a dynamic rank scheduler adjusts update dimensionality based on terrain complexity.

Result: OLIVE achieves +13, +22, and +15 percentage-point improvements in gait smoothness, effort reduction, and motion stability over the strongest baseline, converging within ~1,800 walking steps at 7.4 ms end-to-end latency.

Conclusion: OLIVE enables efficient, real-time online adaptation for exoskeleton control, outperforming baselines across diverse activities including flat walking, stair navigation, slopes, and uneven terrain.

Abstract: Wearable exoskeleton systems hold promise for restoring mobility in individuals with physical impairments, yet most existing controllers rely on static gait policies that lack the ability to adapt to dynamic real-world environments or individual user characteristics. We present \olive (\underline{O}nline \underline{L}ow-rank \underline{I}ncremental Learning for Efficient Adapti\underline{ve} Exoskeletons), a parameter-efficient online adaptation framework that continuously personalizes exoskeleton control during deployment. \olive decomposes the adaptive component of the control policy into a low-rank residual form~$\dW = \At\Bt^\top$ with rank~$r!\ll!\min(d,k)$, reducing online update cost from $\mathcal{O}(dk)$ to $\mathcal{O}(r(d{+}k))$ while preserving the stability of a pretrained base controller~$\Wz$. Parameters are updated via a reward-shaped policy gradient driven purely by on-body sensor feedback (EMG, IMU, vibration), eliminating dependence on offline reference trajectories. A gating mechanism modulates the strength of personalization based on contextual state, and a dynamic rank scheduler adapts the update dimensionality to terrain complexity -- allocating minimal capacity on simple flat terrain and expanding to higher-rank updates on demanding uneven surfaces -- enabling robust performance across diverse activities: flat walking, stair navigation, slopes, and uneven terrain. Experiments on the wearable platform demonstrate that \olive achieves +13, +22, and +15 percentage-point improvements in gait smoothness, effort reduction, and motion stability over the strongest baseline, converging within $\sim$1{,}800 walking steps at 7.4,ms end-to-end latency. Our code implementation is available at https://github.com/FastLM/OLIVE.

</details>


### [206] [A New Quaternion-Joint Cable-Driven Redundant Manipulator Configuration and its Control Through FABRIK and Residual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05236)
*Tanapath Pornthisan,Thanapat Kemthong,Thanyapisit Kangsathien,Pasut Aranchaiya,Paulo Garcia,Viboon Sangveraphunsiri*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a 4-segment, 8-joint cable-driven redundant manipulator using quaternion-joints that achieves broader workspace at lower hardware cost, and shows that Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude in positional and orientational accuracy for controlling this manipulator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic arms capable of traversing arbitrary spatial paths in highly obstructed workspaces are highly desired across industries. Quaternion-joints reduce the number of required motors per degree of freedom for cable-driven redundant manipulators, enabling more compact solutions, but their kinematic complexity challenges configuration decisions and imposes high computational demands.

Method: A 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator configuration using quaternion-joints was designed. Residual Reinforcement Learning was applied for control and compared against the FABRIK algorithm. The complete FABRIK process and corresponding learning implementation are described.

Result: The 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator achieves a broader workspace than extant configurations at lower hardware cost. Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude on positional and orientational accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed configuration is more workspace-effective than prior designs. The control implementation is simpler, and the methodology provides designers with tools for developing new manipulator configurations and corresponding control systems.

Abstract: Robotic arms capable of traversing arbitrary spatial paths, especially in highly obstructed workspaces, are highly desired across several industries. Quaternion-joints have recently empowered a specific class of robotic arms -- cable-driven redundant manipulators -- beyond its prior capabilities. Specifically, quaternion-joints reduce the number of required motors per degree of freedom, paving the way for more compact solutions.An ongoing challenge is that the complexity of the kinematic model of quaternion joints challenges a priori decisions on manipulator configurations and imposes higher computational demands on the control system and its non-linearities amplify all discrepancies between design and physical artifact arising from fabrication imprecision. Here we show a that a 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator can achieve a broader workspace than extant configurations, at lower hardware cost, and that Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms extant state-of-the-art methods -- specifically, the FABRIK algorithm -- on the control of such manipulator. Our results show that this configuration is more workspace-effective than prior designs, and that Residual Reinforcement Learning outperforms FABRIK by three orders of magnitude on positional and orientational accuracy, effecting precise control of the novel 4-segment, 8-joint manipulator. Additionally, the control implementation is simpler: we describe the complete FABRIK process for control and corresponding learning implementation. Our methodology is applicable to the design of new systems, providing designers with further tools for the development of this class of manipulators and corresponding control systems for novel configurations.

</details>


### [207] [Inverse Manipulation through Symbolic Planning and Residual Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05248)
*Yigit Yildirim,Giuseppe Rauso,Riccardo Caccavale,Alberto Finzi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A hybrid framework for inverse manipulation that combines symbolic inverse planning (STRIPS-like operators from demonstrations) with residual RL to bridge the gap between symbolic and continuous dynamics, validated on a PushCube task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic inverse plans fail under continuous interaction dynamics; reversing task execution requires more than reversing symbolic state transitions or rewinding motor trajectories.
