Method: Extracts STRIPS-like operators from demonstrations via soft geometric predicates; constructs inverse restoration objectives (preserve preconditions, restore deletes, negate adds); uses task planner to satisfy objectives; unresolved predicates induce residual RL (Soft Actor-Critic) to refine the skill.

Result: On ManiSkill3 PushCube, symbolic inverse performs coarse pick-and-place restoration; residual SAC policy refines cube pose, turning approximate symbolic inverse into physically grounded inverse skill.

Conclusion: Predicate-derived residual control effectively bridges the gap between symbolic inverse plans and continuous physical execution for robotic manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Inverting a robotic task requires more than reversing symbolic state transitions or rewinding motor trajectories. In robot manipulation tasks, symbolic inverse plans often fail to fully restore the effects of forward executions under continuous interaction dynamics. We present a hybrid framework for inverse manipulation that derives inverse-skill objectives from STRIPS-like operators automatically extracted from demonstrations through soft geometric predicates. For each extracted operator, we construct an inverse restoration objective that preserves preconditions, restores delete effects, and negates add effects. A task planner first attempts to satisfy this objective using available action primitives. Unresolved symbolic predicates then induce a residual operator learning problem solved through Reinforcement Learning (RL). We evaluate the framework on the ManiSkill3 PushCube task. For a forward pushing skill, the symbolic inverse performs a coarse pick-and-place restoration, while a residual Soft Actor-Critic policy refines the cube pose to satisfy the remaining inverse predicates. Our results show that predicate-derived residual control can turn an approximate symbolic inverse into a physically grounded inverse skill.

</details>


### [208] [Efficient Computation of Distance Functions for Navigation Vector Fields in Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05372)
*Vinicius M. Gonçalves,João Baião,Felipe Bartelt,Douglas G. Macharet,Gustavo M. Freitas,Héctor Azpúrua,Luciano C. A. Pimenta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes an efficient method for computing the distance between a point and a G-polynomial curve (generalized polynomial curves on matrix Lie groups) by reducing the problem to polynomial root-finding, significantly reducing computation time while maintaining accuracy for robot control applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vector field methods for robot path-tracking on Lie groups require expensive distance and closest-point computations, especially at high control frequencies on embedded platforms. Efficient computation is needed.

Method: Exploits the algebraic structure of G-polynomial curves to transform the distance computation problem into a small number of polynomial root-finding problems, avoiding iterative optimization.

Result: Simulation results show significant reduction in computation time while maintaining accuracy compared to optimization-based approaches. Method validated experimentally on a robotic manipulator. Practical formulas provided for SE(3).

Conclusion: The proposed method enables efficient distance computation for vector field control on Lie groups, with an open-source implementation available.

Abstract: Vector-field-based methods are widely used for robot control and are often applied to the path-tracking problem. Some vector field approaches require repeatedly computing the distance between the robot configuration and the curve, as well as the corresponding closest point. Recently, vector fields have been extended to Lie Groups. In this case, this computation can be expensive, especially when performed at high control frequencies on embedded platforms. This paper proposes a method for efficiently computing the distance between a point and a curve represented as what is called a G-polynomial curve, which is a curve representation that generalizes polynomial curves to matrix Lie groups. The proposed approach exploits the structure of these curves to reduce the problem to a small number of polynomial root-finding computations. Simulation results show that the method significantly reduces computation time while maintaining accuracy compared to existing optimization-based approaches. Practical formulas are also provided for the case of the group SE(3), and the method is validated experimentally on a robotic manipulator. The methodology is implemented in a computational package, available online.

</details>


### [209] [VASO: Formally Verifiable Self-Evolving Skills for Physical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05395)
*Yunhao Yang,Neel P. Bhatt,Kevin Wang,Samuel Tetteh,Zhangyang Wang,Ufuk Topcu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VASO is a framework that uses formal verification (model checking) to guide the self-evolution of LLM-generated robot skill contracts. It represents skills as semantic contracts with formal and planner-facing interfaces, filters inconsistent contracts via model checking, and uses counterexample traces as textual gradients to update skills without modifying foundation model weights. It achieves 97.2% formal-specification compliance on Jackal and quadcopter tasks with fewer than 100 optimization samples, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While foundation models have reduced the cost of creating reusable robot skills, the cost of trusting them remains high. Existing skill-evolution methods rely on execution feedback, unit tests, environment reward, or LLM self-critique, which only provide trace-level evidence that a skill worked on sampled executions—not that plans satisfy temporal safety contracts under untested conditions.

Method: VASO represents each skill as a semantic contract with two interfaces: (1) a formal interface aligning robot states/observations/commands with logical propositions for model checking, and (2) a planner-facing interface guiding executable behavior generation. A model checker filters logically inconsistent contracts and verifies induced plans against global/local temporal specifications. When verification fails, counterexample traces are translated into textual gradients that update the skill contract while keeping foundation-model weights frozen.

Result: On Clearpath Jackal and PX4 quadcopter tasks, VASO achieves 97.2% formal-specification compliance using fewer than 100 optimization samples, outperforming execution-feedback, prompt-optimization, and fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: VASO is the first framework that closes the loop between formal verification and self-evolving LLM-generated skills for physical AI agents, using formal counterexamples as optimization feedback for reusable robot skill contracts rather than merely verifying one-off plans, tuning prompts, or fine-tuning model weights.

Abstract: Reusable robot skills are becoming the basic units through which embodied agents turn open-ended instructions into long-horizon physical behavior. We argue that, while foundation models have collapsed the cost of creating these skills, the cost of trusting them has not. Existing skill-evolution loops refine skills through execution feedback, unit tests, environment reward, or LLM self-critique, but these signals provide only trace-level evidence: they show that a skill worked on sampled executions, not that skill-induced plans satisfy temporal safety contracts under untested conditions. We introduce VASO, a framework for verification-guided self-evolution of LLM-generated robot skill contracts. In VASO, each skill is represented as a semantic contract with two coupled interfaces: a formal interface that aligns robot states, observations, and control commands with logical propositions for model checking, and a planner-facing interface that guides executable behavior generation. A model checker first filters logically inconsistent skill contracts, then verifies plans induced by the skill against global and local temporal specifications. When verification fails, VASO translates the counterexample trace into a textual gradient that updates the reusable skill contract while keeping foundation-model weights frozen. On Clearpath Jackal and PX4 quadcopter tasks, VASO reaches 97.2% formal-specification compliance using fewer than 100 optimization samples, outperforming execution-feedback, prompt-optimization, and fine-tuning baselines. To our knowledge, VASO is the first framework that closes the loop between formal verification and self-evolving LLM-generated skills for physical AI agents: formal counterexamples become optimization feedback for reusable robot skill contracts, rather than merely verifying one-off plans, tuning planner prompts, or fine-tuning model weights.

</details>


### [210] [MoDex: A Diffusion Policy for Sequential Multi-Object Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05407)
*Haofei Lu,Hongjia Liu,Yifei Dong,Florian T. Pokorny,Jens Lundell,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoDex is a diffusion policy for sequential multi-object grasping with a single dexterous hand. It uses an opposition space condition to reserve unused fingers for subsequent grasps, trains via imitation learning then RL fine-tuning, and outperforms baselines by 2.92-17.92% in simulation and 6.67-17.78% in reality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dexterous grasping methods commit all degrees of freedom to a single object, underutilizing dexterity and leaving no redundancy for subsequent grasps without releasing held objects.

Method: MoDex is a diffusion policy that predicts gripper pose from observations, conditioned on an opposition space (specifying which fingers participate) and point cloud. Training uses two-stage sim-to-real: first imitation learning on expert demonstrations, then RL fine-tuning.

Result: In simulation (MuJoCo-based Franka Emika Panda + Allegro Hand) and real-world experiments, MoDex achieves higher success rates than learning-based baselines, improving by 2.92-17.92% (simulation) and 6.67-17.78% (real-world).

Conclusion: The opposition space conditioning effectively enables partial hand usage for current grasps while reserving degrees of freedom for subsequent grasps. Two-stage training (IL + RL) consistently improves success rates over pre-trained policy alone.

Abstract: This work addresses sequentially grasping multiple objects with a single dexterous hand without releasing those already held. Most dexterous grasping methods commit all of the hand's degrees of freedom to a single object, underutilizing its dexterity and leaving no redundancy for subsequent grasps. The proposed solution, MoDex, is a diffusion policy that predicts the next gripper pose directly from observations, conditioned on an opposition space and point cloud. The opposition space condition specifies which fingers participate in the current grasp, enabling the gripper to use only a subset of its available degrees of freedom while reserving the remaining degrees of freedom for subsequent grasps. To facilitate sim-to-real transfer, MoDex is trained in two stages: first through imitation learning on expert demonstrations, and subsequently through reinforcement learning fine-tuning, which consistently improves success rates over the pre-trained policy. We evaluate MoDex in simulation on a MuJoCo-based Franka Emika Panda robot equipped with an Allegro Hand and on the corresponding real-world hardware platform. Across both simulation and real-world experiments, MoDex achieves higher success rates than the evaluated learning-based baselines, improving performance by 2.92-17.92% and 6.67-17.78%, respectively. Project page: https://modex2026.github.io/.

</details>


### [211] [Learning from Demonstrations over Riemannian Manifolds using Neural ODEs: An Extended Abstract](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05422)
*Diana Cuervo Espinosa,Mahathi Anand,Angela P. Schoellig*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes learning from demonstrations on Riemannian manifolds using neural ODEs to estimate geodesics for encoding both position and orientation data, enabling natural robot motion generation while reducing computational overhead compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional LfD methods operate in Euclidean spaces, but robot states like orientation naturally exist on curved (Riemannian) manifolds. This mismatch can lead to unnatural motion, motivating the need for manifold-aware approaches.

Method: The framework numerically estimates geodesic paths on Riemannian manifolds using neural ordinary differential equations (neural ODEs), which reduces computational overhead compared to existing geodesic computation approaches. The learned geodesics are then decoded back into the original task space for robot deployment.

Result: Initial simulation experiments show promising results with comparisons to other geodesic computation mechanisms, though presented as an extended abstract with preliminary findings.

Conclusion: The approach enables natural, complex motion generation by properly encoding both position and orientation data on Riemannian manifolds, with neural ODEs offering an efficient way to compute geodesics. Challenges and future work directions are discussed.

Abstract: Learning from demonstratins (LfD) is usually performed over Euclidean spaces, while the robot state, e.g. orientation, naturally evolves over curved spaces. Therefore, to ensure natural, complex motion generation, we investigate learning from demonstrations over Riemannian manifolds that are capable of encoding both position and orientation data. Here, geodesic paths provide for natural motion between two arbitrary points within the manifold. We propose to numerically estimate geodesics via neural ordinary differential equations, mitigating large computational overhead of existing approaches. Finally, these geodesics can be decoded back into the original task space before deploying on the robot. In this extended abstract, we discuss the architecture of our framework, provide some initial insights from our simulation experiments, including comparison to other geodesic computation mechanisms, and discuss the challenges and prospects for future work.

</details>


### [212] [Uncertainty-Aware Adaptive Sensor Fusion for Autonomous Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05437)
*Simegnew Yihunie Alaba,Yuichi Motai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid deep learning approach combining Vision Transformer (ViT) and Multiscale CNN (MCNN) with an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) for visual-inertial odometry pose estimation, using adaptive sensor fusion and uncertainty-aware learning to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy at 155 FPS on KITTI dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve pose estimation accuracy and robustness in Visual-Inertial Odometry under challenging conditions such as noisy, incomplete, or unreliable sensor inputs, while maintaining computational efficiency for resource-constrained autonomous systems.

Method: A hybrid deep learning model integrating: (1) Vision Transformer (ViT) to capture temporal dependencies from IMU data, (2) Multiscale CNN (MCNN) to learn optical flow motion cues from visual data, (3) an adaptive sensor fusion module with uncertainty-based dynamic weighting, and (4) a novel uncertainty-aware loss function for robust learning under unreliable inputs.

Result: Outperforms baseline approaches on KITTI dataset in both Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE), achieving 155 FPS inference on an NVIDIA A100 GPU.

Conclusion: The proposed lightweight and computationally efficient model is highly suitable for deployment in resource-constrained autonomous systems, demonstrating robust and accurate navigation under diverse environmental conditions.

Abstract: This work introduces a hybrid deep learning approach integrated with an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) to enhance pose estimation accuracy in Visual-Inertial Odometry (VIO) for autonomous navigation. The proposed model employs a Vision Transformer (ViT) network to effectively capture temporal dependencies from inertial measurement unit (IMU) data and utilizes a Multiscale Convolutional Neural Network (MCNN) to learn optical flow-based motion cues from visual data. An adaptive sensor fusion module dynamically weights IMU and visual features by leveraging estimated uncertainty, thus improving robustness in diverse and challenging environmental conditions. Additionally, a novel uncertainty-aware loss function is proposed to explicitly incorporate prediction uncertainty into the learning process, enabling robust and accurate navigation under noisy, incomplete, or unreliable sensor inputs. Comprehensive evaluations of the KITTI dataset demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms baseline approaches, achieving superior performance in terms of Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) and Relative Pose Error (RPE). The lightweight and computationally efficient model processes data at 155 FPS on an NVIDIA A100 GPU, making it highly suitable for deployment in resource-constrained autonomous systems.

</details>


### [213] [FlowPRO: Reward-Free Reinforced Fine-Tuning of Flow-Matching VLAs via Proximalized Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05468)
*Yihao Wu,He Zhang,Junbo Tan,Xueqian Wang,Zhengyou Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FlowPRO is a reward-free offline reinforced fine-tuning framework for flow-matching VLAs. It introduces RPRO (preference optimization with proximal regularization) to prevent reward hacking, and a teleoperated intervention-and-rollback data collection paradigm with Smooth Interpolation to generate dense preference supervision. It achieves state-of-the-art success rates on four long-horizon bimanual tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training VLA models for reliable real-world robot deployment remains a major bottleneck. SFT/DAgger only indirectly exploit failure signals, and reward-based RL suffers from difficult real-world reward design and training reliable critics.

Method: Proposes FlowPRO framework with two key components: (1) RPRO (Robotic Flow-matching Proximalized Preference Optimization) — a preference-optimization objective with contrastive optimizer plus explicit proximal regularizer that anchors absolute reward magnitude to prevent reward hacking; (2) Data-side: teleoperated intervention-and-rollback paradigm producing paired positive/negative trajectories from a single operator action, plus Smooth Interpolation with batch mixing converting sparse corrections into dense per-state supervision.

Result: On four long-horizon bimanual tasks, FlowPRO achieves the highest success rate, outperforming four representative baselines. Ablations confirm each loss component contributes to performance.

Conclusion: FlowPRO provides an effective reward-free offline reinforcement fine-tuning approach for flow-matching VLAs, eliminating reward hacking through proximal regularization and enabling efficient preference data collection via intervention-and-rollback paradigm.

Abstract: Post-training Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models into policies that can be reliably deployed on real robots remains a major bottleneck. SFT and DAgger exploit failure signals only indirectly, and reward-based RL is bottlenecked by the difficulty of real-world reward design and of training reliable critics. We present FlowPRO, a reward-free offline reinforced fine-tuning framework for flow-matching VLAs. Algorithmically, we propose RPRO (Robotic Flow-matching Proximalized Preference Optimization), a preference-optimization objective tailored to the flow-matching action head of VLA models. RPRO pairs a contrastive optimizer with an explicit proximal regularizer that anchors the absolute magnitude of the implicit reward, thereby eliminating the reward-hacking failure mode of plain Flow-DPO. On the data side, a teleoperated intervention-and-rollback paradigm produces naturally paired positive and negative trajectories $(τ^w, τ^l)$ on a real robot from a single operator action; a Smooth Interpolation procedure, combined with batch mixing, then converts these sparse corrections into dense per-state supervision while preserving the base policy's capabilities. On four long-horizon bimanual tasks, FlowPRO attains the highest success rate, outperforming four representative baselines, and ablations confirm the contribution of each loss component.

</details>


### [214] [Learning Contact Representation for Leg Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05501)
*Emre Girgin,Cagri Kilic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a self-supervised representation learning framework for contact detection in legged robot odometry that uses only joint encoder data, avoiding reliance on force sensors. The method models stance and swing phases probabilistically and outperforms supervised and baseline probabilistic approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legged robot odometry relies on accurate foot contact detection (stance vs. swing phase). Force sensors at the foot are commonly used but not universally available and fail under disturbances like slippage. The authors aim to develop a contact detection method using only standard joint encoders without force sensor augmentation.

Method: A self-supervised representation learning framework that learns from joint encoder data to probabilistically model stance and swing phases. No force sensor labels are required during training; the model learns representations directly from the standard sensor set.

Result: The proposed self-supervised contact detector outperforms supervised methods that require sensor augmentation and labeling, as well as baseline probabilistic approaches. Code is publicly released.

Conclusion: Self-supervised learning from joint encoders alone is effective for contact detection in legged robots, eliminating the need for additional force sensors while achieving superior performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: The estimation of odometry in legged robots depends on the assumption that the velocity of the foot with respect to the world remains zero during the stance phase. Feedback for the main body velocity is derived from the kinematic serial chain of the feet making accurate leg phase detection is a critical subproblem. A considerable number of studies employ ground reaction force sensors mounted at the tip of the foot to classify, yet these sensors may not be universally available for all legged robots. Additionally, these sensors are often unresponsive to unaccounted disturbances, such as slippage, while the foot remains in contact with the ground. In this study, we propose a self-supervised representation learning framework for contact detection that utilizes the standard sensor set of joint encoders without reliance on force sensor augmentations. We employ learned representations to model the stance and swing phases probabilistically. The experimental results obtained confirm the efficacy of the proposed self-supervised contact detector. Our framework exhibited superior performance in comparison to supervised methods which necessitate sensor set augmentation and labeling, as well as baseline probabilistic approaches. Additionally, we make our code available to the public.

</details>


### [215] [Auditing Demonstration Curation Metrics: Action-Only Scorers Fail on the Structural Defects That Degrade Imitation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05588)
*Aarav Bedi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper builds a controlled testbed to evaluate seven demonstration curation metrics, finding that action-only metrics fail to detect structural errors (wrong actions at key moments) and can even be inverted, while state-trajectory metrics perform better but still recover only a third of the downstream performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation-learning policies depend on demonstration quality, but existing curation metrics are validated on different data and protocols, making it unclear which metrics actually identify harmful demonstrations.

Method: The authors construct a controlled testbed where demonstration defects are injected with known types (subtle perturbations and structural errors), then audit seven curation metrics by measuring their ability to separate defective from clean demonstrations and whether curating with each metric improves behavior-cloning policy success.

Result: Subtle perturbations are detectable by multivariate outlier scoring and removal recovers full downstream gap. Structural errors are invisible to action-only metrics; two metrics are inverted (score defective demos higher). Only state-trajectory metrics detect structural errors, recovering just a third of the downstream gap. High detection accuracy does not guarantee downstream improvement.

Conclusion: Action-only curation metrics are insufficient for detecting structural errors, and detection accuracy does not imply policy improvement. The testbed and implementations are released for future research.

Abstract: Imitation-learning policies inherit the quality of the demonstrations they are trained on, and a growing set of curation metrics promise to score and filter low-quality demonstrations automatically. These metrics are each validated on different data with different protocols, so it is unclear which of them actually identify the demonstrations that harm a policy. We build a controlled testbed in which demonstration defects are injected with known type, and audit seven curation metrics along two axes: how well each separates defective from clean demonstrations, and whether training a behavior-cloning policy on each metric's curated subset improves task success. We study two defect regimes. Subtle perturbations (correlated action noise, tremor, truncation) are detectable by multivariate outlier scoring and, once removed, recover the full downstream gap. Structural errors, where the demonstration executes a wrong action at a key moment, are invisible to every action-only metric we test, and two of them are inverted: they score defective demonstrations as higher quality and, used for curation, tend to leave the policy at or below the uncurated baseline rather than above it. Only metrics that examine the state trajectory detect structural errors, and even the best of them recovers just a third of the downstream gap. High detection accuracy does not guarantee downstream improvement. We release the testbed and all curation implementations.

</details>


### [216] [Discrete-WAM: Unified Discrete Vision-Action Token Editing for World-Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05645)
*Ziyang Yao,Haochen Liu,Yuncheng Jiang,Zeyu Zhu,Zibin Guo,Jingru Wang,Tianle Liu,Jianwei Cui,Kuiyuan Yang,Hongwei Xie,Jingwei Zhao,Guang Chen,Hangjun Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Discrete-WAM is a unified latent vision-action world policy that aligns future visual states and ego actions as discrete tokens, enabling compositional causal reasoning and counterfactual reasoning for autonomous driving. It uses a shared discrete diffusion framework to jointly model world dynamics, action policy, and hierarchical decision-making, achieving competitive performance on large-scale benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing end-to-end autonomous driving methods rely on direct state-to-action mappings that capture correlations without modeling action-conditioned dynamics, while continuous-latent world models lack compositional structure for causal reasoning across counterfactual futures. This limits reliable decision-making.

Method: Discrete-WAM represents future visual states and ego actions as aligned discrete tokens, establishing a shared discrete diffusion framework that jointly formulates world modeling, world-action policy, and hierarchical decision-enabled policy, enabling compositional generalization across diverse driving scenarios.

Result: Competitive performance on large-scale autonomous-driving benchmarks, with support for controllable generation and counterfactual reasoning.

Conclusion: Discrete-WAM offers a principled path toward more reliable autonomous driving decision-making by enabling compositional causal reasoning across alternative futures.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires reasoning about how ego actions shape the evolution of the surrounding world. However, most end-to-end methods rely on direct state-to-action mappings, capturing correlations without explicitly modeling action-conditioned dynamics. Conversely, continuous-latent world models often lack compositional structure for causal reasoning across counterfactual futures. We introduce Discrete-WAM, a unified latent vision-action world policy that represents future visual states and ego actions as aligned discrete tokens, enabling compositional causal reasoning across alternative futures. Built upon this unified discrete alignment, Discrete-WAM establishes a shared discrete diffusion framework with unified generative tasks, jointly formulating world modeling, world-action policy, and hierarchical decision-enabled policy, supporting compositional generalization across diverse driving scenarios. Experiments on large-scale autonomous-driving benchmarks show that Discrete-WAM achieves competitive performance while supporting controllable generation and counterfactual reasoning, offering a principled path toward more reliable decision-making.

</details>


### [217] [Safe Embodied AI for Long-horizon Tasks: A Cross-layer Analysis of Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05660)
*Dabin Kim,Daemin Park,Sangyub Lee,Jinsik Kim,Yeongtak Oh,Jongho Shin,Sungroh Yoon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This survey provides a structured review of safety in long-horizon robotic manipulation from an embodied AI perspective, organizing the literature by intervention locus (planning-time, policy-time, execution-time safety) and analyzing evidence strength. It identifies persistent gaps including limited policy-time safety evidence, weak formal support for contact-rich manipulation, immature uncertainty-triggered intervention, and shortage of manipulation-specific safety benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As embodied AI systems increasingly operate over extended horizons in physical environments, safety becomes critical because failures can harm people, damage objects, and disrupt workplaces. However, the safety literature remains fragmented across planning, policy design, and runtime execution, motivating a structured survey.

Method: The paper organizes the literature by intervention locus (planning-time, policy-time, execution-time safety) and analyzes evidence strength across three categories: formal guarantees, statistical support, and empirical safety heuristics. It distinguishes backbone capability papers, direct safety mechanisms, and benchmark/evaluation studies.

Result: The framework reveals where safety claims are well supported and where they remain indirect. Identified gaps include limited evidence for policy-time safety, weak formal support for contact-rich long-horizon manipulation, immature uncertainty-triggered intervention, and a shortage of manipulation-specific safety benchmarks.

Conclusion: The paper outlines research directions for cross-layer assurance, evaluation design, and safer deployment of long-horizon robotic agents in real-world settings.

Abstract: Embodied AI systems are increasingly expected to reason and act over extended horizons in physical environments. This growing capability brings safety to the foreground, because failures in the physical world can harm people, damage objects, and disrupt workplaces. Although safe embodied AI has attracted substantial attention, the literature remains fragmented across planning, policy design, and runtime execution. Long-horizon robotic manipulation is a particularly revealing anchor domain for this problem because semantic misgrounding, subtask-level error propagation, execution drift, and contact-rich physical risk can accumulate within the same closed-loop system. This survey therefore provides a structured review of safety in long-horizon robotic manipulation from an embodied AI perspective. We organize the literature by intervention locus, covering planning-time, policy-time, and execution-time safety, and we analyze the strength of the evidence that each line of work provides, distinguishing formal guarantees, statistical support, and empirical safety heuristics. This framework clarifies the distinct roles of backbone capability papers, direct safety mechanisms, and benchmark or evaluation studies, while exposing where current safety claims are well supported and where they remain indirect. We identify persistent gaps, including limited evidence for policy-time safety, weak formal support for contact-rich long-horizon manipulation, immature uncertainty-triggered intervention, and a shortage of manipulation-specific safety benchmarks. We conclude by outlining research directions for cross-layer assurance, evaluation design, and safer deployment of long-horizon robotic agents in real-world settings.

</details>


### [218] [Preserving Full 6-DOF Actuation Under Abrupt Total Rotor Failures: Passive Fault-Tolerant Flight Control Using a Biaxial-Tilt Hexacopter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05663)
*Yipeng Yang,Yiqiao Tang,Hao Zhang,Jinqi Jiang,Jianfeng He,Rumo Chen,Xinghu Yu,Zhan Li,Huijun Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents passive fault-tolerant flight control for a biaxial-tilt overactuated hexacopter (BTO) under abrupt total rotor failures without requiring fault detection or online optimization. It extends the attainable wrench space (AWS) inscribed-sphere metric with a transient-wrench-jump term, proposes two computationally efficient passive control schemes (HOFA+LESO and MRAC-based allocation), and validates via simulations and experiments that BTO provides larger recovery margins than uniaxial-tilt and coplanar designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional multirotors experience rapid collapse of attainable wrench space under abrupt total rotor failures, making full 6-DOF recovery physically impossible. This work addresses the challenge of passive fault-tolerant flight for overactuated hexacopters without relying on explicit fault detection, isolation, or mode switching.

Method: (1) Extended the inscribed-sphere metric of AWS by incorporating a transient-wrench-jump term for quantitative feasibility assessment under up to three simultaneous rotor failures. (2) Developed two passive schemes: a controller-layer scheme combining HOFA controller with LESO for disturbance rejection, and an allocator-layer scheme using model-reference adaptive control allocation with momentum-based wrench estimation.

Result: Simulations and flight experiments validated stable hovering and 6-DOF trajectory tracking under single and multiple rotor failures. BTO provided larger recovery margins than uniaxial-tilt and coplanar designs. Additional experiments (indoor/outdoor tracking, narrow-frame traversal, contact-based aerial writing) confirmed robustness in complex environments.

Conclusion: The proposed passive fault-tolerant framework enables effective 6-DOF control recovery of BTO under abrupt rotor failures without fault detection. The BTO architecture offers superior recovery margins compared to uniaxial-tilt and coplanar hexacopters.

Abstract: Conventional multirotors suffer from a rapid collapse of attainable wrench space (AWS) under abrupt total rotor failures, rendering full 6-DOF recovery physically impossible. This paper addresses passive fault-tolerant flight of a biaxial-tilt overactuated hexacopter (BTO) under abrupt total rotor failures that are a priori unknown to the controller. The control design and analysis focus on representative abrupt rotor-failure cases for which the post-failure system remains fully actuated, while no explicit fault detection, isolation, or fault-mode switching is assumed. First, we extend the inscribed-sphere metric of the AWS by incorporating the transient-wrench-jump term, enabling quantitative feasibility assessment under up to three simultaneous rotor failures and benchmarking against uniaxial-tilt and coplanar hexacopters. Second, we develop two computationally efficient passive schemes without relying on fault detection or online optimization. One scheme operates at the controller layer by combining a high-order fully actuated (HOFA) controller with a linear extended state observer (LESO) for lumped-disturbance rejection. The other scheme operates at the allocator layer by using model-reference adaptive control allocation with momentum-based wrench estimation to compensate for control-allocation biases. Simulations and flight experiments validate stable hovering and 6-DOF trajectory tracking under single and multiple rotor failures. Further systematic comparisons confirm that the BTO provides larger recovery margins than uniaxial-tilt and coplanar designs. Additional onboard-sensor-only experiments, including indoor tracking under wind disturbance, outdoor tracking under extreme conditions, narrow-frame traversal, and contact-based aerial writing, further validate the robustness of the proposed framework in complex operational environments.

</details>


### [219] [Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery in Robotic Cellular Warehousing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05669)
*Cheng Ren,Ming Li,Xinping Guan,George Q. Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper formulates the Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery problem with internal order evolution for robotic warehousing, and proposes two event-triggered online replanning algorithms (Dynamic Token Passing and Cooperative Token Passing) that significantly reduce order flowtime compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real warehouse operations involve dynamic order evolution where new SKUs may be appended to an order while it is being executed, a practical requirement not addressed by classical MAPD formulations that assume static tasks.

Method: Two event-triggered online replanning algorithms based on the token passing paradigm: (1) Dynamic Token Passing performs localized replanning via add-order decomposition and priority-based token scheduling; (2) Cooperative Token Passing enables idle robots to opportunistically assist newly added pickups.

Result: Simulation results in RCWS environments demonstrate that the proposed methods significantly reduce order flowtime compared with static and non-cooperative baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed event-triggered replanning algorithms effectively handle dynamic order evolution in robotic warehousing, improving system-level efficiency over static and non-cooperative approaches.

Abstract: Robotic Cellular Warehousing Systems (RCWS) give rise to multi-agent pickup and delivery (MAPD) processes in which robots sequentially collect multiple stock-keeping units (SKUs) for each order. Unlike classical MAPD formulations that assume static tasks, real warehouse operations often involve dynamic order evolution, where new SKUs may be appended to an order while it is being executed. Motivated by this practical requirement, this letter formulates the Dynamic Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery problem considering internal order evolution for the first time. Building on the token passing paradigm, we propose two event-triggered online replanning algorithms. The first, Dynamic Token Passing, performs localized replanning upon order updates through add-order decomposition and priority-based token scheduling while preserving collision-free execution. The second, Cooperative Token Passing, further enables idle robots to opportunistically assist newly added pickups, improving system-level efficiency. Simulation results in RCWS environments demonstrate that the proposed methods significantly reduce order flowtime compared with static and non-cooperative baselines.

</details>


### [220] [Accelerating and Scaling MPC-Guided Reinforcement Learning for Humanoid Locomotion and Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05687)
*Junheng Li,Liang Wu,Sergio A. Esteban,Lizhi Yang,Ján Drgoňa,Aaron D. Ames*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes MPC-RL, a framework that efficiently integrates model predictive control guidance into reinforcement learning for humanoid locomotion and manipulation, using a novel centroidal-dynamics MPC reward and a parallel-in-horizon GPU solver (πⁿMPC) to reduce training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MPC-in-RL frameworks suffer from time-consuming problem construction or excessive training overhead, making them impractical. This work aims to enable efficient training-time MPC guidance for humanoid control without these drawbacks.

Method: Introduces a centroidal-dynamics MPC reward formulation that uses MPC trajectory guidance during RL training. Develops πⁿMPC, a parallel-in-horizon, construction-free batched GPU MPC solver that operates on time-varying dynamics, avoiding high memory usage and pre-compilation for massively parallel RL.

Result: MPC-RL achieves superior performance in both locomotion and manipulation skills, validated through comparative studies and hardware experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed MPC-RL framework successfully makes training-time MPC guidance practical for humanoid control, outperforming prior methods in locomotion and manipulation tasks.

Abstract: In humanoid motion control, model predictive control (MPC) offers physically grounded prediction and constraint handling, while reinforcement learning (RL) enables robust whole-body skills through large-scale simulation. However, using MPC inside RL often requires time-consuming problem construction or excessive training overhead, making such frameworks difficult to justify in practice. This work studies efficient training-time MPC guidance for humanoid locomotion and manipulation, termed MPC-RL. We introduce a centroidal-dynamics MPC reward formulation that leverages guidance from MPC trajectories in training time. To make this practical in massively parallel RL, we develop $π^n$MPC, a parallel-in-horizon and construction-free batched GPU MPC solver that operates directly on time-varying dynamics to avoid high memory usage and pre-compilation. Through a variety of comparative studies and hardware validations, we have found that MPC-RL achieves superior performance in locomotion and manipulation skills. The code base is available at https://github.com/junhengl/mpc-rl.

</details>


### [221] [DexFuture: Hierarchical Future-State Visuomotor Targeting for Bimanual Dexterous Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05699)
*Runfa Blark Li,Kuang-Ting Tu,Nikola Raicevic,Dwait Bhatt,Xinshuang Liu,Keito Suzuki,Ki Myung Brian Lee,Nikolay Atanasov,Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexFuture is a hierarchical system for bimanual dexterous tool use that combines a high-level Future-State Visuomotor Target Predictor (using a horizon-conditioned transformer) with a low-level Target-Conditioned Structured Dexterous Policy (using a per-link transformer). It generates future reference trajectories without demonstrations or slow planning, achieving 90% of privileged-oracle performance on OakInk2 tasks while operating at 60 Hz (250x faster than CEM planning).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for bimanual dexterous tool use either depend on future configuration references from demonstrations (requiring privileged states) or use action-conditioned world models that require slow online planning over high-dimensional action sequences. There is a need for a method that generates dynamically consistent future reference trajectories without relying on demonstrations or slow counterfactual planning.

Method: DexFuture uses a two-level hierarchy: (1) a high-level Future-State Visuomotor Target Predictor that processes egocentric RGB, proprioceptive, and geometric history through a horizon-conditioned transformer to generate multi-step future target trajectories; (2) a low-level Target-Conditioned Structured Dexterous Policy with a per-link transformer that tracks these targets. This decouples coarse future reference generation from fine-grained action control and slow long-horizon semantic prediction from high-frequency execution.

Result: On OakInk2 bimanual tool-use tasks, DexFuture achieves 90% of the privileged-oracle performance, compared to only 7% for a no-reference policy. It operates at 60 Hz, approximately 250 times faster than DexWM-style CEM planning with a future action-conditioned world model.

Conclusion: The hierarchical approach of separating future reference prediction from action execution enables efficient and effective bimanual dexterous tool use without privileged information, significantly outperforming no-reference baselines and achieving comparable results to oracle methods while being orders of magnitude faster than planning-based approaches.

Abstract: Bimanual dexterous tool use remains challenging for robots due to high-dimensional hand configurations and complex hand-tool-object dynamics and contact. Most existing control policies depend on future configuration references provided from demonstrations, while future action-conditioned world models require slow online planning over high-dimensional action sequences. A significant challenge is generating a dynamically consistent future reference trajectory without relying on privileged states from demonstrations or slow counterfactual planning. We propose DexFuture, a hierarchical system that couples a high-level Future-State Visuomotor Target Predictor with a low-level Target-Conditioned Structured Dexterous Policy. Conditioned on egocentric RGB, proprioceptive and geometric history, the high-level predictor constructs structured hand-tool-object visuomotor embeddings and uses a horizon-conditioned transformer to generate a multi-step future target trajectory. Then, the low-level policy tracks them with a target-conditioned per-link transformer. This hierarchy decouples coarse future reference generation from fine-grained action control, and slow long-horizon semantic prediction from high-frequency execution. On OakInk2 bimanual tool-use tasks, DexFuture achieves 90% of the privileged-oracle performance, compared to 7% for a no-reference policy. DexFuture operates at 60 Hz, approximately 250 times faster than DexWM-style Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) planning with a future action-conditioned world model.

</details>


### [222] [PiL-World: A Chunk-Wise World Model for VLA Policy-in-the-Loop Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05773)
*Chong Ma,Taiyi Su,Jian Zhu,Jianjun Zhang,Zitai Huang,Yi Xu,Hanli Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PiL-World is a chunk-wise world model for closed-loop evaluation of VLA policies, generating consistent multi-view future observations conditioned on actions and latent histories, reducing VLA success rate estimation error from 63.2% to 12.0% on dual-arm manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models for robot action evaluation are limited to open-loop prediction along pre-collected trajectories, preventing closed-loop VLA evaluation where each action chunk must be conditioned on observations generated by previous execution.

Method: PiL-World is a chunk-wise world model that generates multi-view future observations conditioned on VLA rollouts, using action-derived visual control from head-view robot motion and latent histories encoding task execution context, while jointly predicting complementary multi-view observations. It alternates between VLA inference and world-model prediction for closed-loop evaluation.

Result: On three real dual-arm manipulation tasks, PiL-World generates imagined rollouts highly consistent with real executions. It reduces the error between VLA success rates measured in real-world rollouts and those estimated through closed-loop world-model evaluation from 63.2% to 12.0%.

Conclusion: PiL-World enables effective closed-loop VLA evaluation without real robot execution by generating high-fidelity future observations. Learning from both successful and failed trajectories improves rollout distribution matching.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies operate in a closed loop in real-world robot tasks: a robot observes the scene, executes an action chunk, and conditions its next decision on the resulting observation. However, most existing world models for robot action evaluation are limited to open-loop prediction along pre-collected action trajectories. This prevents them from supporting closed-loop VLA evaluation, where each action chunk must be conditioned on the observation generated by the previous execution. To address this gap, we propose PiL-World, a chunk-wise world model designed for policy-in-the-loop VLA evaluation. Given the current observation and the action trajectory rolled out by a VLA policy, PiL-World generates multi-view future observations that are consistent with the VLA rollout and match the image inputs required by the policy. By alternating between VLA inference and world-model prediction, PiL-World enables closed-loop evaluation without real robot execution at every step. To improve rollout fidelity, PiL-World conditions video generation on action-derived visual control from head-view robot motion and latent histories that encode task execution context, while jointly predicting complementary multi-view observations. Beyond successful teleoperated demonstrations, it also learns from failed execution trajectories, helping the imagined rollouts better match the distribution of real policy executions. We evaluate PiL-World on three real dual-arm manipulation tasks. PiL-World generates imagined rollouts that are highly consistent with real robot executions. More importantly, compared with the baseline, it reduces the error between VLA success rates measured in real-world rollouts and those estimated through closed-loop world-model evaluation from 63.2% to 12.0%.

</details>


### [223] [Visuotactile and Explicitly Force-Controlled Robotic Ultrasound for Abdominal Volumetric Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05848)
*Adrian Piedra,R Brooke Jeffrey,Oussama Khatib*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A robotic ultrasound system integrating stereo vision, force feedback, and expert-informed strategies for autonomous abdominal scanning achieves expert-level image quality while enabling 3D volume acquisition beyond human capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an autonomous robotic ultrasound system that can replicate expert-level scanning techniques, adapt to patient-specific anatomy, and overcome limitations of freehand ultrasound (e.g., difficulty in 3D volume acquisition).

Method: The system (1) records freehand motion and force data from expert radiologists, (2) uses stereo vision for 3D abdominal topography mapping refined by stiffness measurements to delineate rib cage boundaries, (3) executes two scanning paths (upward-angled subcostal sweep and perpendicular soft-tissue sweep), and (4) employs a torque-controlled 7-DOF manipulator with closed-loop force control for consistent probe contact.

Result: The system achieves high-quality imaging comparable to expert scans, dynamically adapts to patient-specific topographies, and enables 3D volume acquisition that surpasses human capabilities.

Conclusion: Integrating expert knowledge with perception-based autonomy and physical reasoning enhances robotic diagnostic performance, with 3D volumetric data acquisition offering improved diagnostic potential.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a robotic ultrasound acquisition system that integrates stereo vision, touch-based feedback, and expert-informed strategies to perform autonomous and adaptive abdominal scans. The system records freehand motion and force data from expert radiologists, creating a framework to capture transducer motion, applied forces, and anatomical scanning strategies. This expert data is replayed to replicate characteristic scans with the robot, forming a foundation for further autonomous capabilities. Using stereo vision, the system generates three-dimensional topography maps of the patient's abdomen, which are refined through stiffness measurements at key points to delineate the rib cage boundary. These combined techniques enable the robot to execute two distinct scanning paths: an upward-angled sweep beneath the rib cage to visualize structures near the upper abdomen and a perpendicular sweep across soft tissue regions. A compliant, torque-controlled seven degree-of-freedom robotic manipulator is controlled to maintain consistent probe contact through closed-loop force control over the varied anatomical surfaces. Physical experiments demonstrate that the system achieves high-quality imaging comparable to expert scans while dynamically adapting to patient-specific topographies. Furthermore, the robotic system surpasses expert capabilities by enabling three-dimensional volume acquisition, which enhances diagnostic potential and provides volumetric data for advanced analyses. This work highlights the integration of expert knowledge into autonomous robotic systems and underscores the potential of combining perception-based autonomy with physical reasoning for enhanced diagnostic performance.

</details>


### [224] [LadderMan: Learning Humanoid Perceptive Ladder Climbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05873)
*Siheng Zhao,Yuanhang Zhang,Ziqi Lu,Pieter Abbeel,Rocky Duan,Koushil Sreenath,Yue Wang,C. Karen Liu,Guanya Shi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LadderMan is a unified system enabling humanoid robots to robustly climb diverse ladders and perform manipulation tasks under constrained conditions, using a two-stage learning pipeline with hybrid imitation/reinforcement learning, depth-based visuomotor policies, and vision foundation models for sim-to-real transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ladder climbing is extremely challenging for humanoid robots due to sparse footholds/handholds, complex whole-body coordination, and sensitivity to perception/control errors. Existing methods struggle with generalization across diverse ladder geometries and real-world deployment.

Method: A two-stage learning pipeline: (1) hybrid motion tracking to learn multiple climbing experts from a single reference motion, (2) distill experts into a unified depth-based visuomotor climbing policy via hybrid imitation and reinforcement learning. Vision foundation models bridge sim-to-real depth perception gap. A separate manipulation policy uses dual-agent formulation for on-ladder teleoperation.

Result: Robust ladder climbing across a wide range of geometries, zero-shot transfer to real-world hardware, and successful manipulation tasks under challenging ladder constraints.

Conclusion: LadderMan demonstrates that a scalable learning-based approach can enable humanoid robots to reliably climb diverse ladders and perform manipulation, with effective sim-to-real transfer.

Abstract: Humanoid robots hold great promise for operating in human-centered environments, yet ladder climbing remains one of the most challenging tasks due to sparse footholds and handholds, complex whole-body coordination, and sensitivity to perception and control errors. We present \textbf{LadderMan}, a unified system that enables humanoid robots to robustly climb diverse ladders and perform manipulation under such constrained conditions. Our climbing policy is built on a scalable two-stage learning pipeline, where we use hybrid motion tracking to learn multiple climbing experts from a single reference motion, and distill these experts into a unified depth-based visuomotor climbing policy via hybrid imitation and reinforcement learning. To enable real-world deployment, we leverage vision foundation models to bridge the sim-to-real gap in depth perception. Building on the learned climbing policy, we further train a separate manipulation policy using a dual-agent formulation, allowing stable on-ladder manipulation via teleoperation. Experiments demonstrate that LadderMan achieves robust ladder climbing across a wide range of geometries, successfully transfers to real-world hardware in a zero-shot manner, and supports various manipulation tasks under challenging ladder constraints. Video results are available at https://ladderman-robot.github.io .

</details>


### [225] [TAGA: Terrain-aware Active Gaze Learning for Generalizable Agile Humanoid Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05880)
*Peizhuo Li,Hongyi Li,Mingfeng Fan,Fangzhou Xu,Shuhao Liao,Yuxuan Ma,Zicheng Zeng,Ze Wang,Yongbin Jin,Yuhong Cao,Hongtao Wang,Guillaume Sartoretti*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAGA introduces a terrain-aware active gaze learning framework for humanoid locomotion that uses attention mechanisms to selectively focus on relevant terrain regions, enabling robust locomotion across challenging terrain including a record 1.2m gap traversal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing humanoid locomotion systems face a trade-off between wide perceptual coverage and precise local geometry understanding under tight onboard computational constraints. Humans naturally look selectively at relevant terrain during locomotion, motivating an active gaze approach for humanoid control.

Method: TAGA fuses vision, proprioception, and motion commands through an attention-based framework that learns to actively attend to specific areas of height scans, selectively using informative regions for downstream control. The gaze behavior emerges naturally through reinforcement learning without explicit supervision.

Result: The policy demonstrates robust locomotion in simulation and hardware, including reliable foothold selection, elevated-platform traversal, competitive sparse-foothold traversal, and the largest reported real-world gap traversal distance of 1.2m among perceptive humanoid systems, while maintaining stability under severe perceptual disturbances.

Conclusion: Active gaze learning improves information density under computational constraints, significantly enhances training efficiency, and produces generalizable perceptive locomotion policies that can traverse diverse challenging terrain in real-world settings.

Abstract: Agile humanoid locomotion across diverse challenging terrain demands both wide perceptual coverage and precise local geometry understanding. Motivated by the way humans selectively look at relevant terrain during locomotion, we introduce TAGA, a Terrain-aware Active Gaze learning framework for Attention-based humanoid control. By fusing vision, proprioception, and motion commands, our framework guides the model to learn anticipatory cues and actively attend to specific areas of the height scan, selectively using these informative regions for the downstream network. This adaptively increases the information density of observations under tight onboard computational constraints, thus enabling fine-grained perceptive locomotion over larger-scale terrains. We find that such gaze behaviors can naturally emerge through reinforcement learning alone, without requiring additional supervision or explicit guidance, significantly improve training efficiency. As a result, the trained policy demonstrates robust and generalizable locomotion in simulation and on hardware, including reliable terrain-aware foothold selection, elevated-platform traversal, competitive sparse-foothold traversal, and the largest reported real-world gap traversal distance of 1.2m among perceptive humanoid locomotion systems, while maintaining stability under severe perceptual disturbances and environmental interference.

</details>


### [226] [A Novel Method with Encoder-Decoder for Cross-Sensor Adaptation in Surface Shape Sensing with Sparse Strain Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05903)
*Shuo Wang,Heng Luo,Dian Jin,Xiaoming Tao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes an encoder-decoder architecture with meta-learning and few-shot adaptation for surface shape sensing using sparse strain sensors, enabling rapid cross-sensor adaptation that reduces error from 23.0 mm to 4.0 mm with under 5% labeled data and <1 second adaptation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Performance variations in sensor arrays due to intrinsic differences or installation conditions cause inconsistent shape sensing results. Traditional methods require large datasets and retraining separate models for each sensor array, increasing cost and time for data acquisition, transmission, and computation.

Method: An encoder-decoder architecture for surface shape sensing based on sparse strain sensors, incorporating meta-learning and few-shot adaptation strategies to enable adaptation across different groups of sensor arrays.

Result: After cross-sensor adaptation, a newly deployed sensor array achieves ~4.0 mm sensing error with <5.0% newly labeled data and <1 second adaptation time, compared to 23.0 mm error without adaptation and 20-minute data collection for retraining. Points with errors below 5.0 mm increased by >65%.

Conclusion: The proposed method substantially reduces cost and training burden of surface shape sensing, with broad potential applications in soft robotics and wearable devices.

Abstract: Performance variations in sensor arrays, caused by intrinsic differences or installation conditions, can lead to inconsistent results during shape sensing. To obtain accurate results, a large amount of data is usually required, and a separate model must be retrained for each sensor array, thereby increasing the cost and time of data acquisition, transmission, and computation. To address this issue, this work proposes an encoder-decoder architecture for surface shape sensing based on sparse strain sensors and further incorporates meta-learning and few-shot adaptation strategies to enable adaptation across different groups of sensor arrays. Experimental results demonstrate that, after the cross-sensor adaptation, a newly deployed sensor array achieves a sensing error of approximately 4.0 mm relying on less than 5.0% newly labeled data and requiring an adaptation time of under 1 second, which represents a substantial improvement from 23.0 mm error without adaptation and 20-minute data collection time required to train a new model. Moreover, the number of points with errors below 5.0 mm increased by more than 65.0%. These results indicate that the proposed method can substantially reduce the cost and training burden of surface shape sensing, and it has broad potential applications in soft robotics and wearable devices.

</details>


### [227] [Learning of Robot Safety Policies via Adversarial Synthetic Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05952)
*Nikolai Dorofeev,Alexey Odinokov,Rostislav Yavorskiy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an adversarial gamification framework (Red Team vs. Blue Team) for generating hazardous synthetic scenarios to learn robot safety policies, enabling discovery of high-risk edge cases beyond random simulation or manual enumeration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical edge cases in Physical AI systems are difficult to capture through random simulation or manual enumeration, creating a gap in robust safety policy learning for real-world environments.

Method: A two-agent adversarial game: Red Team explores potential failures by constructing hazardous scenarios, Blue Team refines safety policies incrementally; combines classical risk modeling with adversarial scenario generation and modern learning paradigms.

Result: Ongoing work; the current contribution is a problem formulation and proposed solution architecture rather than empirical results.

Conclusion: The framework provides a scalable pathway for embedding safety into Physical AI systems by efficiently discovering high-risk edge cases through adversarial scenario generation.

Abstract: In this work, we propose an agentic gamification framework for hazard-informed learning of robot safety policies through synthetic scenarios. We model scenario generation as an adversarial game between two agents: a Red Team that explores the space of potential failures by constructing hazardous situations, and a Blue Team that incrementally refines safety policies to prevent them. This iterative process enables efficient discovery of high-risk edge cases that are unlikely to be captured through random simulation or manual enumeration. By combining classical risk modeling with adversarial scenario generation and modern learning paradigms, this work provides a scalable pathway for embedding safety into Physical AI systems operating in complex real-world environments. The paper describes ongoing work. The contribution is a problem formulation and a proposed solution architecture.

</details>


### [228] [Towards a Data Flywheel for Embodied Intelligence in Logistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05960)
*Anlan Yu,Zaishu Chen,Zhiqing Hong,Daqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a data-centric framework for industrial embodied intelligence in logistics, using a data flywheel that converts daily operations into reusable assets. It introduces WM-DAgger, a World-Model-based data aggregation framework for robust imitation learning, and explores aligning large-scale multimodal data (human demos, operational videos, robot logs) for continual policy improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To scale embodied intelligence from laboratory demonstrations to industrial deployment in logistics, addressing the challenge of how embodied data can be collected, organized, and reused for learning-based policies.

Method: A data-centric framework with a logistics data flywheel: (1) converting daily operations into reusable data assets, (2) using World Models to generate reliable supervision for long-tail parcel manipulation, (3) feeding deployment feedback into policy improvement. WM-DAgger synthesizes out-of-distribution recovery data for robust imitation learning. Ongoing work aligns large-scale multimodal data (human demonstrations, operational videos, robot logs) for policy learning.

Result: Initial result: WM-DAgger, a World-Model-based data aggregation framework that synthesizes out-of-distribution recovery data for robust imitation learning. Ongoing work explores aligning in-the-wild multimodal data for policy learning and continual improvement.

Conclusion: Learning-based policies enhanced by a data flywheel paradigm can potentially scale industrial embodied intelligence, with World Models addressing data scarcity for long-tail scenarios and multimodal data alignment enabling continual system improvement.

Abstract: Embodied intelligence is moving from laboratory demonstrations toward industrial deployment, with the logistics industry serving as a key application scenario. Learning-based policies offer a promising path beyond traditional perception-planning-control pipelines, but their scalability depends on how embodied data can be collected, organized, and reused. This research studies a data-centric framework for industrial embodied intelligence by constructing a logistics data flywheel. Our framework converts daily operations into reusable data assets, uses World Models to generate reliable supervision for long-tail parcel manipulation, and feeds deployment feedback back into policy improvement. As an initial result, \textit{WM-DAgger} introduces a World-Model-based data aggregation framework that synthesizes out-of-distribution recovery data for robust imitation learning. Building on this result, ongoing work explores how large-scale in-the-wild multimodal data, including labeled human demonstrations, unlabeled operational videos, and system-level robot logs, can be aligned for policy learning and transformed into feedback for continual system improvement.

</details>


### [229] [World-Language-Action Model for Unified World Modeling, Language Reasoning, and Action Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05979)
*Yi Yang,Zhihong Liu,Siqi Kou,Yiyang Chen,Yanzhe Hu,Jianbo Zhou,Boyuan Zhao,Zhijie Wei,Xiao Xia,Xueqi Li,Pengfei Liu,Zhijie Deng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes World-Language-Action (WLA) models, a new class of embodied foundation models that jointly predict textual subtasks, subgoal images, and robot actions by combining world modeling (from WAM) and language reasoning (from VLA). Using an autoregressive Transformer backbone with a World Expert and Action Expert, WLA achieves 92.94% success rate on RoboTwin2.0 Clean and 56.5% on RMBench, with 40ms inference on RTX 5090.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify the strengths of world-action models (learning from egocentric videos) and vision-language-action models (language reasoning for complex long-horizon tasks) into a single embodied foundation model.

Method: WLA uses an autoregressive Transformer backbone (instead of bidirectional diffusion) to jointly predict textual subtasks, subgoal images, and robot actions. It employs a World Expert for physical dynamics supervision (world modeling objective) and an Action Expert for action generation. Meta-queries make world prediction implicitly impact action generation, allowing world prediction to be disabled during inference or activated for test-time scaling.

Result: WLA-0 (2B active parameters, 40ms inference on RTX 5090) achieves 92.94% success rate on RoboTwin2.0 Clean, 56.5% on RMBench, and demonstrates state-of-the-art multi-task and long-horizon learning, including learning from cross-embodiment robot videos without action annotations.

Conclusion: WLA models effectively combine world modeling and language reasoning for embodied AI, achieving strong performance in complex long-horizon tasks while maintaining efficient inference. The approach also enables learning from cross-embodiment robot videos without action labels.

Abstract: We propose world-language-action (WLA) models as a new class of embodied foundation models. WLA takes textual instructions, images, and robot states as inputs to jointly predict textual subtasks, subgoal images, and robot actions, conjoining the \emph{world modeling interface} to learn from extensive egocentric videos as in the world-action model (WAM) and the \emph{language reasoning} capacities to solve complex long-horizon tasks as in vision-language-action (VLA) models. At the core of WLA lies an \emph{autoregressive (AR)} Transformer backbone, instead of a bidirectional diffusion Transformer as in WAMs, to predict the \emph{next state}, comprising the \emph{semantic-level} textual intention and complementary \emph{fine-grained} physical dynamics. The physical dynamics are supervised by the world modeling objective based on a dedicated World Expert, and are leveraged to ease the characterization of the state-action correlation for the Action Expert. WLA leverages meta-queries to make the world prediction \emph{implicitly} impact the action generation so that the former can be disabled during inference. The world prediction can also be activated to enable test-time scaling for improved robot control. Our WLA-0 prototype, with 2B active parameters, achieves 40 ms per inference on an NVIDIA RTX 5090. Evaluations across simulated and real-world environments demonstrate that WLA-0 achieves state-of-the-art multi-task and long-horizon learning abilities, e.g., 92.94\% success rate on RoboTwin2.0 Clean and 56.5\% success rate on RMBench. WLA-0 also holds the promise to learn novel tasks directly from \emph{cross-embodiment robot videos} without action annotations.

</details>


### [230] [Merging model-based control with multi-agent reinforcement learning for multi-agent cooperative teaming strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06011)
*Christian Llanes,Spencer W. Jensen,Samuel Coogan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes MA-AC-MPC, a framework combining multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) with model-predictive control (MPC) for safe, dynamically feasible cooperative multi-agent tasks. Extends actor-critic MPC to MARL. Validated in pursuit-evasion simulations and heterogeneous drone-rover landing hardware, achieving 100% success rate vs. 60% for a multi-layer perceptron baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leverage MARL for learning cooperative policies from non-differentiable rewards over long horizons while incorporating MPC's robustness and safety for short-horizon dynamically feasible actions.

Method: Extends actor-critic model predictive control to multi-agent settings (MA-AC-MPC), integrating MARL policy learning with MPC's short-horizon replanning for safe action execution.

Result: MA-AC-MPC achieved 100% success rate in heterogeneous drone-rover landing hardware vs. 60% for MA-AC-MLP; demonstrated robustness in both pursuit-evasion and hardware experiments.

Conclusion: Combining MARL with MPC yields safer, more dynamically feasible cooperative policies, outperforming pure neural network approaches in both simulation and real-world multi-agent tasks.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a framework that combines multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) with model-based control to achieve safe, dynamically feasible actions in cooperative multi-agent tasks. Multi-agent reinforcement learning provides the advantage of learning cooperative policies for multi-agent teams from discrete non-differentiable rewards in a long planning horizon. Model-predictive control is robust and offers safe, dynamically feasible actions in a fast replanning framework for short horizons. We propose an algorithm that extends actor-critic model predictive control for MARL which we refer to as multi-agent actor-critic model predictive control (MA-AC-MPC). We demonstrate the capabilities of this algorithm by applying it to a multi-agent pursuit-evasion scenario. Specifically, we compare the evader team's strategy using the MA-AC-MPC model and a multi-layer perceptron model (MA-AC-MLP). The pursuer team uses augmented proportional navigation as it is accepted as an advanced adversarial control law. We also provide an example with a heterogeneous environment where a drone and omni-wheeled rover cooperate to achieve repeatable and successful landing with 100% success rate in hardware for MA-AC-MPC compared to 60% for MA-AC-MLP. We demonstrate the robustness of the proposed MA-AC-MPC algorithm in hardware for both environments.

</details>


### [231] [RealDexUMI: A Wearable Universal Manipulation Interface for Dexterous Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06033)
*Chaoyi Xu,Yixuan Jiang,Jiahui Huan,Yuhui Fu,Haoyu Zhou,Weitian Yuan,Jiayi Yu,Wanpeng Zhang,Haoqi Yuan,Zongqing Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RealDexUMI presents a wearable universal manipulation interface with a shared dexterous end-effector module combining a lightweight hand, in-hand vision, and fingertip tactile sensing. A palm-side isomorphic teleoperation glove enables retargeting-free, intuitive hand control. Across eight real-robot tasks, policies trained on RealDexUMI data achieve 88.75% average success rate, generalize to unseen poses, and transfer across three embodiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pipelines for dexterous manipulation data collection either lose deployable dexterity through retargeting/embodiment conversion, or rely on costly, non-scalable robot-specific teleoperation lacking intuitive, contact-aware control.

Method: A wearable universal manipulation interface built around a shared dexterous end-effector module (lightweight hand, in-hand vision, fingertip tactile sensing) with a palm-side isomorphic teleoperation glove for real-time, retargeting-free mapping of human finger inputs to robot-hand joint commands.

Result: Across eight real-robot tasks (fine-grained, contact-rich, long-horizon, bimanual), policies achieve 88.75% average success rate, generalize to unseen initial poses, and transfer across three embodiments.

Conclusion: The shared hand and sensing module design yields zero-gap end-effector data, enabling deployable dexterous manipulation without retargeting or embodiment conversion losses.

Abstract: Learning dexterous manipulation requires demonstrations that preserve fine hand-object interactions while remaining executable at deployment. Existing pipelines either lose deployable dexterity through retargeting or embodiment conversion, or rely on robot-specific teleoperation that is costly to scale and often lacks intuitive, contact-aware control for dexterous data collection. We present RealDexUMI, a wearable universal manipulation interface built around a shared dexterous end-effector module that integrates a lightweight dexterous hand, in-hand vision, and fingertip tactile sensing. A palm-side isomorphic teleoperation glove maps human finger inputs to robot-hand joint commands, enabling real-time, retargeting-free, intuitive, and precise hand control. The shared hand and sensing modules yield zero-gap end-effector data, with matched in-hand observations, tactile signals, contacts, and hand actions between collection and deployment. Across eight real-robot tasks spanning fine-grained, contact-rich, long-horizon, and bimanual manipulation, policies trained on RealDexUMI data achieve an average success rate of 88.75%, generalize to unseen initial poses, and transfer across three embodiments. Website: https://research.beingbeyond.com/realdexumi

</details>


### [232] [Gotta Grow Fast: Design and Benchmarking of a Tip Mount for High-Speed Vine Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06040)
*Antonio Alvarez Valdivia,Robert Reeve,Ankush Dhawan,Ciera McFarland,Chad Council,Margaret McGuinness,Nathaniel Hanson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a triangular roller tip mount that reduces internal resistance during growth by rolling rather than sliding against the robot body, enabling consistent eversion on TPU-coated ripstop nylon vine robots for the first time. A custom testbed was introduced to isolate tip mounting effects by measuring tail tension, and comparative experiments show the triangular roller mount achieves the lowest tail tension and most repeatable growth performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating cameras and sensors at the tip of soft growing vine robots is uniquely challenging because the material forming the tip is constantly renewed during growth. Friction, added weight, and fabric constriction hinder sensor/tool mounting, limiting deployment for inspection and search tasks.

Method: A triangular roller tip mount that reduces internal resistance via rolling contact instead of sliding. Design refined through iterative failure analysis. A custom testbed measures tail tension during eversion to isolate mount effects. Comparative experiments across multiple mount variants including prior designs.

Result: The triangular roller mount achieves the lowest tail tension and most repeatable growth performance among all tested variants, enabling consistent eversion on TPU-coated ripstop nylon vine robots.

Conclusion: The work establishes both a validated tip mount design and a repeatable benchmarking framework for advancing sensor and tool integration in soft growing robots.
